A19. Complementarity and coherence
However, ASIDP is expected to be used to launch the Partnership for Low-carbon and Climate-resilient Rangeland Management in Mongolia, which will provide financing for low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management in Mongolia based on the mitigation results achieved. See Section 8.5 given in particular Box 10.

The Executing Entity of ASIDP is Government of Mongolia, represented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Light Industry (MOLI), the Development Bank of Mongolia (DBM), and the Asset Management Company of Development Banks of Mongolia (AMC-DBM). More details are available on the Green Inclusive Regional Agribusiness Fund (GIRAF).

A21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
Rangelands are one of the world’s predominant ecosystems, representing about 70% of the earth's land surface and covering more than 4 billion hectares globally. The total carbon stock in rangelands is estimated at 1.1 trillion tCO2eq/year globally. However, rangelands are vulnerable to climate change and undergo significant degradation due to overgrazing, unsustainable livestock management practices, especially in regions where rangelands can turn into a desert if not well managed. Despite the large surface areas involved and the significant adaptation concerns and mitigation potentials, little climate finance has been directed towards rangeland conservation.

In Mongolia, rangelands are the most common land type, covering 82% of the country. Meanwhile, the livestock sector is vital to the Mongolian economy in terms of employment (representing about 25% of total employment) and export earnings (accounting for approximately 30% of GDP). However, the impacts of climate change, unsustainable rangeland and livestock management, and weak urban-rural linkages.

As stated in the NDP of Mongolia, the annual mean air temperature over Mongolia has increased by 2.07°C since 1961 (1981-2010), with a significant increase observed from 1995 to present. The precipitation is more uneven, with an overall trend towards a reduction in annual precipitation, especially in summer. However, evaporation has clearly increased and outpaces precipitation, causing water shortages and desertification of rangelands. These changes have led to the degradation of about 70% of Mongolia’s land cover since the first half of the 21st century. Some of the key impacts and vulnerabilities are:

- Approximately 70% of pastoral land has degraded, while changing climate patterns.
- The frequency of extreme weather phenomena has doubled in the last two decades. This is expected to increase by 23–65% by the middle of the century as compared to present conditions.

Number based on the GWP of a second report assessment of the IPCC, which are the number Mongolia has used in its latest national inventory. Based on the GWP in the first report of the IPCC, the emission reductions are 3.3 million tCO2eq/year. Therefore, the emissions from this FP, the DBM from this FP, and the AMC-DBM from this FP have already been costed.

Furthermore, we have used this net number for the calculation of key ratios in Section E of the funding proposal.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 5 OF 126

Climate change has reduced the productivity of rangelands, affected glacier-fed water regimes, and increased exposure of herders and the animal husbandry sector to climate-related natural disasters. These issues have severely impacted livelihoods as well as ecosystem integrity and quality, with a significant decline in the collapse of the Mongolian steppe, of the 1950s, of the livestock herd and the urban productive and service functions delivered to the rural economy. Herders have increased their "herds" size to unsustainable levels, overgrazing rangelands, which has led to a dramatic reduction in grassland productivity (averages of 22.6% above the ranging carrying capacity (27.4% in the three western aimags). Overgrazing and climate change are reinforcing each other, leading to further degradation of rangelands and reducing the size of their herds as safety net. As a result, 70% of pastoral land has been degraded.* This means both livestock productivity and resilience have declined significantly, with the potential for the steppe to lose its capacity. Reversing the degradation and restoring the health of Mongolia's vast rangeland area offers a very large mitigation potential, estimated at over 12 million tons CO2 equivalent per year over a 25-year period.

The Mongolian Government is fully aware of the severity of the situation and has set out strategies and objectives to reverse the current overall trends and implement sustainable climate-resilient rangeland management. Several important stand-alone initiatives from donors have also been implemented. However, there are still significant challenges in reversing the degradation trend due to the complex nature of the problem and the complicated and interrelated barriers invested in the livestock sector. Both aspects, resilience to climate change and adaptation to climate change, need to be addressed simultaneously by improving grazing methods and limiting the number of animals. A comprehensive approach at the territory level is required for success, and national development strategy is required to achieve the desired adaptation and mitigation outcomes.

The Aimags and Soums Green Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP) proposes a functional model that integrates climate change resilience into land use planning, sustainable land development, and more efficient urban-rural linkages. ASDIP integrates climate issues within a territorial perspective, which is essential for addressing the complex nature of the problem. The interrelated transformations are mutually reinforcing to achieve greater and more sustainable climate impacts. rangelands become more productive and resilient, while urban-rural linkages (the aimags) become more efficient and integrated. The integration of these two elements (land use planning of an aimag) becomes the anchors of climate-smart agribusinesses. These agribusinesses then constitute more diversified Low-carbon Climate-resilient Livestock Value Chains (LCLVCs) that promote sustainable land development, urban-rural linkages and rural-urban integration. While having a global focus on Mongolia's western aimags, the program promotes climate finance and private sector investment mechanisms to support the implementation of the model. The following are some of the intended interventions needed to occur:

1. Transform existing low-carbon climate-resilient urban settlements with improved living conditions and adequate infrastructures and services to anchor LCLVC investments at the point of need and provide a strong foundation for future development.
2. Introduce transformational low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices, with increased livestock productivity and resilience as key outcomes.
3. Provide innovative climate mitigation finance attractive for LCLVC investments to sustain herders' low-carbon practices; and
4. Build capacity and awareness to formulate and implement transformational low-carbon climate-resilient development plans and policy, and the knowledge and MRV system necessary for replication.

* http://123.0.nn/real-nsr/JS/T/GR/6976_L_10.5

According to Mongolia's Third National Communication to the UNFCCC, the "Splinter area which urgently requires Warming" is defined as the western aimags of Mongolia (Bayan-Ulgii, Bayankhongor, and Darkhad) due to rapid climate change with its pastures and recovery capacity and to change of present pasture use to agriculture. Moreover, The Third National Communication also states that 60% of the livestock population is concentrated in these three aimags.

Furthermore, the Action Plan of Mongolian Agenda for Sustainable Livestock of 8 June 2018 gives an estimate that 65% of the total livestock population is located in the western region data based on MOFA/UNDP Mongolia Livestock Program, 2017 and on NGO, 2017. Western region data based on MOFA/UNDP Mongolia Livestock Program, 2017.

To achieve these four transformative strategic objectives, ASDIP will have four outputs. They will be delivered through a multitranche financing mechanism (MFM), sequenced into three tranches over a 10-year implementation period:

- Climate-resilient, low-carbon, and attractive agri and scourt centers developed
- Rangelands managed for climate-resilience, high carbon sequestration, and sustainable herding (renewable energy and water resources)
- Low-carbon and climate-resilient livestock value chains created and strengthened through accessible finance
- Capacity and policy reforms for low-carbon and climate-resilient agro-territorial development improved

For its implementation ASDIP will establish a cross-sector and cross-governmental level implementation mechanism, including the establishment of an independent project management unit (PMU) to manage it. Given its high total investment costs compared to Mongolia’s limited availability to provide financing, GCF funding is critical for the successful implementation of this project. The GCF contribution will be USD 105 million; GCF funding will enable the project to build and expand on existing foundations and create a transformational and innovative integrated model for low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland and more effective rural-urban linkages in Mongolia.

ASDIP will deliver the following quantitative outcomes:

- Climate mitigation impact: 112.40 million tCO2e net GHG emission reductions, of which 87.65 million tCO2e come from avoided methane emissions and 24.75 million tCO2e from CO2e emission reductions from avoided methane and nitrous oxide emissions; 4.6 million tCO2e through GHG mitigation in urban areas; 0.49 million tCO2e emissions reduction from the construction of new buildings, against which a total of 3.82 million tCO2e emissions are generated during the construction phase
- Adaptation beneficiaries: direct beneficiaries: 552,300 people, including about 54,000 vulnerable water users and 17,000 households in rural areas and environmental conditions in the target aims; indirect beneficiaries 3.2 million
- Ecosystem services: sustainable low-carbon management of an estimated 28.1 million hectares of rangeland in Mongolia.
- Main socioeconomic and livelihood benefits: ASDIP will create low-carbon economic diversification job opportunities, generating about 11,400 green jobs through its three windows and guarantee component, creating in return about 150,000 indirect jobs. The program is categorized by ADB as Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM).

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate Change (max: 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The climate change rationale of ASDFP focuses on climate change adaptation and mitigation (with supporting information provided below). For further details that are not covered in this section, please refer to Annex 15: Climate Change Assessment.

## Global context

Rangelands are the world's predominant ecosystems, representing about 70% of the earth's land surface, excluding Antarctica. Rangeland degradation is a global challenge as rangelands account for considerable carbon stocks and provide important ecosystem services (e.g., water regulation, biodiversity conservation). The equivalent year per (tCO2eq/year). Despite these significant mitigation potentials, limited climate finance support has been provided to rangelands globally. ASDFP aims to address this gap by developing a systems model considering all the systemic urban and rural linkages to achieve sustainable rangeland management through innovative, transformative, and replicable combinations of people, public, and private sector interventions working together long-term to reduce greenhouse gas emissions while generating co-benefits.

## Climate change and adaptation challenges in Mongolia

### Introduction: The main climate challenges for the animal husbandry sector

To focus on the provision of discussion of climate change, it is useful to highlight the main mechanisms that are expected to be affected by climate change. The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) affect the livestock sector and rangeland management in Mongolia before turning to quantitative data regarding observed climate change and climate change projections.

The climate change adaptation challenge in Mongolia is primarily based on observed and projected increases in (1) temperature, which leads to increased evaporation and drought; and (2) frequency and severity of dust events. Drought leads to a decrease of rangeland biomass productivity, which in turn leads to a reduction in output per animal in terms of milk, meat, wool, and hides. Dust events lead to reduced pasture quality due to the presence of sand particles (high snowfall and/or low temperatures), and which lead to increased livestock losses during the winter. The climate change adaptation challenge is also based on observed and projected increases in temperature, which, together with drought, also lead to other secondary effects:

- Reduced water availability for livestock, which reduce access to water resources for livestock;
- Reduced vegetation and fodder available at the end of the summer and throughout the winter.

These increases in drought and consequent reductions in water and vegetation reduce livestock conditions by the end of summer, which increases the risk of mortality during the following winter. If the winter is harsh (due to low temperatures), this will further increase the mortality rate among livestock. The climate change challenges are one of the factors behind unsustainable livestock practices, which increases the vulnerability to climate change (see insert 1).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL v2.1 | PAGE 8 OF 126

## Unustainable land management practices also contribute to land degradation and affect Mongolia's water resources, and in part the result of climate change. Coping mechanisms, such as increasing the herd size to avoid a total loss during droughts, are often mentioned by herders and experts in Mongolia but is also clearly visible in the statistics, which show that livestock numbers have increased since 1990. The discussion on land degradation and its causes (see Section 3.2.4 of Annex 15) contains an extensive discussion of the period from 1990 until today, which breaks down into several periods:

- **1990–1999 pre-drought period:** The average annual increase in animal numbers is 2.94% per year.
- **2000–2008 drought period:** The average annual increase in animal numbers is -35.76% per year.
- **2002–2009 post-drought recovery period:** The average annual increase in animal numbers is 10.4% per year.
- **2010–2018 pre-drought period:** The average annual increase in animal numbers is -35.76% per year.
- **2019–2020 post-drought recovery period:** The average annual increase in animal numbers is 10.4% per year.

The interpretation that the drought leads to increases in herds as a coping mechanism is furthermore reinforced by data showing that the number of households with livestock increased during this time, from 358,729 (in 2000) to 600,011 household. In the first recovery period (2002–2009), the number of households with herds dropped from 423,230 to 358,729 and then increased again in the second recovery period (2010–2018) to 600,011. In the third recovery period (2019-2020), the number of households with herds increased from 216,570 to 233,317, while the number of animals increased by 44% and the number of livestock owners increased by 8.5%. In this period (2019-2020), the number of households increased by 167 (less than 0.1% from the 2002 level), while the number of animals increases by 58,4 million (244% of the 2002 level).

## Climate change: the evidence base

The following climate change observations, projections, and direct impacts, further elaborated in Annex 15 Section 3.2.4, are based on the observed changes and annually revised data of temperature, precipitation, and the animal husbandry sector to droughts and floods.

As stated in the NDC of Mongolia, the annual mean air temperature in Mongolia has increased by 0.7°C since 1961. The frequency of extreme cold events has decreased while the occurrence of heatwaves has increased. Annual rainfall has decreased and is expected to continue to decrease, while winter snowfall has increased in some regions.

The climate change projections for Mongolia are based on the results from the CMIP5 models. These show that extreme climatic events resulting in high livestock losses. Average annual precipitation (heavily concentrated in summer) will decrease by 10–20% and temperatures will increase by 3°C or more, with a drying trend and increased aridity, particularly during summer. This warming and drying trend has contributed to desertification in Mongolia, affecting 70% of the country's grasslands to varying degrees. The pastureland health and availability are expected to decrease significantly over time as well. These changes will have significant impacts on Mongolia. Winter pasture yields and grazing capacity following a dry summer versus a relatively mild summer season can be reduced by up to 50%. This is particularly true for the semi-arid regions of the country, especially in the steppe-steppe regions.

The area of grasslands has decreased by over 30% over the last 20 years, leading to herders' range changes: expected to continue. The number of lakes and reservoirs has increased while some have dried up or disappeared. Lakes volume are expected to change, with some rising due to glacier and permafrost melting, but mostly lowering or disappearing altogether as a result of climate warming and the drying trend.

The 2014 Mongolia Second Adaptation Assessment Report on Climate Change (MARC 2014) and the Ministry of Environment and Green Development (MoEGD) show that the permafrost distribution shrinks by 5% in the last 20 years over the country's territory.

year hottest years likely) will be 1-in-2-year or annual events. While there may potentially for an overall increase in precipitation, this will be exceeded by increases in the evaporation rate, reducing water availability and increasing the risk of droughts and heatwaves. The projected pattern (scenario RCP8.5) is a regional climate model (RegCM3). Mongolia's average mean temperature will warm by 2.0-4.1°C by 2030, 2.2-3.4°C, and 4.0-5.7°C by 2030, 2050, and 2080. Mean annual precipitation is projected to increase by 5.5-9.6% in the period up to 2030, but decrease by 1.5-3.4% from 2030 onwards. There is an accompanying projected 10-20% decrease in summer rainfall throughout central parts of Mongolia.

The projected decreases in summer rainfall and temperature increases (increasing aridity during summer) are expected to have a significant impact on the livestock sector, which accounts for about 45 percent of GDP where decreasing precipitation, increasing temperatures, and potential evapotranspiration during summer could lead to reduced pasture productivity. The combination of heavy snowfall and low temperatures in winter (12-16, 27-30°C) increases the intensity of droughts and dzuds (Climate Change Assessment, Figures 31-34). The projected increase in temperature and decrease in precipitation during summer will also lead to increasing temperatures and decreasing precipitation during the summer season in the target areas (Climate Change Assessment: Figures 55-59) and thus resulting increases in the intensity of droughts and dzuds.

Natural disasters also impact the country, including harsh winters, drought, snow and dust storms, flash floods, and landslides. The frequency and magnitude of natural disasters have tripled due to climate change in the last decade. Since 1950, Mongolia has experienced a significant increase in the number of cold spells (Figure 32), with an increasing trend in the area affected by drought between 1951 and 2012. Observations indicate that since 1987, there have been more frequent occurrences of heavy snowfall during winter and heavy rains or winters with heavy snowfalls and/or lower temperatures; the combination of which leads to dzud events. On average, Mongolia has experienced a 3°C increase in temperature over the past century (reducing pasture), has led to a significant increase in dzud events since 1999 (Climate Change Assessment, Figure 34).

Because of these climate change threats, the livestock sector is affected in two major ways:

* The first is noted above, is the increase in frequency and severity of natural disasters leading to livestock deaths. A recent IMF report[1] notes that “livestock herds living in degraded environments are also more vulnerable to droughts and other extreme weather events.” In Mongolia, 20 percent of GDP (or 82 percent of GGP in a year) during harsh winters called “dzuds” –that have grown in frequency (GDP share: 1950-74 = 3.6%, 1975-99 = 10.5%, and 2000-18 = 20.5%) – are at risk of loss. Results suggest that the combination of harsh winters and harsh summers strongly predicts adult livestock loss. On average, Mongolia has experienced a 3°C increase in temperature over the past century (reducing pasture) and thus resulting increases in the intensity of droughts and dzuds.
* The second impact is a reduction in rangeland and animal husbandry productivity. Animal weights and sizes have decreased in recent decades because of increased drought frequency and pasture production

# Rangeland and livestock sector mitigation potential in Mongolia

In Mongolia, rangelands are the most common land type, covering 85% of the country. They are uniquely characterized by their high biodiversity and a unique ecosystem with an unbalanced proportion of herds with a high proportion of goats², and poor rangeland management practices. Currently, overgrazing in Mongolia exceeds sustainable carrying capacity, resulting in soil erosion, desertification, and land degradation. Approximately 70% of pastoral land has been degraded³. This means that both above- and below-ground biomass is reduced, leading to a decrease in the amount of carbon sequestered by rangelands.

It is estimated that because of this, the actual amount of carbon sequestered is 1.2 billion tCO2e below potential sequestration levels (see Table 3). The total amount of CO2 emissions from livestock production is estimated at over 440 million tCO2e countrywide over a 20-year period (from soil carbon sequestration). ASDP will achieve a significant part of this potential: 94 million tCO2e over a 20-year period.

The proposed reduction in animal numbers, as the main direct measure to restore rangelands, leads to a reduction in livestock emissions. Because of the large size of the animal husbandry sector and the significant absolute reduction in animal numbers, the climate change mitigation potential is very high. The estimated emission reductions from these measures is very significant, estimated at over 17 million tCO2e over a 40-year period with a 20-year time horizon on the impact of animal reduction measures that are gradually phased in⁴.

## Baseline

The situation of climate change vulnerability in the livestock sector, the reduction of the number of livestock, and promising mitigation actions were discussed at the National Livestock Development Program (NLDP) workshop organized by the Government of Mongolia (GoM), articulated in its national sector policies (e.g., National Mongolian Livestock Development Program) and in its range submissions to the UNFCCC (e.g., GoM’s Mongolian submission of 2010, Mongolia’s TNA report).

## B

In the livestock sector climate change adaptation and greenhouse gas mitigation both require a rationalization in the use of scarce resources (water and biomass from rangelands) where climate change exacerbates existing resource stressors, requiring, among others, a reduction in number of animals considered as baseline development for both mitigation and adaptation is worsening increasing animal populations.

Figure 1 provides an overview of the development of livestock numbers between 1970 and 2019 (total numbers are given in thousands). The total number of livestock has increased from approximately 65 million to less or more stable. In 1990, the total number of livestock (sheep, goats, cattle, camels) was 25.86 million, with the composition sheep (34.3%), goats (19.8%), cattle (11.6%), horse (8.7%), and camels (2.1%).

The need for a rapid increase in animal numbers continues to be a major objective of the Mongolian government[1]. It should be noted that the past failure of the NMLP does not mean that ASDIP will fail. On the contrary: ASDIP has been designed and developed to provide comprehensive support for increasing animal numbers (see Table 3) and is based on the results of interviews that address the problems of climate change adaptation and mitigation from all aspects. Finally, it should be noted that the historical impact of climate change in Mongolia is reflected in the changes observed in vegetation types, grazing regimes and rangeland productivity; see the Climate Change Assessment Section X.D.) and hence reduce the number of animals on the land. The increase in animal numbers will not lead to an overgrazing of animals, meaning that after a transitional phase, these increases in animal numbers do not lead to an environmental degradation.

Project location

ASDIP Tranche 1 focuses on three western aimags, Bayan-Ulgii, Khovd, and Uvs (see Map 1 for the targeted aimag centers, soum centers, and intercentrum of Tranche 1), each of which is highly dependent on the animal husbandry sector for employment (in the range of 38-57%). All these aimags have higher than average temperatures compared to other parts of Mongolia. The climate in these aimags is characterized by a combination of drought and summer and harsh winter conditions with a deeper snow cover, dudzuu. This means that the vegetation type has been changing; productivity has been declining and that dzuds are expected to result in increased losses of livestock. In addition, glaciers are melting, temperatures are increasing, and precipitation patterns change. These trends result in (i) changes in water availability for pastoralists; (ii) changes in soil moisture content leading to land degradation; and (iii) change in water flow during peak season leading to higher erosion, overried damages, and sedimentation of rivers. The climate change vulnerability assessment conducted by the project team shows that the aimags are highly vulnerable with respect to the carrying capacity, which is 5 points higher than the national average. The climate change vulnerability index (CCVI) for each aimag is shown in Table 4. These changes in water availability and soil moisture content, as well as changes in temperature, precipitation, and frequency of natural hazards show that all aimags will be categorized as highly and extremely vulnerable during 2050 and beyond[1].

The lack of infrastructure development in the three targeted aimags within Mongolia’s central region has left these Three Western Aimags isolated and under-equipped, despite being of strategic importance as a western development corridor to balance the territorial development of the country. The prominence of this area is also reflected by its high potential for agricultural production, especially with respect to livestock's potential, especially with the proximity of Central Asian countries and potential demand for green agri- products in these countries. This is further supported by the fact that the project team has identified as its first phase (2006–2020), implementing advanced herder organization level, using PUG organization, and laying down a fundamental pre-condition for ASDIP to develop its integrated model.

Figures 2a-2g show the gradual increase in the number of animals, which is especially pronounced from 2010 onwards. The figures also show the impacts of the dzud (see below) in reducing the animal numbers in the early 2000s and 2010, especially in the Western aimags (all aimags shown, except Dornod and Sükhbaatar). The most significant feature of this graph is that it shows a decline in animal numbers between 2017 and 2018, which is expected to continue into 2019. This decline is due to the lack of rain in summer 2017 (pastures too poor to adequately feed the animals), followed by a late start of reproduction in autumn 2017 - due to the lack of rain in summer 2017 (pastures too poor to adequately feed the animals). As a result, there were fewer calves born and many animals died during winter. The breeding season was also delayed because it is too late to complete the vegetative cycle of the plants before the first frost of September; as a result, there were fewer grasses available for grazing in spring 2018. This led to poor pasture conditions throughout the winter, and pastures were poor. Thus, the breeders preferred to slaughter many of their herds to valorize meat (which is sold at higher prices) instead of meat and skins at the end of the year. The reduction observed is, therefore, typical of what could be expected under summer drought conditions; it is not a result of a herder's willingness to adjust to the carrying capacity.

However, most of these projects have focused solely on cooperating with herders on natural resources management or investments into the value chains. Relatively little consideration has been given to the long-term sustainability of these initiatives in terms of their ability to generate sufficient economic incentives for herders’ organizations and change herders’ empowerment to facilitate their productive participation in agriculture value chains. This is because most of them have not addressed the following: the strengthening of herders' organizations in Mongolia; the signing of Range Land Agreements (RUA) raising herders’ awareness on the necessity of RUA and the need for a more equitable distribution of grazing resources; the support for improved market access, including the establishment of cooperatives as an intermediary between herders and processing activities, the absence of traceability and certification system, the lack of information about the structure of ASDIP and its funding mechanism, the lack of sufficient economic incentives for herders to comply with the relevant provisions of the RUA to reduce the number of livestock in their possession; the lack of support from the World Bank’s Livelihoods and Landscapes and Rural Development Project (I additional financing), which focused on support to processing factories and value chain development, but not on the strengthening of herders’ organizations or the creation of the base of the agri-business value chain. Finally, none of the existing and prior initiatives systematically addressed the need for a more integrated approach in terms of the linkages between production and processing facilities (see Section B.2). In general, one of the key components of ASDIP is to provide a sustainable framework that will allow herders’ organizations to access sufficient economic incentives and funding as discussed in Section 8.6. However, because of their complementarity with some of ASDIP components, all those parallel initiatives provide an opportunity for synergy and ASDIP replication to reach more herders. The World Bank’s Livelihoods and Landscapes project is a good example here because it requires information about the output structure of ASDIP (that only then is introduced), Annex 25, and other relevant documents. This will allow herders’ organizations to better understand their approach to which they can contribute the long-term in a synergistic manner. ASDIP is developing a model that will provide a framework for herders’ organizations to access sufficient economic incentives on one hand, create the necessary linkages and integrated framework to realize sustainability and maximize climate impact.

## B.2. Theory of change (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

### The Causes: climate change and unsustainable livestock management

**Climate change:** Climate change causes a decline in biomass production over rangelands and a decline in biodiversity. It also increases the frequency and intensity of dust storms. It increases the vulnerability of the livestock sector, causing herders to increase herd size as a coping mechanism (See Section 8.1 and the Box below).

**Number of animals:** Since the approval of the National Mongolian Livestock Program in 2010, the Government's objective has been to reduce the overall livestock numbers to keep them at sustainable levels. At that time, the total number of livestock was estimated at around 35 million heads. In 2021, it reached 47 million. However, this objective has so far been elusive.

The Ministry of Food, Agriculture and Light Industry (MOFLAI) estimates that Mongolian rangelands' sustainable carrying capacity is 51.6 million heads based on the current livestock population in 2021. At the same time, the National Livestock Program sets a target for the current pace, the livestock number is expected to exceed 100 million by 2025.

The water resources and the biodiversity of rangelands are not sufficient to feed by the rangelands (for the livestock sector) nor cope with these increasing livestock populations.

**Grazing practices and social organization:** The lack of collective range management among herders, combined with a high population density in the western region, has led to overgrazing. This is concentrated on grazing areas around a limited number of water points such as rivers and lakes), and the decrease of hay and fodder production. These factors have contributed to the decline in rangeland productivity.

The Green Gold project has demonstrated that grazing management can lead to vegetation recovery and/or increased forage availability in rangelands. The social organization operating during the so-called "socialist period" (1924-1989) was characterized by a lack of range management, which led to overgrazing.

The 2017 study also shows the strong links between overgrazing, rangeland displacement (due to higher number of livestock), and the decline in hay production. The social organization operating during the so-called "socialist period" was characterized by a lack of range management, which led to overgrazing.

**Herds composition:** The impact of goats on rangeland degradation has been significantly higher than other livestock species (sheep, cattle). This is because goats are more efficient at consuming grasses and shrubs that destroy grassland more than the biomass intake on its own would indicate. NSO data (1970-2018) shows that the proportion of goats in total livestock has increased from 35% to 46%. The proportion of sheep started to increase from the 1990s, with strong increases in the 2000s, especially after the 2009-2010 drought. Currently, the proportion of goats is 41.2%, and the ratio between sheep and goats is 52:48, while a genuine equilibrium was observed during the socialist period with a 70:30 ratio**.

<sub>1 National data based on MOFLAI National Livestock Program, 2017 and on NSO. 2017 Western region data based on MOFLAI sub-region level datasets provided by MOFLAI.</sub>

<sub>2 NSO: National Statistics Office</sub>

<sub>3 Source: Socioeconomic baseline study of herder households, SOA, 2017.</sub>

The Reasons

In 1992, with the disengagement from the Soviet Union, Mongolia entered a transitional period. Many people lost their jobs, as state enterprises were shut down or privatized, and the centralized administration system withdrew. State cooperatives, processing facilities, and services to the rural economy were dispensed or abolished. The collapse of the livestock industry was particularly severe in the 1990s. As a result, many households with little experience in livestock management went back to herding activities as most livelihoods became more precarious.

With the collapse of their economic, processing functions, and the dismantling of cooperatives, aimag and soum centers could not play their role as anchors of economic activities and in attracting quality production inputs. The decline of the livestock industry was exacerbated by a series of droughts that occurred due to unhealthy and low-quality livestock raw material and relying on quality with low price differentiation and small-scale processing facilities. This led to the collapse of the livestock industry, which had no veterinary services, and the massive loss of livestock after drier herders started to migrate massively to urban areas, particularly to the capital city, in search of livelihood opportunities. Households migrated and settled in urban areas, especially Ulaanbaatar, where they established their own settlements on land that was once farmland underpinned standardless areas, named ger areas, which represent about 60% of Ulaanbaatar's total population.

Since 2000, the large waves of migration, especially to Ulaanbaatar, resulted in an urbanization ratio of about 70%, of the country’s 3.2 million population in 2019, while the average urbanization ratio in Asia is only 54%. The capital city has become a major economic and social hub for the entire country, with 68% of the urban population, and 63% of the gross domestic product. The capital city's demographic and economic weight is so significant that it can be considered as an independent economy. Ulaanbaatar is characterized by overcentralized political, economic, cultural, and social activities in Ulaanbaatar City and demonstrates the urgent need to implement a plan for developing city districts. Rural-urban migration also increased the pressure on urban systems, which are already underdeveloped and inefficient and undersized to meet present needs and anticipated future growth. See also Insert 3.

Insert 3. The decline of Mongolia aimag and soum centers

Looking at population changes and migrations, the first characteristic of the aimags and soums is the decline of the agroecosystem. In the three targeted aimags (Bayan-Ulgii, Khovd, and Övörkhangai), the aimag and soum centers are very small, on average about 2,000 inhabitants). The figure below shows the actual aggregated population of the three targeted aimag and soum centers. The decline in the population is also reflected by the fact that the number of households during the same period taking the national population growth rate for each year (red line) used as a proxy for natural.

The large amounts of livestock that perished in past events demonstrate herders' vulnerability and the extent to which they are dependent on their livestock. The aimags and soums have been hit by droughts, with some having lost their livelihoods to the severe winter conditions. In 2010, an estimated 75,000 households had half or more of their livestock die.

The aimags and soums are characterized by low- and middle-income households living in loosely aligned plots, with irregular and unprepared settlement patterns. These settlements are often located on steep slopes, which increases the risk of landslides and soil erosion. The aimags and soums have high levels of poverty, with 40% of the population earning less than $2 per day. They also suffer from high rates of unemployment (58%), low literacy rates (37%), and poor access to basic services such as water supply, sanitation, and health care.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 22 OF 126

The shortening of aimag and soum centers are of paramount importance within the current situation because they cannot deliver their potential function as urban centers serving the rural economy, and thus, the rural population. Aimags and soums are the main administrative units in Mongolia that support rural-urban linkages and markets, hindering LCLVC investments. They also do not have adequate facilities and logistics to effectively link and support the livestock herders with the necessary post-production, processing, and marketing services. The aimags and soums are located at a high altitude where they face a high level of pollution and vulnerability to flooding and the absence of social and community facilities make them poorly equipped to provide the required infrastructure for the development of value chains and supply chains. For all these reasons, aimag and soum centers largely fail to attract value chain investments and thus hinder rural-urban linkages in Mongolia.

## The key lever: Reducing animal numbers

To address the twin problem of climate change mitigation and adaptation in the livestock sector, reducing the animal numbers is key. Climate change causes the resources available for the livestock sector to diminish, as water availability decreases, rangeland availability decreases (desiccation), and rangeland's productivity declines. The number of animals that can be sustained by a given area of land depends on its impacts primarily hinges on reducing the number of animals while increasing the ratio of output to the herd. This is because the livestock sector has a high carbon footprint, and climate change increases this impact hence making better use of the diminishing resources, which is important to provide food security to Mongolia's growing population. The reduction in animal numbers will also increase the productivity of rangeland resources, water efficiency measures, breeds that are more resistant to droughts, more sustainable grazing practices, and the adoption of climate-smart agriculture technologies such as agroforestry and silvopasture as well. These also impact reducing the sector's vulnerability to climate change, limiting the herd's increase as a coping mechanism mentioned above.

### Mitigation: Reducing animal numbers

Overgrazing due to ever-increasing herd sizes reduces the amount of carbon stored in grassland soils and increases GHG emissions from CH4 and NO2. A reduction in the number of animals combined with an increase in the output per animal will, with a constant output, reduce the sector's overall GHG emissions by reducing the production of livestock outputs as well.

The main idea is that energy: the amount of biomass is required for the herd to be continued into the future. If the amount of biomass consumed exceeds the carrying capacity, carbon sinks will decline. Energy is required to maintain the animals and produce livestock outputs (meat stock meat, milk, wool, hides, etc.). The more animals there are in a given area, the less energy each animal is "available". Therefore, an increase of the output to herd ratio will reduce the GHG emissions per unit of output and increase carbon sinks in the rangelands while maintaining the same overall output.

## The Intervention: Theory of Change

Achieving the huge potential for increased climate resilience and enhanced carbon sequestration in rangelands and reduced emissions in the livestock sector requires reducing the number of livestock, combined with a change in herd composition and grazing practices (and also increasing the availability of fodder). The current approach to reducing the number of livestock has been to reduce the number of livestock for decades, without success. However, it lacks the funding to carry out this initiative. This is why the ADISDP is being proposed as an alternative to the current initiative because it is perceived to reduce income.** The ADISDP approach to these challenges is through a combination of incentives and disincentives.**

The ADISDP will provide targeted incentives for livestock owners to reduce their herd size per animal in the herd through targeted investments in low-carbon agribusinesses and low-carbon climate-resilient livestock production systems. These investments are expected to increase the output and value-added per head of livestock. The preconditions for these LCVC investments are investments in the infrastructure and urban services of Mongolia's arid and semi-arid centers.

## Increased food security and production while fighting against climate change

Food security issues in Mongolia are largely related to animal losses due to disease outbreaks and poor health conditions, which are exacerbated by a lack of access to veterinary care and low levels of livestock preemption. The climate-related challenges are further exacerbated by the livestock diversification, meaning that livestock losses will be higher than those experienced under more uniform production systems. Herdsmen in Mongolia are particularly vulnerable.

In terms of food security, another issue in Mongolia relates to dietary diversity (i.e., the number of food groups consumed). Herder households generally eat fewer calories from their wheat, meat and milk-based diet; but for poor herders, this is not a problem because they have access to other foods that are not part of livestock production is limited. The project includes support to vegetable growing, which will support agribusiness development in Mongolia.

An approach based on quality reduces risks of livestock losses and thus improves the stability of production and quality of food output (as one area to aid food security).

Through these measures, ADISDP will transform the livestock sector in Mongolia, break with the trend of ever-increasing animal numbers (since 1990), and instead reduce the number of animals, reduce CH4 and N2O emissions.

The often-made assumption is that the ratio between output and number of animals is constant, and in fact reduces. The reality is that the ratio between output and herd size has been increasing over time. This is because the ratio between output and the herd size also reduces animal numbers allows for a migration of the rangelands, which significantly increases the carrying capacity per unit area. This will increase the productivity per animal. These factors make it possible that a reduction in number of animals is partially or completely offset after a period of adjustment, through increases in output per animal.

## Diagram 1: Theory of Change Summary.

### Problem Statement
The development of the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is increasingly being impacted by climate change and urban infrastructure services, leading to reduced productivity and high vulnerability to extreme weather events such as floods. This has resulted in a decline in water availability and increased population density, which has led to an increase in water scarcity and limited productive land availability resulting in a steady decrease in carrying capacity. Additionally, the DRC is also facing challenges related to deforestation and forest degradation.

### Goal
ASDP promotes a paradigm shift in the low-carbon and climate-resilient managed agriculture and urban-transportation sectors of the DRC through the development of institutional arrangements for the implementation of large-scale GHG mitigation with significant replication potential in Mongolia and North Central Asia. Institutional capacity will be developed to ensure that the sector is able to implement and enforce policies and plans for the low-carbon and climate-resilient managed agriculture and urban-transportation sectors.

### Good Practice Statement
The DRC has a high level of institutional capacity for the implementation of large-scale GHG mitigation with significant replication potential in Mongolia and North Central Asia. The DRC has a high level of institutional capacity to ensure that the sector is able to implement and enforce policies and plans for the low-carbon and climate-resilient managed agriculture and urban-transportation sectors.

### Outcomes
1. **Outcome 1:** Climate adaptation and resilience in the agricultural sector will be improved through increased land productivity and reduced vulnerability to extreme weather events.
2. **Outcome 2:** Climate adaptation and resilience in the urban-transportation sector will be improved through increased infrastructure capacity and living conditions for urban residents.

### Indicators
1. **Indicator: 1:** Number of farmers benefiting from climate-resilient agricultural practices.
2. **Indicator: 2:** Number of households with access to clean water and sanitation facilities.
3. **Indicator: 3:** Number of people employed in the low-carbon and climate-resilient managed agriculture sector.

### Conclusion
The DRC has a high level of institutional capacity for the implementation of large-scale GHG mitigation with significant replication potential in Mongolia and North Central Asia. The DRC has a high level of institutional capacity to ensure that the sector is able to implement and enforce policies and plans for the low-carbon and climate-resilient managed agriculture and urban-transportation sectors.

- Concessional and regular grants for energy and energy efficiency investments

## Risks & Barriers
- Lack of finance to address the following constraints:
  - Limited access to roads, infrastructure and services in remote areas
  - Rural-urban linkages and value chain development
  - Non-AIFSD investments
- Lack of awareness and access to income-generating activities
- Lack of interest from herders to move closer to market centers or pasturards

## Key Assumptions
- Continued interest of the Mongolian central government and local governments in addressing climate change impacts, including improving the carbon and nutrient balance on degraded lands.
- Continued interest of the international community in addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation and supporting Mongolia's efforts to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions.
- Interest in other Asian countries to replicate successful models for low-carbon and climate-resilient range management.

## The goal of AGDP is to achieve a transformational change in Mongolian animal husbandry and rangeland management, which is achieved by the localization of sustainable development of the secure and rational mitigation of over 10 million tCO2e per year. This will protect 28.6 million hectares of rangeland ecosystems, and maintain food security in the face of climate change. Achieving these goals, which is fully in line with the Mongolian government's national strategy for sustainable development, requires a number of interventions to help herders make the change to smaller herd sizes and (2) a sustainable source of income if they make this choice. The proposed project will provide both of these interventions: (1) a range of technical assistance that makes the switch to reduce herd sizes and enhance improved sustainable and low-carbon, and climate-resilient range management.

The sustainable sources of income to compensate for reducing the number of animals comprise (1) increasing output and value-added per animal in the herd of participating herders, which can be among

other benefits are achieved through improvements in breeds, feed, access to water, health, and herd management (e.g., earlier offalage)²⁹; (b) improved processing (reducing losses and hence increasing the value-added in the livestock per animal unit); (c) increased use of low-carbon technologies in production processes; and (d) a share of the values added in the value chain that participates herders herein. As elaborated in the Climate Change Assessment (Annex 15), the first two of these lower the number of animals needed to achieve a certain output, while the third increases the output per animal unit. The fourth is achieved through better processing in the agro-processing facilities which are achieved by using low-carbon technologies (embracing emissions reduction and energy efficiency) such as: (a) improved feed conversion ratios; (b) use of renewable raw materials, and using clean and low-carbon processes to manage waste and wastewater).

The above approach targets³⁰ herders who have committed to reducing the number of animals and improving their processing. This is achieved by providing them with access to a range of water resources. It requires specific investments in the Low-carbon Climate-resilient Livestock Value Chain (LC-CLRV) infrastructure, which includes: (a) improved feed conversion ratios; (b) reducing emissions per animal in the herd (point [c] above); and reduce wastage among others by relocating agro-processing facilities to the point of need reducing waste during transport, and by improving the production processes so that they are more efficient. The LC-CLRV infrastructure is designed such that the benefits created within the project is that they share ownership or profits with participating herders represented in co-operatives or other forms of collective organization.

Several conditions need to be met before this approach can be implemented.

This is achieved by providing them with agro-processing and the creation of the conditions to restore their key function in the rural economy where they can be located.

The LC-CLRV infrastructure (e.g., solar panels, insulation for heating, low-carbon waste treatment infrastructure [e.g., anaerobic digestion, water treatment, climate-proofed roads]), partially inside agro-parks and partially inside agmas and sown communities. This is achieved by providing them with the necessary inputs to build the LC-CLRV infrastructure at the end of the transfer payments, however using low-carbon and climate-resilient technologies instead of the out-dated ones. The LC-CLRV infrastructure is designed such that it can be maintained after the end of the transfer payments.

## B

**Aim:** The rangeland centers need to have sufficient infrastructure to fulfill their role in the LCLVCs as processing and market centers. Without adequate energy and heat supply, water supply, waste management facilities, communication networks, etc., the LCLVCs will not be able to function effectively (see also the description of Output 1 in B.3). To attract and retain professional service providers, these centers also need to have a good working environment. This output motivates ASIDP’s investments in the amirag and rangeland centers to complement the rangeland activities.

**Climate change poses significant additional challenges for the centers:** Temperatures have been increasing and are expected to continue doing so (see Section 3). The frequency of extreme weather events has also increased, especially in short-term events. Heavy rain and flash flooding, squally winds, hail, and lightning can cause damage to infrastructure and equipment, as well as loss of livestock and other assets (see Section 2 for targeted aims), making investments in wastewater treatment and waste management prevent health problems in the centers.

The Third National Communication (p. 200-207) describes that extreme weather and climate events have been increasing, especially in extreme short-term events. Heavy rain and flash flooding, squally winds, hail, and lightning can cause damage to infrastructure and equipment, as well as loss of livestock and other assets.

About 51% of total extreme events and disasters belong to the short-range events, and 40% of these are flash floods. The number of extreme events has increased by about 27% in the last two decades (see Section 3). However, the number of disasters that lead to damage and damages have increased by twice as much in the last 20 years. However, this is not necessarily due to this; the consequence of climate change and that climate change is a causality factor in the ASIDP situation.

Therefore, ASIDP will also invest management and wastewater treatment as urban adaptation measures and investment in energy and heat supply, water supply, waste management facilities, communication networks by GCF funding. Apart from this, urban mitigation investment (solar PV, insulation), of which only solar PV panels are mentioned here.

The 2.0 creations of incentives for reducing the number of animals to achieve low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management requires the organization of herders into groups that use the same pasture areas. This is called Pasture User Groups (PUGs). These groups are organized in larger groups collectively. ASIDP does this through Pasture User Groups (PUGs) established by SDC, based on historical and traditional grazing practices. The members of a PUG are called the "members," while those who are not called the "neighboring herders." Output 2 provides the incentives for the herders to get better organized and to implement improved low-carbon, climate-resilient rangeland management and reduce herd sizes. This is done by providing them with a marketable product (see Box 3). The incentive must be bottom-up and be on based on herders' willingness to participate. Raising awareness among the herders about the benefits of reducing herd size will be an important part of this output, as well as raising awareness over rangeland use, and the necessity that these issues can only be resolved collectively is an important step. ASIDP will provide incentives for the herders to reduce their herd sizes (see Box 3). Based on traditional rotational grazing management, this will lead to a more sustainable social-ecological system in the rangelands. This output also addresses the lack of market access, without which the model might be more difficult to implement (see Insert 9) successfully. Output 2 also creates incentives for herders to reduce herd sizes and improve their livestock management practices to maintain animal productivity through harsh winters, and addresses the low health of animals, so that outputs can be maintained and premature deaths can be avoided. By providing herders with better veterinary services, ASIDP will help them to address these issues (see Box 3). This output also addresses the reasons for increasing herd sizes (the wish to have a viable herd after calamities such as droughts, exacerbation of poverty, lack of knowledge and awareness about the benefits of reducing herd size, lack of traceability and certification, leading to an expected improvement in marketability and price premiums for products, lack of access to markets, lack of financial resources, lack of information on public investments, lack of knowledge and examples of ways to reduce herd sizes while improving herd productivity, lack of awareness about the benefits of reducing herd size, lack of traceability and certification). ASIDP addresses these barriers by making finance available, creating various support


---
**Page 31**
---

system (Output 2), and documenting these systems and knowledge gained during their implementation (Output 4).

3. Sustainable increases in herder incomes to compensate for reducing the number of animals requires investments in LCLVCs. Partially these investments follow from the Low-carbon Climate-resilient Agribusiness Development Plans (LCAADPs), which are developed with the support from Output 1, investment in capacity building and policy development.

1. Provide herders with additional income resulting from their share of the profits (or income ownership shares) generated by LCLVCs. This is achieved through a combination of direct payments to herders as an integral part of the climate change mitigation and adaptation strategy to reduce GHG emissions and increase resilience, and through the development of new markets for products that are more resilient in dealing with resource base and cope with climate change.

2. Provide herders with access to improved services, improved access to water, and facilities to help them adapt to climate change and lower their GHG emissions and grazing pressure:

3. Include the adoption of climate-resilient technology and promote the productive use of waste materials in processing; and

4. Retracte resources to facilitate the point of need, meeting the requirements for transport, and reducing the time spent by herders on these activities.

Lack of finance is the main bottleneck for private sector investments into LCLVCs. ASDIP addresses this barrier through a combination of public and private sector financing mechanisms. The core of ASDIP provides a low-cost and long-term source of financing for investments into the LCLVC by the private sector, while also providing a mechanism to recover costs from the sale of products generated in the LCLVC. This is achieved through a combination of concessional financing, where the only source of semi-self-financing is in the form of payments for mitigation results, as discussed above, and public sector co-funding, which provides additional funding for investments into this area. This means that ASDIP's financing will be partially concessional and pave the way for non-concessional financing from private investors to invest in LCLVCs through the provision of ecosystem services through the proposed partnership, so creating an exit strategy to replace GCF and public sector financing with payments that are based on the ecosystem and carbon services from improved rangeland management.

To implement the approach indicated in 1-3 above and to monitor the results of the implementation process, a number of outputs will be generated. These include: Output 2, which is described in Output 4; building the capacity, establishing the MRV system and producing the knowledge products that can be used by herders and policy makers; and Output 5, which is described in Output 6. Outside Mongolia, this output will create the policy mechanisms and the capacity to formulate, implement, monitor and enforce policies for sustainable rangeland management at national level.

Both inside and outside Mongolia, the Partnership for Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management (LCCRM) will be established as a platform for knowledge sharing and exchange. If this is achieved, providing a way to mobilize the needed finance for sustainable rangeland management.

It is important to note that the overall model structure does not aim to reduce the number of herders or to change the herders' way of life. Instead the aim is to decrease their vulnerability, establish a more sustainable livelihood and improve their resilience in dealing with climate change. This will also provide more jobs in urban areas for people living in urban areas. Marginal herders may decide to stop herding and take up other forms of employment.

The above description elaborates the ASDIP approach and the barriers that need to be overcome. ASDIP implementation is expected to produce the knowledge, experience and case studies that demonstrate the viability of this approach. The results will show how sustainable rangeland management practices and animal husbandry can contribute to mitigation that is economically and financially viable. It will also provide example of how specific systems and mechanisms can be created to support the shift towards low-carbon

The Partnership for Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management in Asia will make it possible to mobilize financial funding through results-based payments (linked only to mitigation results) for the replication of the project activities in other pilot areas, and in Asia as a whole, with the aim of scaling up and replicating them.

The combination of knowledge creation, formulation of replicable mechanisms and systems, transformation of rangeland management and animal husbandry to become low-carbon and climate-resilient, and a mechanism to replicate the successes of ASDIP outside and inside Mongolia means that ASDIP can be expected to provide paradigms for other rangeland management initiatives in Asia and beyond.

## 1.3 Strategic Objectives (more specifically, approximately 4 projects)

The implementation of the theory of change components under ASDIP will be delivered through a road map composed of the following four strategic, transformative objectives: (i) upgrade (revive) arid and semi-arid centers into low-carbon and climate-resilient urban settlements with improved living conditions to anchor low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices; (ii) provide incentives, organizational structure and transitional support for herders to adopt low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices in their livelihoods; (iii) sustain herders' low-carbon and climate-resilient practices; and (iv) build the capacity and awareness for formulating and implementing national-level rural development plans, policy and, the knowledge and MRV system necessary for replication.

The translation of these strategic objectives into physical and non-physical interventions into a sequenced set of outputs will be achieved through four output streams. The specific activities and interventions targeted will constitute the scope of ASDIP. According to the theory of change, ASDIP has four output streams that address the identified barriers and risks and deliver the program's strategic objectives.

### Output 1: Low-carbon and climate-resilient urban settlements

Output 1 consists of a series of urban centers developed to implement priority low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services to promote the role of these centers as anchors for low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices in rural, semi-rural economy, and to improve the living conditions in these arid and inter-seasim centers. The output will address the following barriers: (i) lack of access to basic infrastructure services; (ii) energy deficiencies (water, power, wastewater treatment, waste collection and treatment, district heating, drainage, etc.) that are not sustainable or affordable for rural communities; (iii) lack of access to renewable energy in the form of solar panels, energy efficiency through insulation and efficient heat exchange systems, and other technologies; (iv) lack of a low-carbon and climate-resilient urban redevelopment approach including affordable housing supply, will upgrade the arid centers' sub-gerdarian areas, which are the main sources of CO2 emission and pollution during winter, address water scarcity in these areas, and provide access to public services such as health care. A park specifically dedicated to CCIVL will be developed, including utilities adequate for the development of urban centers. The output will also address the following risks: (i) lack of a low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services upgrade of Arid centers; (ii) Low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services upgrades of Urigam centers; (iii) Low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services upgrades of Intersium centers; (iv) Implementation of smart land management system.

### Output 2: Rangelands managed for climate-resilience, high carbon sequestration, and sustainable herding

Output 2 consists of a series of rangeland management practices that will be implemented to maintain food production despite a dwindling resource base, increase rangeland carbon sinks and reduce livestock GHG emissions through incentives and improve organizational structures and transitional support for herders to adopt low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices. It will support herders' organizations (PUGs, cooperatives & associations) by implementing

Through the Green Inclusive Regional Agribusiness Fund (GIRAF), it will provide financial products designed to support herders cooperatives and SMEs in implementing output 2, which is LCLVC. The GIRAF will use innovative financial products such as low-carbon and climate-resilient livestock with more reasonable lower-interest rates, higher loan limits, longer grace periods, higher loan values, and an adjustable repayment period that take into account the production cycle of herders cooperatives and SMEs. These financial products will be provided to herders cooperatives and SMEs as collateral substitutes and reduce the risks of the financiers thereby contributing to lower interest rates, and for the first time in the region, it will provide a revolving fund for the development of pioneering low-carbon and climate-resilient agribusiness enterprises by advancing green agriculture products, processes, and business model innovations. Output 3 has two activities:

(i) Provide financial products grants for herders cooperatives and SMEs;

(ii) Provide financial products grants for low-carbon and climate-resilient livestock value chain to qualified herders and SMEs.

Under output 4, consulting services will be provided on the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and management and implementation. It will also provide technical support services to establish and operate the GIRAF (under output 2), which is tasked to provide accessible innovative and climate-smart financing.

## Box 1: Complementarity and synergy with other initiatives to maximize climate impact and support replication

The significant number of ongoing and planned complementary initiatives in Mongolia (as described in Section B.1 and Annex B) represent great learning exchanges and cooperation opportunities. Because those previous and ongoing efforts are already well underway, they can be leveraged for the purposes of this project. This will allow us to anchor rural development into a sustainable, low-carbon, climate-resilient framework effectively. These interconnections are as follows:

1. Cooperative approaches among herders for pasture management have been developed for the sustainability of rangelands and livestock production in Mongolia. The cooperative approach can be scaled up to increase overall productivity and create stronger linkages between herders and more efficient carbon-based energy systems.
2. The project will address the necessary reduction in emissions from livestock production, which would longer-term benefit the environment and human health. However, it is important to note that some of these institutional arrangements with impacts on the market are also missing. One important aspect to consider is how the project can complement existing efforts.

ADAPTIP will approach these from the mitigation angle, while other donor projects, including the proposed UNDP project, will approach these issues from the adaptation angle. ADSP will coordinate with other donors to ensure that recommendations and lessons learned from this project are shared across all of them.

consider is that some initiatives related to low-carbon and sustainable rangeland management are aligned with the ASDIP approach (see Annex 25) because they are built on the same herders' organization platform implemented by GCF.

* Investments into value chains are focusing on economic development and herder productivity without necessarily considering climate change adaptation or resilience. The focus is more on increasing livestock production, which may lead to overgrazing in some areas. ASDIP's specific needs for investing locally: Investment in the value chains also does not address the need to invest in the infrastructure and services that are required to support sustainable rangeland management.

* Projects implementing the priority infrastructure and urban services barely take into account the necessary linkages with climate change adaptation, including low-carbon and sustainable rangeland management and the water sector, especially for desertified lands.

The gaps in each category of interventions described above call for a more integrated and comprehensive modal approach. The UNDP project on Improving Adaptive Capacity and Risk Management of Rural Communities in Mongolia aims to address these gaps by focusing on four areas: (1) climate information; (2) water use planning; (3) sustainable rangeland management, and a climate-smart agribusiness value chain. The approach is premised on each initiative being implemented through the same herders' organization platform that has been established under the 10-year multi-stakeholder implementation structure to build and capitalize on existing local interventions while bringing them into line with the ASDIP approach. This will be achieved by combining a participatory, demand-driven, and results-oriented program strategy combined with the necessary policy and institutional reforms and implementation support, and a strict set of eligibility criteria for project selection. The UNDP project is designed to complement the ASDIP approach in terms of program components and use resources more strategically to cover a wider geographical scope (see also Section D.2).

### Complementary and Synergy with UNDP project

The UNDP project for Improving Adaptive Capacity and Risk Management of Rural Communities in Mongolia aims to address these gaps by focusing on four areas: (1) climate information; (2) water use planning at the national and sub-national levels; (3) sustainable rangeland management, and a climate-smart agribusiness value chain. The project will be implemented through three outputs:

* Output 1: Disseminating climate information into land and water use planning at the national and sub-national levels
* Output 2: Scaling up climate-resilient water and soil management practices for enhanced small-scale herder livelihoods
* Output 3: Build herder capacity to access markets for sustainably sourced, climate-resilient livestock products

Through its outputs 2 and 3, UNDP aims to improve herders' capacity and organization for sustainable rangeland management and the development of a climate-smart agribusiness value chain that will support the production of livestock products. These are similar activities that ASDIP Output 2 (Activities 1 and 2) and Output 4 (Activity 2) will implement, respectively. The UNDP project will also be complementary to the GCF-funded Mongolia Adaptation Program, and using RUAs for monitor overgrazing and sustainable rangeland management (see Box C). This means that the two projects can work together in a coordinated manner to achieve their respective objectives while sharing outputs/activities.

* ASDIP Output 1 will address a key challenge related to lack of facilities and services to herders and to agribusiness processing industry that prevent them to be located at the point of needs, closer to rangeland and better connected to markets.
* ASDIP Output 2 will complement UNDP Herder Producer Organizations (or cooperatives) by providing the necessary infrastructure and services for sustainable rangeland management. The project will also bring into line (through Output 2 Activities 3 and 4) essential complementary components that will contribute to reduce the vulnerability of herders, including access to credit and insurance schemes, improved livestock health and disease control, better market information, and a wider range of inputs and services.
* ASDIP Output 3 by providing effective access to finance and credit risk guarantees for CLVC (climate sensitive VC) for UNDP through the use of RUAs. This will allow herders to invest in sustainable rangeland management practices that are more productive per fodder hectare, and animal health and disease free establishment.
* ASDIP Output 4 by providing a market access platform for sustainably sourced products which otherwise will have difficulty to materialize (10). This will also be highly complementary with the UNDP project as it is expected that sustainable rangeland management will be most probably unsustainably practiced without market access.

The GCF-funded Mongolia Adaptation Program will develop a set of enabling conditions (e.g., development of knowledge products) that will be developed in close coordination and supervision of MOFA/II. It will align their policy support, local planning documents, and results monitoring framework with the UNDP project. The two projects will also share their respective program components to jointly develop under MOFA/II supervision to ensure full compatibility. ASDIP includes a

### Supporting targeted Aimag and Soum centers as anchors of low-carbon climate-resilient livestock value chains

Infrastructure gaps and deficiencies in targeted Aimag and soum centers have created human settlements with high CO2 emission, are polluting and offer substandard living condition for their residents. Restoring these areas to a more sustainable state will require addressing the following infrastructure needs (e.g., slaughterhouses, storage facilities, feedlots, banking services) and developing services specifically dedicated to livestock production in rural areas. ASDIP will address key infrastructure and service needs (water, power, wastewater treatment, roads, affordable housing for the former herders using low-carbon technologies (renewable energy, solar panels), heat, roads, affordable housing for the former herders using low-carbon waste and wastewater treatment technologies) to decrease urban CO2 emissions and urban pollution by creating more livable and carbon-neutral urban areas. In addition, these services will address water shortages in rural areas.

* * *

Aimags are administrative divisions of Mongolia that cover a large area but have limited infrastructure accommodating almost 70% of the population. 20 to 35% of this population, depending on the targeted aimag center, will be connected with clean service resulting in a significant GHG reduction, much more than the generation due to the improved service delivery.

and adapt and reinforced centers against flooding.
