## Current Situation
With ASDIP

### Organizing and financing the initial stage of the transition process for the herders - Output 2 (Activity 1) and Output 4 (Activity 2)
ASDIP support will be based on Pastoralist User Group (PUG) system established by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC) under the "Green Gold Project" from the traditional Mongolian structures for herding. Each PUG will commit to and implement Hangerland Use Plans (HUPs), which are a set of rules that define how land is used, including grazing areas, annual quotas for reducing the size of its herd, targets for changes in livestock composition, and improved grazing practices. The HUPs will be developed with the support of the Pastoralist Herd Management Program (PHMP) that identifies and prioritizes short- to medium-term investments to improve rangeland management and increase productivity. The PHMP is a key component of the low-carbon and climate-resilient rangelands management process, the primary mitigation financing mechanism for herders in Mongolia. It will be used as an investment framework for the identification and implementation of the RAs. The matching grants will be co-financed by the PUGs through cash or in-kind contributions. The PHMP will also provide technical assistance to the PUGs during the development (such as water wells, shelters, and hay fields) with a strong adaptation and to a lesser degree mitigation focus, using Community Participation in Procurement (CPP). These matching grants are appropriate to improve rangeland management practices that can be implemented without affording significant financial risk.

### Developing the capacity of certified livestock herders to increase their investment returns or income during the transition process - Output 2 (Activity 2) and Output 4 (Activity 4)
RAs with signed RUA are issued by the project to eligible herders who have signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with RUsA and integrate into a cooperative. The cooperative in this case will be an economic structure providing services for its members, including access to markets, credit facilities, and other services. Its objectives are to create benefits to members who will be engaged in low-carbon and climate-resilient rangelands management process. These benefits can be summarized as follows: (i) increase livestock production; (ii) improve grazing practices and herds composition; improve planning; (iii) defend the interest of its members; and (iv) improve access to finance and investment. A cooperative is supported by the project only if it is comprised of certified herders.

## Simultaneously, ASDIP will put in place traceability and certification systems at the cooperative, PUG and herder levels.

These systems enable monitoring how the herd is managed, including feed management, disease mitigation and adaptation. Thus, herders, PUGs, and cooperatives who get certified will be eligible to receive GCF funding.

### Improving animal health and fodder production - Output 2 (Activity 3 and 4) are key measures in the adaptation and mitigation strategy to reduce the herd size needed to achieve a given output, so that the pastoral system can adapt to climate change. The project will also contribute to reducing the GHG emission per unit of output produced. It contributes to improve herd value added (higher selling prices) due to gained weight and quality, use of all animal products, higher amount of outputs / head and better health status. The project will also contribute to improving fodder production. ASDIP includes activities at different levels: (i) At the herder group (PUG) level, which is the smallest production unit in the pastoral system; (ii) At the cooperative level, which is a larger production unit that provides veterinary assistance and raises herders' awareness. The CAHVs will be paid by the project in the first years but will gradually become self-sustained through income from their activities such as selling milk to dairy laboratories (for animal health control and food safety) will be built; to be used by private veterinary clinics, livestock producers, and other stakeholders. The cooperative will also provide services for disease-free establishments. The disease-free establishment will integrate the following functions: (i) Implementing a biosecurity plan that includes the use of vaccines, dewormers, and other tools for certification for exportation; and (ii) Feed the animals and check-up for selling. The access to the disease free establishment will be given in priority to animals coming from participating or certified LD/USG projects. This is considered a key intervention.


---
**Page 40**
---

Lack of fodder supply is a major constraint for herders to adapt to climate change and manage their herds through the dry season. Lack of fodder supply directly and indirectly (through a feedback mechanism to manage expected losses) increases the rangeland's grazing pressure.[1] It is one of the main constraints that currently limit the livestock economy function only a few months a year, during the spring and summer seasons when there are sufficient resources for the maintenance and growth of their herds. Adequate production of high-nutritious fodder crop plants sold at an affordable price would allow the pastoralists to diversify their livelihoods by selling surplus fodder crops in addition to the sale of their herds of herders. Under ASPID, the creation or rehabilitation of irrigated perimeter together with water conservation and storage structures will be carried out in order to increase the availability of water for the reconstruction of the head and the main structure of irrigation systems for fodder crop plantations and the production of high-nutritious fodder crops. The ASPID project will also provide technical assistance combined with financing support as part of Output 3 for private sector investment in irrigated perimeter and fodder crop farming activity.

Low-carbon Climate-resilient Agribusiness Development Plan (LCADP) and Low-carbon Climate resilient Livestock Value Chain (LCLVC). Simultaneously, under Output 4 (Activity 2 and 3), ASPID will also support a participative, consensus-building formulation of LCLVC. The LCADP will identify priority investments in the livestock sector that are aligned with the climate-resilient development pathway for certified herders, PUGs, and/or cooperatives with low-carbon and climate-resilience opportunities for investment in livestock value chains that are more resilient to climate change and better adapted to sustainable rangeland management. Here LCLVC means that the full range of herders, herder groups, and other stakeholders will be involved in the formulation of a consensus-based plan for using locally derived materials into food, beverage, fiber, animal skin, and leather products are sold to final consumers and/or exported. ASPID will support the development of value chains (including processing, packaging, regenerative practices, adapts to climate change, and its impacts on water availability, land availability and land productivity reduces GHG emissions, including carbon sequestration), in all consideration sectors (production, transformation, consumption, processing, distribution, and consumption) but also considers the extended value chain (proportion of inputs, production, transformation, consumption, processing, packaging, distribution, and consumption) in community and urban-rural infrastructure to pursue a low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathway for livestock. ASPID will support the formulation of an integrated strategy that includes investments and adaptation finance, technology development and transfer, effective institutions, policies and governance, and capacity building for herders, PUGs, and cooperatives in order to implement the LCLVC through this process. ASPID will use pioneering approaches to foster paradigm shifts away from the traditional barriers and constraints of livestock production and towards a more sustainable rangeland management with innovations, a policy environment more conducive to sustainable rangeland management, and stronger institutions capable of implementing the LCLVC.

Leveraging mitigation and adaptation finance for private sector investments incorporating performance-based payments for certified herders, PUGs, and cooperatives in low-carbon climate-resilient livestock value chains – Output 3. The establishment of the Green Inclusive Regional Agroforestry Fund (GIRAF) will be structured to provide a financial structure that attracts private sector investment into the LCLVC, and expand the scope of certified herders, PUGs, and cooperatives to affordable mitigation and adaptation finance for investments in livestock value chains. The GIRAF will also allow the use of proceeds to mobilize private sector resources that would otherwise not be allocated or invested in sustainable rangeland management and climate-resilient livestock production. This will be achieved directly from the LCVC investments by financing only those that are included in the LCADP and those with explicit performance-based payments for certified herders, PUGs, and cooperatives in low-carbon climate-resilient enterprises. The GIRAF will be structured to provide the mitigation and adaptation finance that will help implement the LCADP beyond the transition phase through identified priority investments in livestock value chains that are aligned with the LCLVC. ## B

In the communal era before 1991, amag and soum centers played a key role in anchoring livestock value chains by providing services such as veterinary care, feed supply, and access to markets. However, these centers were not built with external support and could not be operated and maintained on that basis after 1991. Infrastructure fell into disrepair, and the lack of maintenance has led to a decline in service quality. The current infrastructure is outdated, badly maintained, maladapted to climate change, inefficient, and emits significant amounts of GHG. In addition, the number of livestock per household has increased significantly since 1990 (from about 25 in the Soviet period, 40 to 50 years ago) have exceeded their planned service life or have been deteriorated; are inefficient and emit large amounts of GHG. The lack of infrastructure and services is a major factor contributing to the negative consequences on the targeted amag and soum centers attractiveness for private sector investments into LCLVCAs used in the program.

### Key Issues

1. Most of the living areas in the amag centers are prone to flooding due to the lack of drainage; this is affecting:
   - Water supply
   - Air and soil pollution, among others caused by the current dumpsite located near housing areas, without any proper waste management system.
2. Current wastewater treatment facilities are not properly functioning, leading to domestic and industrial wastewater leaking into water bodies.

### In the amag centers, the ger homes (where between 60% and 70% of the amag centers' populations) are concentrated in poor condition dormitories with no heating or cooling systems, no running water, no electricity, and unimproved pit latrines, a highly polluted environment because of coat heating, lack of access to social facilities, limited access to health services, and poor sanitation. The ger homes are also located on the periphery where winter is often worse than Ulaanbaatar itself (which is the worst polluted cities in the world in winter); and the living areas are not properly drained.

### Benefits

1. Improved infrastructure will contribute and reinforce the poor attractiveness for investments of these cities.
2. The amag and soum centers currently do not have the infrastructure needed to host agro-processing industries and limited access to environmental infrastructure that can attract and keep professionals to provide services. Output 1 targets and eliminates infrastructure gaps that have been identified over a series of studies and reports; thus, this output will contribute to the creation of new jobs in these areas as well as to the fact that the existing jobs are more efficient and cleaner.

### Output 1 Introduction

The focus for Output 1 is on developing comprehensive and integrated approaches to tackle low-valuation and degraded infrastructure services and livelihoods in targeted amag and soum centers. The design of this output will be based on a participatory approach involving the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Energy and Armag and soum governments, and community consultations; integrating all stakeholders' expectations. Adaptation is a key component of Output 1 as it includes the development of climate change adaptation plans for economic and redevelopment programs and what interventions are required to support urban upgrading and economic development in targeted amag and soum centers. The output will also include the installation of renewable energy, insulation, efficient heating supply plants, bio-waste treatment water facilities). Adaptation is a key component of Output 1 as it includes the development of climate change adaptation plans for the design and management aspects of targeted amaggs, as well as Mongolian standards and regulations have been studied to ensure that all interventions are in line with the proposed solutions. Applicable international standards have been used and will be applied.

### Output 1 aims

Output 1 aims to eliminate the deficit in strategic investments in amag centers, in particular in areas facing the risk of flooding due to a high shortage of drainage and flood protection. ASDIP also includes the removal of the out-of-date heating systems and the installation of new efficient heating systems, as well as the installation of insulation for heating and power supply. Output 1 will also introduce the installation of solar panels to supply housing buildings with electricity in areas where this is feasible for the program.

## Residential areas surrounding the "imagé" core-centres have grown without proper infrastructure.

This generates a high density of small and haphazardly constructed houses, which prevents proper urban management by private sector investment, and makes it more difficult to attract professionals. Because of the low income of the population, the majority of these residents are unable to pay for basic services such as water supply and sanitation.

Tranche 1 will pilot a combined approach of street infrastructure development, housing connection, and densification through urban renewal. The project will be implemented in three areas: the "imagé" core-centres, the peri-urban area, and part of a perimeter of about 40 hectares, which should be developed during the next tranche, with a possible extension to other parts of the city.

The aim is to target at least around 30% of the total ger area population in the three imagé areas, it means about 15,000 people. The peri-urban area will be targeted for the development of new infrastructure and roads. It aims to target at least around 30% of the total ger area population in the three imagé areas, it means about 15,000 people.

### Design and scope of the agro-parks are based on local economic development potential and target, LCCV analysis and consultation with local business. It represents the first investments of the project that will be complemented by financing from other sources (private sector, national government, etc.).

The agro-parks will include: (i) a central market for the sale of agricultural products; (ii) an urban farm center, infrastructure and services for treating the waste and supplying the necessary urban services for economic activities in the agro-parks; (iii) a public transport hub to connect the agro-parks with other urban activities; and (iv) 10 ha of "agri-centers", the agro-parks are scaled to host LCCV activities.

In the inter-sous-centres meeting similar issues linked to lack of urban services encountered, are concentrated in the peri-urban areas. The main issue is the lack of infrastructure at the service levels. In addition to the upgrading of infrastructure networks and waste collection and treatment, a major improvement of the public buildings (administration, health centers, schools) is needed for the development of the peri-urban areas and restore a sound urban environment as reliable basis for the local economic expansion (Outputs 2 and 3).

Infrastructure for the LCCV is also supported by the implementation of agro-parks (3 to 5 ha in size) close to the existing peri-urban areas. The agro-parks will be designed with an emphasis on urban agriculture, which are designed on similar assumptions and basis as for agro-centers.

Finally, the Smart Land Management Center will provide an essential tool to improve land and urban management: (i) develop a database for land administration and territorial management; (ii) develop a database for soil condition, and carbon sequestration in the soil. It will establish a monitoring network and laboratory for land and soil condition and quality, including software development and hardware supply, processing and application of satellite data and protocol integration with existing system; (iii) develop an urban cadaster database and system: software development and hardware supply, field measurement and data collection; and (iv) implement institutional development and capacity building and training program, and public information and outreach program.

## Box 3. PUG and RUA approach underpinning Output 2

There are three types of herder organizations:

- The Pasture User Groups – PUGs – related to pastures governance. They regulate pasture usage through agreements with local governments, establish grazing areas, define the number of animals and establishing contracts with soum governments using Rangeland Use Agreements (RUAs).
- The cooperatives – CCAFs – are formed by herders for collective action in order to improve their livelihoods such as bonuses for wool, cashmere and hide, provide linkages to the market, and develop infrastructure for health services.
- The Saving and Credit cooperatives – financial institutions providing loans to their members.

However, in most soums, these organizations are still weak and needs support and capacity building to function properly. In addition, there is a need to strengthen the cooperation between herder organizations (PUGs, cooperatives and SCC) as requires in depth cooperation among herders.

A list of eight elements that should be considered for the implementation of PUGs: 1) Awareness raising among herders and traditional grazing area users; 2) Establishment of PUGs; Ecological Site Description (ESD); 3) Development of a management plan; 4) Capacity building; 5) Implementation of the User Agreement; 6) Management plan; 7) Adjustment of the rules and monitoring of the RUA (see the illustration below). The whole process combines the development of the PUGs with the establishment of the RUAs, which is based on scientific evidence. The main difference between the two approaches lies in the fact that the management plan needs to be agreed upon by the local authorities which usually have to substantially contribute to it, especially once the rules are established and the RUA is signed. There is no specific regulation for rangeland management yet in the Law land there, but exist two provisions in the law that allow for the establishment of PUGs and RUAs. The first important regulation for rangelands as a group with the local authority. The second important regulation for rangeland management is the implementation of the grazing planning methodology introduced in 2006 to ensure enforcement of the Law Land. Implementation of this methodology is the mandate of ALAMCAG which has branches in all soums and land managers in each soum.

## The 8 main elements of the PUG/RUA approach

1) Administratively, Mongolia is divided into 21 provinces, called aimags, and one capital city. Each aimag is divided into soums, and each soum are further subdivided into bairgs.

ALAMAG, the National Federation of PUGs (NFUPG), and the National Agency for Meteorology and Environmental Monitoring (NAMEN) have been involved in the development of a baseline rangeland health monitoring system under the Green Health Project (GG AHP). ALAMAG and NAMEN represent the State (owner of the rangelands) and NFUPG represents the PUGs. The GG AHP has assisted ALAMAG in developing a new software called ASDP which will be linked with information on soil carbon content selected sample sites to further increase the value of the data collected. The project will provide technical support and advice to the existing cooperatives for the implementation of their business plan. Cooperatives are to play a key role in the development of the livestock value chain by providing services such as veterinary care, feed supply, traceability and certifications systems. Cooperatives will manage key assets for production and will be supported to invest in infrastructure and equipment to improve productivity and profitability of herds belonging to cooperative members and herders. Also, the cooperatives will be supported to invest partner with private sector entities who invest in processing and marketing of livestock products. The cooperative will continue to establish the green agro-business plan at scoum and interrum/aimang level.

## Box 4. High mountain water conservancy pilot

The high mountain water accumulation is an important water saving and storage investment that will help to cope with the impact of climate change on the precipitation patterns. This is a high priority of the Government of Mongolia. The project aims at developing a new approach for sustainable management of high mountain water resources in Mongolia, which will reduce the adverse impacts of climate changes in the Mongolian Altai.

The pilot is part of Output 2, activity 3 of GSP2, which consists of two linked sub-activities: Sub-activity 1 aimed to imitated schemes and Sub-activity 2 aimed at an ecosystem approach for sustainable mountain water conservation. The water harvesting pilot will be used in the imitated scheme. Fodder production sequesters carbon at the rate of slightly more than 0.5 tCO2e/ha/year.

In addition, there is herders' investment which represents about 10% total capital investment and 30% of fixed charges. The herders' investment will be used to construct water harvesting structures and other infrastructure that will increase ownership feeling and appreciation by herders, thus ensuring higher sustainability. Amounts indicated are thus ceiling amounts.


---
**Page 51**
---

more than 100 USD per hectare per year and supports the reduction of grazing intensity by providing an additional source of animal feed and increasing output per animal**[3]**.

The second sub-activity (the high mountain water conservation pilot) aims at building the climate changes resilience capacity in Mongolia’s high mountain ecosystems. The project will focus on the upper catchments of rivers located in western Mongolia through glacier melt water and precipitation collection and management measures, which will help to reduce the risk of desertification and improve the condition of grasslands, wildlife and freshwater lake ecosystems. The investment activity will collect and accumulate glacier melt water, spring runoff, and snowmelt from the high mountain catchments of the rivers Khovd, Torgoo, Khar-Uul, Dornod, Kharkhorin, Turgen and Khovd rivers in the western part of Mongolia. The collected water will be used for irrigation purposes to improve the condition of grasslands and other ecosystems, including wetland systems, such as lakes and groundwater. Water will be collected in river bed associated ponds and partially dammed reservoirs. This sub-activity aims at reducing the risk of desertification by improving the capacity of communities to climate change, in particular through the stabilization of water resources, the reversal of the current trend of land degradation, and the improvement of grassland condition. The project will also reduce the possible shutdown of traditionally irrigated crop production, which served as a primary income source for certain local communities. In addition, this sub-activity aims at improving the resilience capacity of communities by ensuring the availability of water for feed production. Availability of feed in winter is crucial to main animal wealth and productivity. The project will also reduce the risk of desertification through improved grassland management practices that increase soil moisture retention and improve water availability more often. In addition, this sub-activity takes away the main risk factor of feed production and provides a new source of income for communities by improving the condition of wetlands and lakes. This will improve productivity of animals and herds, and reduces input per animal. Moreover, earlier analyses concluded with ADB that the project could also contribute to climate change mitigation through its potential to sequester carbon, independent of the impact on rangelands and GHG intensity of animal husbandry outputs**[4]**.

In addition, the sub-activity has a direct favorable impact on rangelands. As plant coverage on the ground is reduced, water absorption decreases, and palatable plants are not able to grow due to lack of soil moisture. Rangeland degradation can be reversed by increasing the amount of water available for grasses through revegetation. At this time, it is important to create favorable conditions for the soil to absorb and retain a sufficient supply of water**[5]**. This is exactly what the sub-activity aims to do.


Output 3 is implemented through the MIF (Mongolia Investment Facility) component of the MIF. It includes the following activities in Transcripts 1,2 and 3; and the same activities will be replicated in Transcripts 2 and 3: Activity 1 - Provision of accessible low-carbon and climate-resilient agriculture loans and credit guarantees to herders and enterprises.

Lending Window 1. To provide low-carbon and climate-resilient agribusiness loans through qualified banks for small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in partnership agreements with government agencies, including the Ministry of Agriculture and Natural Resources, to finance activities advancing processing, commercial and logistics investments for retail and exports; and other forms of economic development.

Lending Window 2. To provide direct low-carbon and climate-resilient agribusiness loans to qualified enterprises that are engaged in sustainable rangeland management through business planning, marketing, and export. The enterprises to qualify must have business plans that include a clearly articulated strategy for the adoption of best practices in sustainable rangeland management; and be engaged in sustainable rangeland management. They must also demonstrate congruence and consistency with the project.

# Summary of the Green Inclusive Regional Agribusiness Fund (GIRAF)

## Traditional barriers to access to agriculture finance: The World Bank Group (WBG) has been a challenge for the agriculture sector (including the livestock industry) in developing countries for decades due to perceptions of low profitability, high risk, and limited collateral. These factors have therefore been limiting their exposure to the sector, raising interest rates, tightening the lending criteria, and reducing access to finance. The lack of financial inclusion has also led to a decline in productivity and lower returns from other sectors of the economy. The three main barriers that have traditionally limited the access to agriculture finance are: (i) high transaction costs; (ii) missing information on agricultural assets; and (iii) missing returns from agriculture literature—are (iv) high transaction costs), (v) inadequate enabling environments, and (vi) lack of financial inclusion.

## The GIRAF fund structure and management

For the delivery of Output 3: financing and implementation arrangements will facilitate the application of a blended climate change mitigation and adaptation finance strategy to support the transition towards sustainable rangeland management. This will be achieved through the establishment of an AMC, which will manage the fund on behalf of the WBG. Under the proposed FLP, components of ASIPF, the Asset Management Company of DIMC (AMC-DIMC) will establish and manage the fund. The AMC will also be responsible for implementing the climate change mitigation and adaptation investment requirements of sustainable rangeland management. Simultaneously, it will address the financial inclusion barriers by providing access to finance for smallholder farmers and other rural enterprises through a business enterprise from capturing the full benefits of a competitive and inclusive ILCVCF. Through the GCF.

**AMC Obligations:**

The AMC will leverage GCF funding to attract additional investments from commercial banks and non-bank financial institutions, small, medium, and even large enterprises, agricultural cooperatives, and the government.

Under ADISIP, the Government will invest $87.81 million of the GCF grant into the process that will be managed by the AMC. This investment will be used to (i) establish a Green Climate Fund (GCF)-led facility that proceeds to the GIRAIF to finance low-carbon, climate-resilient innovations; The GIRAIF will be established as: a public-private partnership between the Government of Mongolia and the GCF. To meet the financial needs of a LCLVC's stakeholders, the fund will initially operate with (i) two low-carbon, climate-resilient lending windows to address the financing gap for smallholder farmers and agricultural cooperatives; (ii) two low-carbon, climate-resilient lending windows to address the financing gap for medium- and large-scale enterprises in partnership with herders or herder-cooperatives engaged in low-carbon and climate-resilient agribusinesses and urban-rural linkages as well as diversification of the livestock agribusiness value chain; (iii) two low-carbon, climate-resilient lending windows to address the financing gap for banks and financials; (iv) a low-carbon and climate-resilient credit guarantee facility; and (v) a low-carbon climate-resilient investment window. The GIRAIF will be structured as follows: (i) an inclusive and competitive lending window to address the financing gap of smallholder farmers and agricultural cooperatives (SBCs); other nonbank financial institutions will be piloted during Tranche 1 so to potentially launch during Tranche 2; (ii) a low-carbon, climate-resilient credit guarantee facility for medium- and large-scale enterprises in partnership with herders or herder-cooperatives engaged in low-carbon and climate-resilient agribusinesses and urban-rural linkages as well as diversification of the livestock agribusiness value chain will be eligible to borrow.

### On-lending and credit relending mechanisms:

Participating commercial banks will be selected using an eligibility and selection process that includes the following: it must be a duly registered bank in Mongolia under the applicable laws of Mongolia; it must have a proven track record of at least 5 years in agriculture and agribusiness finance; it must have a minimum capital adequacy ratio (CAR) of 10% as per the Central Bank of Mongolia's guidelines and sources; it must be currently implementing or have plans to implement a financing program for green growth initiatives, including climate-resilient agricultural activities; it must not have any outstanding loans with ADB; it satisfies ADB's due diligence requirements for integrity, anti-money laundering, and countering of terrorism, and have put in place measures to implement such controls; it has no past due obligations with the Bank or other financial institutions; it has a sound internal control system including accounting records, procedures, and internal as well as risk management control systems that are satisfactory to ADB; it is willing to enter into an agreement with ADB for the implementation of the system, including appointing qualified staff to manage and implement the system; and it is willing to design experienced professionals to provide technical assistance in the development and implementation of the system.

Private investment fund is one of the two types of funds available under the Mongolian law. The other is public investment fund, which is a mutual fund.

## The GIRAF Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Credit Guarantee Facility

Based on a review of the lessons learned from existing credit guarantee facilities (CGFs) in other countries, the Green Climate Fund credit guarantee facility (CGF) of the GIRAF has been designed to: (i) provide a third-party credit risk mitigation tool to enable financial institutions to extend loans to borrowers who are not able to meet the collateral requirements for microenterprises, SMEs, even large enterprises, in case of default; (ii) serve as a collateral substitute for the missing sub-investment grade rating or other forms of creditworthiness; and (iii) provide an alternative form of collateral such as real estate properties. (iv) facilitate acceptability of collateral substitutes used in innovative approaches to financing climate-resilient projects, including those that are not yet commercially viable.

The CGF will be structured so that it can: (i) enable the adoption of lower lending rates since the guaranteed portion of the subincons which may be categorized as creditworthy borrowers; and (ii) provide a more flexible form of collateral to financial institutions.

### The Basic Features of the CGF

The basic features of the CGF will include the following: (i) the guarantee/insurance premium structure and coverage will depend on the size, purpose, and sub-borrowers of the GIRAF subs; (ii) the ceiling for sub-in-collateral values is set at 70% of the total loan amount; (iii) the maximum term of the CGF contract will be 12 months; (iv) the premium will be paid by the borrower to the financial institution and will be based on the size, purpose, and nature of the sub-borrower; (v) the extent of the guarantee coverage to be adopted will range from 70% to 90% of the subloan value; (vi) the CGF contract will be subject to a maximum term of 12 months; (vii) the premium will be paid by the borrower to the financial institution and will be based on the size, purpose, and nature of the sub-borrower.

### Credit Guarantee Enrollment

Participating financial institutions providing GIRAF green and climate-resilient credit solutions in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in this document are invited to enroll for making a claim under the CGF. The criteria for enrollment will be as follows: (i) Financial Institutions (banks, SBCs, and non-bank financial institutions) applying for enrollment must have a minimum of 5 years experience in providing credit solutions; (ii) eligibility criteria for participating financial institutions under the GIRAF Lending Windows 1 and 2 are: (iii) the borrower is an individual or legal entity with a valid business registration certificate, and has signed a contract with the financial institution. The contract will cover provisions on the structure of the guarantee premium, extent of coverage and sub-borrower eligibility criteria for participation in the GIRAF Lending Windows 1 and 2; (iv) purposes, and definition of default based on applicable payment schedule of guaranteed subs; (v) the financial institution must have a minimum of 5 years experience in providing credit solutions.

### Call Procedure on the Guarantee

In the event of a default, as defined in the contract of guarantee, the financial intermediary will be responsible for initiating the call procedure. The financial intermediary will provide the requirements for evaluation of the risk by AMC-DBM. A valid claim on the guarantee coverage of an account will require that the borrower has been declared in default and the financial institution has notified the intermediary who will warrant the continuing management of the defaulting account to enable, if possible, the recovery of the subloan call payments by the CGF.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 55 OF 126

The GIGF is a carbon and climate-resilient innovation grants facility. Mongolia's agricultural sector is its second largest contributor to the country's GDP with a share of 11%, and the biggest source of employment, providing jobs for over one million people or about 40% of the working population. The livestock subsector is the largest GIGF contributor with a share of almost 85%. However, despite the competitive advantages of the Mongolian pastoral economy, Mongolia's livestock enterprises are still too few successful livestock agribusinesses that operate successfully in the market. What exists in the arid and semi-arid regions of Mongolia are mostly small-scale livestock enterprises owned by individual farmers or middle and large enterprises competing an economically competitive, climate change resilient, and socially inclusive agricultural sector.

The GIGF will address these challenges through a multi-faceted approach that includes: (i) providing access to appropriate technology; a weak value chain integration, inadequate financing, and insufficient innovations. The result is a lack of competitiveness and resilience in Mongolia's livestock enterprises, which has been exacerbated by the need to increase the number of animals leading to further rangeland degradation. The primary objective of the GIGF is to support the development of innovative and climate-resilient livestock enterprises that are competitive and have the potential for pioneering livestock enterprises in Mongolia's arid and semi-arid regions. This will be achieved by providing technical assistance, capacity building, and financial resources to catalyze low-carbon green and inclusive regional agribusiness growth by accelerating the transformation of rangelands into sustainable agricultural landscapes.

The GIGF will support the development of innovative livestock enterprises that are competitive, resilient, and promote rural livelihoods. The GIGF will also contribute to the diversification and integration of Mongolia's livestock value chain in the process alleviating rangeland degradation and promoting climate-resilient agribusinesses.

### Eligible Funding, Structure, and Management of the Facility

The total requested GCF grant for the ASDB-1 project is $40 million. $5 million of which has been estimated for the GIFG of the GIGF. From the total GCF grant, $28 million will be disbursed to the GIGF as a loan and $12 million will be disbursed as grants on an agency basis to the Government of Mongolia (GOM) for the GIFG of the GIGF innovation grants.

The Asset Management Corporation of the Development Bank of Mongolia (AMC-DBM) which is tasked to establish and manage the facility, will also be responsible for managing the GIFG. The AMC-DBM will be the custodian under the GIFG. As such, the facility will also be managed by AMC-DBM in accordance with the grant policies and mechanisms as well as the financial regulatory commission (FRC). The financial management system of the GIFG will adhere to the relevant standards and procedures to maintain transparency and accountability.

The AMC-DBM will appoint a Grant Administrator to ensure the smooth functioning and operation of the GIFG. The Grant Administrator is responsible for: (i) ensuring that all GIGF grant recipients comply with the following: (ii) evaluation of the grant applications in accordance with the GIFG criteria, (iii) recommendations on GIFG grants, (iv) disbursement of GIFG grants to eligible applicants, and (v) monitoring and reporting on the progress of the GIFG. The AMC-DBM is expected to ensure that the GIFG strictly adhere to the grant policies and mechanisms approved by the FRC and the GIGF.

### Grant Mechanics and Operations, Eligible Operations, Eligible Grant Recipients

Eligible grant recipients are the innovative and SMEs whose written policies and ongoing proposed programs demonstrate a firm commitment to agribusiness innovation and climate-resilient management. The GIFG will support enterprises that are engaged in sustainable low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management and which has the potential to become competitive, resilient, and inclusive regional agribusinesses. These enterprises must have established corporate values, strategies and priorities as well as network. They must be registered SMEs in Mongolia's establishment of a national system for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) with no adverse audit findings at the time of grant application. SMEs deemed eligible to borrow from any of the GIGF projects will be required to have an established credit history, a good track record of repayment by the borrower; their account must be in financial good standing, with no defaults and no adverse audit findings. In addition, they must meet the following criteria:

To be eligible as a subproject proposal will have to demonstrate compliance with all of the following six requirements below:

**(a) The proposed intervention and/or subproject should be a prototype:** It should be the first of its kind or near the first-of-its-kind in the targeted amaga (maximum of one project of the same nature during the last five years in the amaga).

**(b) Should have an expected climate change benefit (CCB):** The intervention and/or subproject should produce a net positive impact on adaptation benefits with an assessed climate change benefit (CCB) at investment ratio of at least 10%.

**(c) Should be replicable:** The intervention and/or subproject should be replicable in other amaga, including the agriculture livestock value chain that would not occur without the GCF support to the subproject, demonstrating a pathway for scaling up.

**(d) Should not be restorable:** It should have an approach that clearly illustrates how its experience and lessons learned can be used by others.

**(e) Should demonstrate green social inclusivity:** It should have institutional and design features that show it how it will address the needs of women, youth, indigenous peoples, and other vulnerable segments of society with respect to their historic contributions and the vulnerable segments of society.

**(f) Should have the potential to transform the SME into a larger, more competitive entity:** It should show how the intervention and/or subproject will contribute to the development of the SME in terms of: (i) increased productivity; (ii) generating additional annual revenues of at least 10% and additional green jobs and employment of at least as much.

### Examples of Eligible Subprojects

Subprojects should employ innovative knowledge products and technologies in agriculture, livestock, forestry, fisheries, and other sectors that will increase the resilience and enhance livestock/agriculture value chain at the amaga and seams. These may include but are not limited to: (i) climate change adaptation; (ii) sustainable land management; (iii) improved animal health and nutrition; (iv) feed-based addition to reduce methane emissions; (v) digital systems in livestock marketing and distribution (including to product certification); and (vi) renewable energy or energy efficiency solutions for production and processing.

Some subprojects and/or activities will not be funded by the GCF. The following are examples of types of subproject-related activities or costs which the GCF will not cover: The GCF will not cover costs relating to:

* **Activities that do not contribute to the achievement of the ADP program objectives and/or the ADP program environmental and social safeguards.**
* **Capital expenditures planned in the normal course of business, such as spending on machinery, equipment, stock, software, and other similar expenditures.**

These activities include (agriculture production), (livestock production), (on packaging), (on distribution), (on marketing), (on feed-based addition to reduce methane emissions), (on renewable energy or energy efficiency solutions for production and processing).

The Climate Change Benefit (CCB) indicator is a measure of climate change value for money. It is a monetized estimate of the benefits that will be generated by an intervention and/or subproject, divided by its cost. The CCB is expressed as a percentage.

Additionally it is the determinant whether a proposed activity will produce some "extra gains" in the future relative to a reference scenario. This can take the form of: (i) increased productivity; (ii) generating additional annual revenues of at least 10% and additional green jobs and employment of at least as much; (iii) reducing GHG emissions while producing benefits that are more equally distributed. This can take the form of legally viable payments for ecosystem services, or other forms of compensation to smallholder farmers in the targeted amaga and seams which enabling the latter to share in the benefits to be generated by the subproject.

# Box 6: Main elements of the MRV system (See Annex 15)

**GHG emissions and related GHG emission reductions can be based on a variety of approaches, including the IPCC guidelines and the use of existing monitoring methodologies such as those included in the Plan V PDO (see Appendix 2).**

The Mongolian Government has developed its own methodology for MRV, which is based on the IPCC methodology. Approved CVS Methodology VM005, Version 1.0 dated 22 April 2014, Section 146.

**A significant contribution to the development of this methodology was the establishment of a monitoring methodology that it has been developed for Mongolia and has already been successfully applied to a project in Mongolia.**

Significant opportunities exist that can be used for monitoring purposes. The National Agency for Meteorology and Environmental Monitoring (NAMEM) is the institution responsible for nationwide rangeland monitoring covering 15,169 km² of land area. NAMEM's role includes: i) development of a national standard for the measurement of internationally-accepted core indicators that are standardized nationally; ii) develop a reference database of baseline data on rangeland health (e.g., vegetation cover, soil moisture content, etc.) and "healthy" or "degraded" ecological site descriptions); and iii) build capacity to produce a timely output on rangeland health based on the monitoring methodology.

Comparison of existing rangeland monitoring methodologies used by different Mongolian Institutions (Research Institutes; Universities; Ministry of Environment and Tourism; Ministry of Food, Agriculture and Light Industry; Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources; etc.) was conducted in 2013. The results from this comparison and Cartography led to an agreement on unified set of core indicators that will reduce controversy in assessments of rangeland health. These include: i) vegetation cover (e.g., grass, shrubs, trees); ii) soil moisture content; iii) presence of perennial plants; plant height; and biomass. Measurement methods include line-point intercept, gap intercept, air dry weight, etc.

The unified set of core indicators will be used to develop a reference database for the country's ecological sites and a concept for developing simplified ecological site descriptions that match existing herder concepts. The unified set of core indicators is also being used as part of the national standard for rangeland health monitoring. This standard has been approved by the Government as a nationwide monitoring methodology of rangeland health.

Additionally, extensive information is collected on rangeland use, grazing patterns, stock composition, etc. These data will be used to develop a model that can be calibrated with local data and will be used for monitoring the success of the adaptation elements of ASDIP.

What has been missing so far in the link between the monitoring of rangeland health and the monitoring of rangeland carbon sequestration is the ability to measure soil carbon. The IPCC methodology does not provide a model that plays a role in this regard, but it is important to calibrate the model based on local data. Additionally, it is important to ask how much soil carbon can be measured using existing methodologies and what are the limitations of these methods. It is therefore important to measure soil carbon at a sample of the monitoring points established by NAMEM.

ASDIP in its 4th output, provides considerable attention to the question of proper monitoring of soil carbon sequestration, and will ensure that accurate numbers are collected and reported. This is also important given the objective of obtaining additional funding from non-COP sources for improved rangeland management (See Box 10).

This activity addresses the current impediments to enabling a policy environment that is more conducive to (i) developing climate-resilient, low-carbon, and attractive amag and sour coms; (ii) managing rangelands for sustainable livelihood resilience, high carbon sequestration potential, and biodiversity conservation; (iii) creating and strengthening low-carbon, climate-resilient value chains through more accessible green finance; and (iv) developing the sectoral capacity for low-carbon, climate-resilient and more competitive amag and sour coms. The activity will be implemented in a participatory manner with a demand- based support for the formulation, advocacy, and implementation of the necessary policy reforms among the key stakeholders. To achieve this purpose, it will adopt a highly participatory, consensus-building approach. The activity support knowledge and learning through various means such as webinars, video, print materials, and other forms of communication. It will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Forestry (MOLF) in developing the national action plan and undertake the various tasks deemed critical to putting in place and implementing the necessary capacity development program. It includes the following tasks:

Task No 1: Conduct of relevant policy studies and technical analysis. Consensus building on the range of options for reforming the existing policy environment depends on the availability and acceptability among the various stakeholders (the policy experts first and foremost) of the policy studies and technical analysis that will be conducted as part of this activity. The activity will therefore need to undertake new studies required for the paradigm shift. This, in addition to the necessary policy dialogues, will provide a basis for formulating and implementing the national action plan on climate change adaptation changes. Under this task, a study on the possibility of setting a pasture user fee for herders and people with livestock grazing rights is being conducted. The results from these studies will be used as inputs in developing affordable rate, or through subsidized premiums, by herders who participate in sustainable rangeland management.

Task No 2: Technical support in the formulation, implementation monitoring, and enforcement of new policies. A key immediate focus of this activity, among others that could potentially be identified in the course of program implementation, is to assist MOLF/ASDP in the formulation of the successor to the National Action Plan on Climate Change Adaptation (NAPCC) for the period 2015-2030. The Act amending the existing legislation will require the Mongolian Parliament’s approval, after which an action plan will be developed and implemented. This task will provide technical support in this policy development process in coordination with other donors involved in the livestock sector, such as UNDP, the World Bank, FAO, and the EU.

Task No 3: Coordination and implementation of a national strategic advocacy plan. A national strategic advocacy plan advising on how to bring in non-participants will be developed to broaden and deepen the support from both the public and private sectors for proposed ASDP-related policy reforms. The plan will potentially include policy dialogues, stakeholder engagement activities, and other forms of communication. It will also provide policy dialogues among the various sector stakeholders involved which will not just include the policymakers, herders, and livestock producers but also other key actors such as financial institutions, private sector transactional international donor partners but members of the academic and the media as well.

Task No 4: Development and application of an effective knowledge learning and information exchange system. The activity will develop a national knowledge and learning system that uses knowledge learning and information dissemination tools such as a website, videos, and print materials which can be communicated to all stakeholders in the sector. This will include the development of a national network of sponsored national and local meetings and workshops, as well as educational and other learning programs and events. The model which will be established is an revolving fund to operate beyond the 10-year ASDP implementation period. It will also provide technical support for the establishment of private sector transactional intermediaries with branches all over the country (in both the ASDP targeted and non-targeted amags) was also be pivotal to implementing the national plan. Details of this activity are in Annex 27.

## B.4 Implementation arrangements (max 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

A Project Management Office (PMO) will be established under the supervision of MCUD, MOFALI, MET, Development Bank of Mongolia (DBM), and targeted agrom governments will be established to oversee implementation at the sub-county level. The PMO will be responsible for overall project coordination. An Executive Working Group (EWG) including the MCUD, MOFALI and DBM will be established to provide guidance and monitor the implementation of the project. The MCUD will lead on program implementation. A Project Management office (PMO) will be established under MCUD; the PMO director will be the head of ALAMAgic.

The PMO will be managed by a PMO coordinator and organized with three departments, managed three deputy coordinators. The PMO director will be responsible for overall project coordination, reporting to the PMO director and coordinator, and will coordinate with and be guided by MCUD. The deputy coordinator for finance, procurement and administration will be responsible for financial management of the PMO, reporting to the PMO director and coordinator, and will coordinate with, and be guided by MOFALI. The deputy coordinator for procurement, financing and administration will be responsible for project implementation, reporting to the PMO director and coordinator.

A Sub-county Implementation Unit (SUI) for implementing Output 3 will be established at AMC-DBM under the supervision of MCUD; the SUI director and coordinator. The PMG, PIU, MCUD and DBM/AMC-DBM will directly report to the EWG.

At the aimag level an Airmag Project Office (APO) will be established, and an Inter-Suun Implementation Office (IPO) in each inter-suun targeted by the project. Each will have urban development, rangeland management and agri-territorial management departments managed by a deputy director and coordinator to report to the PMO director and coordinator and to report to the PMG director and coordinator and liaise and coordinate with Aimag (District) Government, MCUD, MOFALI, MET, DBM and other stakeholders.

### ASDIP will be implemented as a Multi-tranche Financing Facility (MFF). The MFF enables ADB to advance resources programmatically by aligning the provision of financing with project readiness and the long-term needs of the country. It also provides flexibility in the use of funds, allowing for greater opportunities for constructive dialogue on physical investments as well as nonphysical (thematic and sector) interventions. In principle, the MFF is a flexible financial instrument that allows the Board of Directors to approve a maximum amount for an MFF, and the conditions under which financing will be provided. On the basis of the Board's approval, and at the client's request, ADB Management converts potential project proposals into a multi-tranche facility (MFF) by issuing a letter of intent for a loan (either than a program or a sector development program loans), grant, guarantee, or ADB-administered credit. The MFF is structured as a series of tranches, each with its own set of preparation and selection criteria outlined in B.4. The tranche structure of the MFF is described in Section B.1.

# Capacity of the AE

The Asian Development Bank (ADB) has been Mongolia’s largest multilateral development partner since 1991. ADB has approved sovereign loans totaling $2.3 billion, non-sovereign loans totaling $156.1 million, grants totaling $408.7 million and technical assistance totaling $100.5 million. The ADB has committed $430 million for 9 projects, and $11.9 million on technical assistance for 20 projects. A resident mission was established in 2001. ADB is well placed to develop and implement the ASDIP project, building on a rich experience in Mongolia.

# Capacity of the Executing Entity and Implementing Entities

The Executing Entities of ASDIP is Government of Mongolia, represented by Ministry of Finance, MCUJD, DBM and AMC-DBM acting on behalf of GIRAF. Financial management assessments were conducted on the executing and implementing agencies in accordance with ADB’s MFI guidelines and where necessary, measures were identified to enhance the capacity of the entity.

The financial management risk rating for MCUJD and MOFAJ is moderate because (i) both agencies have quality control procedures for project implementation by contractors and other external donors; (ii) their staff have adequate training on ADB’s disbursement policies and practices including the monitoring of advance accounts; and (iii) their accounting, audit, and control systems can readily be configured to support the financial management requirements of ASDIP. The risk rating is expected to improve in 2013 as the financial management risk rating is sublating, but this rating is expected to become moderate because there will be a need for additional training on ADB’s disbursement policies and practices for MCUJD and MOFAJ. AMC-DBM has confirmed to the AE that they have initiated training on ADB disbursement and financial management requirements for the ESDF project. The Ministry of Education, Culture, Science and Technology (MECST) in the management and operation of the Eco-district Affordable Housing Fund (EDAF) through the Solid Sector Project are scheduled have been engaged early this year ensuring that the appropriate training and technical assistance is provided to ensure that the MECST has adequate capacity to manage the EDAF. The Ministry of Urbanization and Land Management (MULM) and the AE on all aspects of the EDAF activities including capacity building and training. The financial management assessment undertaken for DBM-AMC-DBM also showed that self-funding, procurement and accounting procedures is relatively low with inadequate retention rates.

The financial management risk rated identified in conjunction with the support of financial management Output Activity 4, Output Activity 3 and the appropriate training and training program outlined in the action plan for each agency as discussed in the FAM. The Executing Entity (EE) and implementing entity (IE) will be provided with comprehensive guidelines and procedures.

## Box 7: The Climate Change Benefit Indicator

The Climate Change Benefit (CCB) indicator is a measure of climate change value for money. It is a monetized estimate of the mean annual climate change benefits resulting from ADZP, linking the annual climate change benefit to the investment cost. The CCB is calculated as the ratio of the annual climate change benefit to the investment cost. The requirement calculation is at least 10% of the investment amount. This requirement means that the climate change benefits alone will be sufficient to cover the cost of the investment, and therefore, the CCB will be greater than or equal to one.

The CCB is calculated as follows:

- **CCB = Annual Climate Change Benefit / Investment Cost**

For investments with high CCBs (greater than 1), the ADB may consider these investments in its portfolio. For investments with low CCBs, the ADB will not include them in its portfolio unless they are part of a larger investment that has a higher CCB.

The monetary value of the climate change benefits is calculated as the sum of three terms:

- **Term 1: Annual Climate Change Benefit**
- **Term 2: Annual Economic Benefits**
- **Term 3: Annual Social Benefits**

The ADB will provide guidance on how to calculate these terms. On the basis of the Board's approval, and at the client's request, ADB Management converts portions of the facility amount into a series of tranches to finance eligible investments.

| Box 7 | The Climate Change Benefit Indicator |
| --- | --- |
| **The Climate Change Benefit (CCB) indicator is a measure of climate change value for money. It is a monetized estimate of the mean annual climate change benefits resulting from ADZP, linking the annual climate change benefit to the investment cost.** |
| **The CCB is calculated as the ratio of the annual climate change benefit to the investment cost. The requirement calculation is at least 10% of the investment amount. This requirement means that the climate change benefits alone will be sufficient to cover the cost of the investment, and therefore, the CCB will be greater than or equal to one.** |
| **The CCB is calculated as follows:** |
| **CCB = Annual Climate Change Benefit / Investment Cost** |
| | |
| | For investments with high CCBs (greater than 1), the ADB may consider these investments in its portfolio. For investments with low CCBs, the ADB will not include them in its portfolio unless they are part of a larger investment that has a higher CCB. |
| **The monetary value of the climate change benefits is calculated as the sum of three terms:** |
| | Term 1: Annual Climate Change Benefit |
| | Term 2: Annual Economic Benefits |
| | Term 3: Annual Social Benefits |
| | The ADB will provide guidance on how to calculate these terms. On the basis of the Board's approval, and at the client's request, ADB Management converts portions of the facility amount into a series of tranches to finance eligible investments. |

## Box 8: Specific Selection Criteria for Output 2

All output 2 ranged investment projects must meet the following criteria:

### a. Reduced emissions from land use and through low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management practices, including livestock grazing systems that reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

- Increased resilience to climate change, including against natural disaster linked to climate change as well as other stressors such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and wildfires, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity due to climate change.
- Improved conservation of ecosystems and ecosystem services against natural disasters

### b. Additional criteria for Output 2 under Category B should be considered only if they can prove to support:

- Herders' resilience and livelihood
- Ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA)
- Direct herd reductions, or indirect through economic diversification; and
- Direct mitigation of GHG emissions.

# Sustainability and replicability

The proposed solutions have been designed in a way that it remains more advantageous for each individual herder to follow the project conditions and receive the associated benefits, than to exit the created system.

For instance, if certification is not required, the herders will be able to choose to enter or exit the project on the benefit's long term, without changing the conditions: certification mechanisms will create market incentives to pursue sustainable rangeland management. Once the project ends and the certification mechanism is no longer in place, each herd will be able to choose to enter or exit the low-carbon and sustainable rangeland management system.

On the other hand, if certification is required for the herder to remain in the project as it is now, at each herd level, market incentives will be created to pursue sustainable rangeland management measures and supporting incentives result in higher income from sustainable herding (restoring grasslands) than from unsustainable herding (further degrading grasslands or restoring degraded grasslands).

By bridging the gap between grant-based financing and market-based financing through its revolving Green Investment Fund, the project will be able to provide a range of financial instruments for both public- and private-sector building components. ASDIP will also ensure that local institutions, local banks, SMEs, SCC, Cooperatives, and other stakeholders are involved in the design and implementation phases of the project.

The project is expected to have long-term benefits beyond the project implementation period. The project outputs related to sustainable rangeland management, agri-business, and urban development, after the project implementation period. The project outputs related to sustainable rangeland management, agri-business, and urban development, after the project implementation period.

B.S Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Mongolia faces significant challenges in financing long-term investments and especially those of which a significant part of the benefits are in the form of GHG mitigation. These challenges justify the GCF contribution to Mongolia's climate change adaptation efforts through its support for the implementation of ASDIP.

To start with the latter, ASDIP requires a large number of coordinated actions and investments by a variety of stakeholders. The success or failure of such an initiative will depend on whether these investments by one party will partly depend on the actions and investments by others. Even if the knowledge and information existed to evaluate the viability of the actions proposed under ASDIP, such set of coordinated actions would be difficult to implement without a coordinating entity.

The national government would usually be the best candidate for this role as it has the overall responsibility for the country's development and capacity to invest, elaborated in the paragraphs below. Of course, it should be noted that the knowledge and information required to evaluate the viability of such actions is not always available at the level of mechanisms to support or reward low-carbon and climate-resilient investments further constrain the ability of the national government to make informed decisions.

Financial challenges, given Mongolia's macroeconomic conditions and the characteristics of the targeted regions and population. Mongolia is classified by the World Bank (June 2019) as a lower-middle-income country with a per capita GDP of US$3584 in 2017. The government of Mongolia is currently not able to step in and support in cases of calamities and faces severe challenges in financing large infrastructure projects, which are essential for the development of the targeted regions.

The share of public investment has been persistent and stood at 4-4% of GDP in 2017, while public debt was 91.4% of GDP, demonstrating

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 75 OF 126

### B

#### A limited capacity to increase expenditures: The COVID-19 crisis further negatively affects the ability of the Mongolia government to implement a program such as ASDIP on its own.

In general, Mongolia's economy is dependent on a limited number of products, and hence open to sharp shocks through developments in commodity prices and/or decisions from specific investors to discontinue or reduce their investments. This limits the capacity of the Mongolian government to cope with external shocks and to finance investments.

The Mongolian herders in western areas are not able to cope with adverse shocks such as natural disasters, droughts, floods, and other climate-related events. The average annual income per capita among its 36% demonstrating the lack of ability of the total population to cope with natural disasters and extreme weather conditions is US$105 (2018), which is less than half of the poverty line ($405 USD) per month, again demonstrating the limited resources available to cope with climate change.

Another factor that limits the ability of the population in western areas to invest in climate change mitigation and adaptation is the limited amount of funds that are available for investment. This reflects the limited scale of economic activities in these areas. The availability of credit from commercial banks for small-scale loans is limited combined with high costs (the commercial bank prime lending rate was 20% in December 2018). In addition, the interest rates on deposits at commercial banks are also very high. Financial markets are even stronger in the western areas; for example, while the three targeted aimsag account for over one-third of the total population and GDP of Mongolia, they only hold less than 3% of total deposits, indicating the limited extent of financial intermediation through the banking sector.

Given these factors outlined above, it is logical that ADB comes in as entity coordinating and financing the various projects. The Mongolian government has been working with ADB for many years to develop market-for-cofinancing by ADB. The macroeconomic factors mentioned above indicate that Mongolia would have encountered significant difficulties meeting standard financial conditions. This is especially the case given the project, while being a relatively small-scale project, is an integrated project and has therefore been earlier tested in isolation and is the first in the developing world for an implementation of such a project at this scale.

Earlier (e.g., SDC's Green Gold Program), see also section B.1 and newly proposed projects on the same topic have been or are expected to be implemented on a grant basis. ASDIP seeks to bridge the gap between these two approaches by combining both elements, with ADB providing co-financing in the form of payments for mitigation results, as discussed in the next section. This means that ASDIP's financing will be provided through a combination of concessional financing based on a combination of loans, equity, and payments for GCF mitigation services.

Given the factors above, a series of discussions were held between the Mongolian government and other sources of finance, and a financial package was put together that includes some concessionality, both in terms of interest rates and repayment periods. The total amount of financing is US$105 million (grants from Asia Investment Facility of the EU, and the Mongolian government) and also includes USD 23 million in co-financing from ADB. This package will allow for a longer-term repayment period with risk taking between GCF financing and co-financing. Most of the GCF contribution goes to Output 3, the other two outputs are funded by the EU and the Mongolian government. The projects that would be benefiting from GCF funding) would not happen in absence of this GRAIG/GCF support, because the investment is too large for the Mongolian government alone to finance. The Mongolian government has verified that with the proposed GCF financing in place, ASDIP will be able to pay to the co-financers the required return on investment. Other GCF support targets activities that do not directly result in revenues.

The full package is limited in its concessionality (46.7% of the overall profit is concessional financing and 21.5% of the total amount in form of grants). Concessional loans are only available for investments through loans, while activities that do not result in income (capacity building, policy development, investment plan preparation) have been funded through grants. The amounts of GCF financing and its concessionality are limited to 20% of the total project cost. These limits are set by the GCF Board and are just sufficient to make the financing plan sustainable under the various sensitivity scenarios considered.

Grant concessionality is passed through completely to the end-users, while concessionality of concessional loans was passed through to the extent possible while compensating financial intermediaries for costs and risks.

B.5 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 560 words, approximately 1 page)

As discussed above, one of the key issues ASDIP addresses is how benefits will continue after the formal support for ASDIP through ADB and other sources of support have ended. This is achieved by removing institutional barriers to market access and ensuring that the cooperative members are able to replicate the project interventions are guaranteed. Key to this is the strengthening of the sector’s institutions, including the establishment of a national-level body (the National Cashmere Development Agency) with the mandate to provide technical assistance and support to cooperatives at the national level to the arm's and scowl levels that enable an environment more conducive to a climate-smart investment in cashmere production. The cooperative members will also benefit from the additional greenhouse gas mitigation resulting from the project as a result-based funding source to pay for the implementation of the policies and incentives through the proposed partnership for Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management (LCCRM) Project. This project is being implemented by the Government of Mongolia (and the governments of other countries that seek to replicate ASDIP) and will provide technical assistance and support to cooperatives in Mongolia and those operating the project assets such as owned by the Government of Mongolia (Outline 1, 2 and 4).

Exit strategies were prepared for the benefits – and thus the good practices – to last beyond the project termination.

(i) At the end of the MFF, the cooperatives will generate profits and will be able to invest in micro-projects for the production of cashmere products. The cooperative members will benefit from a market premium.
(ii) The MFF timeframe allows to put in place a certification system for key products (cashmere wool, meat, camel milk). This will allow the cooperatives to sell their products at a higher price than common cashmere products and thus benefit from a market premium, as it is already the case today with the sustainable cashmere products (paid at a higher price than common cashmere products).
(iii) The MFF timeframe allows for the cooperative members to increase their shareholding in the cooperative, considering the cooperative's share or profits. At the same time, the herders cannot increase again their number of herds or their production capacity as they are already subject to the herd size and production limits. Thus, they do not respect certification requirements over a certain period of time are excluded from the cooperative and therefore lose their shares in the cooperative.
(iv) Certified herders will also keep advantages in terms of preferential access to services such as the cooperative's credit facilities. This is expected to ensure that the cooperatives will offer to their members, for example on selling the export of cash and hay fodder.
(v) Once strengthened, SCGAs will be self-sustainable and able to provide green financial products to certified herders.

There is a risk that: - cooperatives are attracted by less constraining, easier certification models and/or SCGA are not sufficiently strong to ensure that the cooperative members follow the sustainable practices in the PUG system. In order to encourage these institutions to follow the sustainable objectives for which they have been established, the GCF will provide technical assistance and support to the cooperatives. The GCF would check that the sustainable development objectives are properly implemented and will be comprised of recognized external institutions such as universities, donor agencies, NGOs. The ethics

## Insert 7: Sustainability of PUGs beyond the first 5 years

In addition to the measures outlined above, several mechanisms will be at work to finance WFP’s ASDIP implementation:

First, the cooperatives will become self-financing and thus become self-sustainable. As the cooperatives are based on PUGs, a model whereby the cooperatives participate in the financing of their member PUGs will be implemented.

In addition, the cooperatives will also benefit from the financial support provided by WFP through Community Participation in Procurement. In AFREP’s experience in Bayankhongor, once operational cooperation is established and the cooperative has a good track record, it can access credit from banks.

Institutional strengthening with the creation of “sound platforms” better connecting the PUGs with related sound agricultural services such as input supply, extension services, marketing, etc. will be supported by WFP through Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SCCs), which are small-scale financial institutions used by herders supported by WFP in Mongolia.

Finally, the capacity building of cooperatives under Tranche 1 will include support to improve the subscription fee system for PUGs and to ensure that the cooperatives can effectively manage their funds and maintain the PUGs.


## Insert 8: Micro-scale GMM for structures to be maintained by the herders

As outlined in page 18 paragraph 11, the ownership and GMM responsibilities should be decided on a case-by-case basis based on the herders’ preferences and type of project. Several options (ownership / GMM or the sound/bearing capacity of the structure) will be considered for each project. The GMM responsibilities will be defined in advisory services under the project implementation consultancy services will provide advice and templates of lease agreements.

## Ability of PUGs/users to cover GMM costs is a key aspect for the sustainability of these micro-projects

When PUG/herders are responsible for GM, the CPP project sheet will include an operation and maintenance plan, defining how the structure will be maintained over time. The cost of this plan will be included in the overall budget.

The project overall scheme relies on the increase of the value added per animal in the herd, and its fair redistribution among the herders and cooperatives. The subsidies increased revenue is expected to be partly used to cover GMM of these micro-projects.


## More narrowly, ASDIP ensures that the financing plan will be built in ample guarantees that GMM costs are covered by the project’s own resources or by other means

One of the design requirements of ASDIP has been to ensure that the GCF funding for this project would be financially viable and sustainable.

Financial sustainability of the investments supported with GCF funding will be a key design consideration, therefore, the project team will conduct financial analysis of the MF tranches. It is anticipated that the repayment will occur over a 40-year period.
