# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 76 OF 126

## Box 10: Partnership for Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management in Asia

Rangelands are among the largest ecosystems in the world and contribute to the livelihoods of more than 800 million people globally, including pastoralists. The majority of rangelands in Asia, especially in Central and Northeastern Asia, are degraded through overgrazing and other causes.

Restoring rangelands requires the reduction of grazing pressure. In the process of rangeland restoration, depleted soil carbon is sequestered by growing grasses and legumes. During this process CO₂ is sequestered as soil carbon. While the amount of soil organic carbon that can annually be sequestered by increasing plant cover on degraded rangelands in Mongolia (Box 10.2) and Kazakhstan (Box 10.3) is estimated at approximately 5 million tonnes per year, the potential for carbon sequestration in Turkmenistan and Uzbekistan can be conservatively calculated as 54 million tCO₂e per year, with a corresponding reduction of GHG emissions.

The above estimate is preliminary and based on several simplifying assumptions. If anything, the estimate of the potential is conservative. Moreover, measures to restore rangelands will in most cases also reduce GHG emissions from other land uses.

ASDP has prepared more detailed emission reduction calculations for the sequestration of soil carbon from improved rangeland management and will implement accurate GHG emission reductions calculation as part of the UNFCCC reporting process.

GCF funding for carbon sequestered will include a combination of a concessional loan and a grant, but the amount of finance provided by the GCF will be limited compared to the value of the carbon sequestered. At any reasonable pace of restoration, the total cost of the project would exceed $10 million per year (the current cost ASDP (expected as soon as reach $75 M) and would also otherwise be beyond the capacity of GCF to pay.

It is therefore proposed to use ASDP and initial GCF funding as a catalyst to pilot and develop an ASDP-managed Partnership for Low-Carbon and Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management in Mongolia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, and DMCs in Central & West Asia and East Asia (an initial focus on Mongolia) with funding from developed member countries. The proposed partnership would aim to establish and improve low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management and

124 A current price assumption would be $10-15 per tCO₂e, which is below the social cost of carbon.

#### B

enhance soil carbon stocks on previously degraded land in the targeted region. The partnership could be expected to focus on:

- Measurement of soil carbon stocks and validation of the monitoring methodologies used to estimate soil carbon stocks.
- Measurement of the impact of changed rangeland management practices on soil carbon stocks.
- Cost-benefit analysis of alternative land use options.
- Analysis of the impact of various policy instruments and plans on rangeland use practices, herder income levels and soil carbon stocks.

5) Formulation and implementation of projects and policies to improve rangeland management.

6) Monitoring and evaluation of project results and impacts, including lessons learned from pilot projects and projects.

7) Benefit-sharing of results-based payments for carbon sequestered through improved rangeland management practices. These payments would be made directly to herders or their communities and could either be set as either direct distributions to herder households or more likely used to finance policies and incentives.

8) Knowledge sharing, including the development and dissemination of knowledge products and their dissemination, for improved implementation of future projects and policies.

9) Linking the project out to other countries that could benefit from low-carbon and sustainable rangeland management.

ASDP and the catalytic GCF funding would also be used to validate and demonstrate the viability of the approach (Unit 4.5). The results will be shared with the GCF Secretariat (see 6 above). The results will be used to expand the proposed partnership. The proposed partnership would initially pay for (and depending on the scale, potentially fund) a pilot project in the target region. This pilot project would be implemented by ASDP and requested by ASDP in excess of a threshold of $2.2 million USD, and assuming enough appetite, carbon sequestered through this pilot project could be sold to GCF. The proceeds from such sales would provide a source of revenues that could help pay back funding and could additionally function as collateral for future funding.

There is a strong interplay with some of the project activities related to replication. For example, Activity 4.5 focuses on knowledge sharing and policy formulation, implementation, monitoring and enforcement, which are ingredients necessary for successful replication. The proposed partnership would also provide technical assistance and supporting policy change. Concrete reductions of livestock numbers relative to the baseline in non-targeted areas will be a key indicator that the project is having an impact on rangeland degradation as well. ASDP does not intervene in non-target area, and GoM has no resources available to do so, this may not happen easily. However, the proposed partnership could step in to realize the replication potential.

The government has requested an MFF in an amount up to $448 million, or 61.9% of the total investment. This includes concessional OCR loans and grants from ADB, a concessional OCR (COL) loan, and grant(s) to be administered by ADB. duties in accordance with relevant laws and regulations. Administered co-financing is estimated at $175 million provided as loans and grants. The GCF will provide grant financing equivalent to $52 million, and co-financing from the Government. Contribution from the private sector and the beneficiaries are estimated at $31.7 million. The EIB loan will have a 20-year term including a grace period of 5 years and an annual interest rate of 2% per year during the grace period and thereafter, and such other conditions to be determined in accordance with EIB's London interbank offered rate (LIBOR)-based lending facility, and such other terms and conditions as may be agreed upon by the parties. The GCF loan will have a 15-year term including a grace period of 5 years, an annual interest rate determined in accordance with ADB’s London interbank offered rate (LIBOR)-based lending facility, and such other terms and conditions to be determined.

The interest and commitment charges during implementation will be capitalized as part of the GCF loan.

For the FIP, the component of the program, the government will invest about $84.7 million of the GCF loan proceeds into the Green Inclusive Regional Agribusiness Fund (GIRAF) to finance out crops. The DBM through its existing programs and projects will provide co-financing for the program. GIRAF is the apex financial institution for the program funds to be channelled through financial intermediaries. It is expected that the government will contribute $25 million of its own resources, and it is also expected that commercial banks will contribute $25 million. Moreover, about $5 million of the GCF grant will be passed on to the government to provide social protection and other provision grants through the GIRAF to qualified farmers for climate innovation in green, low-carbon agriculture.

For Tranche 1, ADB will finance $303 million or 34.4% of the total investment requirement. About $45.0 million equivalent will be financed from the concessional OCB and $45.0 million from regular OCB. The GCF loan will have a 15-year term including a grace period of 5 years, an annual interest rate determined in accordance with ADB’s London interbank offered rate (LIBOR)-based lending facility, and such other terms and conditions to be determined.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section notes the performance of the project inputs against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1. PROJECT OUTPUTS (20 years, approximately 1 page)

ASIDIP is expected to contribute significantly to GCF’s adaptation and mitigation agenda by addressing:

- Adaptation: by addressing climate change vulnerabilities of aimag and soum centers through improved crop production.
- Adaptation: by adapting the animal husbandry sector to the expected reduction in Mongolian rangelands due to climate change (see sections B.1 and B.2).
- Mitigation: through carbon sequestration resulting from restored rangelands and through avoided emissions of methane and nitrous oxide.
- Mitigation opportunities in the urban sector through improved energy efficiency and the uptake of renewable energy technologies.

To address the reduction of rangelands productivity caused by climate change (see Sections B.1 and B.2 and the Climate Change Assessment), it is necessary to achieve a reduction in the size of herds to adapt to its lower resource availability (water, pasture, and forage) and degradation of the rangelands). The herd population will be more vulnerable to adverse shocks. Powers of reproduction are much higher than for the general population in Mongolia. As a result, herder households, therefore, have a hard time reducing their herd sizes. This is because they need to maintain a certain number of animals even further, increasing the sector’s vulnerability to climate change. If a reduction in animal numbers would be possible, it could lead to an increase in rangeland productivity and the restoration of degraded rangelands and enhanced carbon sequestration, as well as a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.

Therefore, the decrease in animal numbers should be accompanied by an increase in value created per animal. This can only happen with an increase in output per animal, quality of outputs, and price of the outputs from the animal husbandry sector and is in ASIDIP supported through several interventions (see section D.2). The reduction in herd size will lead to a reduction of grazing pressure on rangelands and enhanced carbon sequestration, as well as a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.

Increasing output per animal and improving the quality of the outputs and their prices requires a vibrant market for livestock products and services (e.g., veterinary services, extension services), both of which need to be in cities targeted by ASIDIP. This means that the cities where there is a demand for livestock products and services are also the ones where ASIDIP will have an impact.

One of the important factors that limit the attractiveness of cities is climate change. Therefore, investments to improve energy efficiency and the uptake of renewable energy technologies, as well as using low-carbon technologies, are a necessary part of the ASIDIP project.


The specific impacts from ASIDIP are as follows (see the calculation spreadsheet for the mitigation outcomes):

- **532,100 direct beneficiaries** from adaptation (calculated as the population of the aimags targeted by ASIDIP)17. The population in the aimag will benefit through more resilient cities, improved rangelands, reduced greenhouse gas emissions and increased carbon sequestration.
- **The whole population of Mongolia (over 3 million people)** will benefit from restored rangelands and enhanced carbon sequestration, as well as avoided emissions of methane and nitrous oxide. The admitted data source https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_food_energy_intake ranks Mongolia as 143rd, with a per capita food energy intake of 2249 (average daily minimum requirement 1800).
- **28.8 million hectares** of rangeland ecosystems protected.


17 Source: National Statistics Office (2019) National Statistical Yearbook 2018, Table 3.5, accessed through www.1212.nn.
Population figures are for 2018.

### Box 11: Leakage from livestock sector under ADGP

Leakage emissions are, according to the IPCC, phenomena whereby the reduction in emissions (relative to a baseline) in a jurisdiction/sector associated with a project results in an increase of emissions elsewhere outside the jurisdiction/sector through induced changes in consumption, production, prices, land use and/or trade across the jurisdictions/sectors. The term leakage is also used for emissions that are not captured by the project but are caused by the project (e.g., when credits are added).

In the context of Carbon Dioxide Capture and Storage (CCS), CO2 leakage refers to the escape of injected carbon dioxide (CO2) from the storage location and eventual release to the atmosphere. In the context of other substances, such as hydrogen sulfide (H2S), methane (CH4), or ammonia (NH3), leakage refers to the release of these substances (e.g., through equipment leaks, fugitive emissions, venting, flaring, or accidental spills) and hydrofluoric acid (HF) leakage (e.g., from refrigeration and air-conditioning systems). [WGIII]

The text referring to physical leakage of GHGs has been added for completeness and is not relevant for our purposes.

Potential leakage effects considered by ADGP are: 1) leakage effects within the rangeland targeted by the project; 2) leakage effects in other areas where livestock production occurs, including but not limited to, activities catalyzed by the project (including emissions during construction, emission from the use of roads and emission from the transport of animals); and 3) leakage effects related to the use of water as a natural resource. Enforced RUA will stop conflicts between herders, define more sustainable grazing practices, and set a clear boundary for livestock production in the project area. The RUA is not open to other herders who cannot bring their herds in the project's rangeland. Also, with the RUA, non-rangeland herders are not allowed to graze their animals on the project's land. ADGP will not lead to excess amounts that can be grazed elsewhere (excess animals will be slaughtered, while the rest of the herd will be relocated).

The GHG calculations are very complex. Our estimate equates 4 CO2e/ha/year for the project’s lifetime. The reference approach GWP in Kyoto Protocol (FPT1) also considers a range management component with an estimated reduction of more than 23 tCO2e/ha per year. The RUA rules in the newly approved funding proposal (FPT2) are expected to reduce emissions by about 40% compared to the reference approach. This is equivalent to 9.5 tCO2e/ha/year, which is more than twice our conservative assumption.

The GWP values used for this project are based on the IPCC AR5 (revised in: Climate Change 2013: The Physical Science Basis. Contribution of Working Group I to the Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change [IPCC]. Cambridge University Press, Cambridge, UK and New York, NY, USA, 2014). For comparison purposes: "The net change of anthropogenic emissions by sources of GHGs which occurs outside the project boundary but is caused by the project. This includes changes in land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) activities other than afforestation/reforestation; and the increase or GHG emission by sources or decrease in carbon stock in carbon pools such as soil organic carbon stocks, peatlands, etc., which occurs outside the project boundary but is caused by the project. 2) Potential leakage in the form mentioned is a misunderstanding that does not apply in Mongolia. There are no provisions for any kind of compensation or mitigation of emissions from livestock animals in ASDP targeted rangelands. In this context, there is no rational reason for herdiers outside the project area to receive payments under the ACR/CDM methodologies. The only way to avoid leakage would be to include another rangeland outside the project rangeland, but see Box 12), will not increase herd size because of the ASDP and other programs that are already in place. Herdiers outside the project area who have received payments from ASDP are not receiving benefits from ASDP. Therefore, based on IPCC definition of leakage, this source of leakage is not applicable.

On the contrary, ASDP has increased herd productivity and a change in quality, controlled by the certification system that will incentivize other areas to adopt the same model to access more profitable commercial value chains through livestock production. This will have a positive effect on the rangelands outside the project area and contribute to reducing herd sizes. The Mongolian government will have access to an improved toolset to achieve animal husbandry goals, which will be in line with the country’s commitments to comply with its climate change resilient, sustainable range management practices.

Based on the arguments outlined above (see points 1 and 2), leakage in the form of human/animal livestock has not been quantified. The only way that could be considered is if herdiers outside the project area adopt and outside the project area. If herdiers outside of the project area beyond what is reasonable given historic trends, and if such adoption leads to a net increase in emissions from livestock animals, then it would be possible to quantify leakage.

It should also be noted that there are no provisions for any kind of compensation or mitigation of emissions from livestock animals in ASDP targeted rangelands. In this context, there is no rational reason for herdiers outside the project area to receive payments under the ACR/CDM methodologies. The only way to avoid leakage would be to include another rangeland outside the project rangeland, but see Box 12), will not increase herd size because of the ASDP and other programs that are already in place. Herdiers outside the project area who have received payments from ASDP are not receiving benefits from ASDP. Therefore, based on IPCC definition of leakage, this source of leakage is not applicable.

3) The following leakage emissions outside the animal husbandry / livestock sector have been quantified:

* **The total construction emissions** has been estimated as 149,202 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project or 0.13% of the net emission reduction.
* **Emissions due to the use of roads constructed by ASDP** have been estimated as 2,567,167 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project.
* **Emissions due to the operation of GIRAF investments** have been estimated as 912,306 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project.
* **Emissions due to the operation of Output 2 Investments** have been estimated as 361,502 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project.

This is an exhaustive list of plausible sources of emissions from ASDP. It should be noted that in an earlier stage of the formulation of the FP, the AE received comments about the possibility of "boomerang" emissions that would be triggered by the development of the project area and its infrastructure. The AE has not quantified these emissions.

However, this is extremely unlikely. It should be mentioned that the AE and the consultants involved in the formulation of the FP have no experience with such projects. In fact, the AE and the consultants involved in the formulation of ASDP do not create "boomerangs" and are not aware of any literature suggesting this possibility. The targeted areas have been identified as having a high potential for development and settlement. It is unlikely that the infrastructure created by ASDP do not make the area and source currently very attractive for settlement and investment. The area would be more attractive to people who want to settle there, which would lead to an increase in population growth, which would create more employment opportunities for people already living in the centers than anything else. Finally, it should also be noted that the AE has not quantified any emissions from the potential increase of urban population and growth elsewhere (notably in the capital UB), and hence a displacement of emissions rather than a net increase in emissions.

Only rangelands in the participating aimag/regions for which Pasture User Groups (PUGs) have signed rangeland agreements (RULAs) are part of the project boundary for carbon sequestration activities. The project boundary is defined by the exclusion of leakage under ACR/CDM methodologies, leakage through such mechanisms has been assumed zero.

corresponding rangelands that belong to a PUGs with signed RUAs in the participating areas and states.

Within the areas covered by the ASCP, all rangelands that are covered by a specific RUA belong in their entirety within the given project boundary; all rangelands for which no RUA has been signed belong in their entirety outside of the given project boundary.

The PUG boundaries are defined based on traditional practices as well as geographic constraints. Traditionally, herders have divided their lands into zones and each zone is managed by a group of herders. The formal constitution of a PUG is a formalization of these traditional herder groups, and the RUAs are based on these territorial divisions.

The subunit (administrative subdivision of a Provincia/Almog) is the first territorial administrative entry point for rangeland use management. The area of available rangeland in a subunit is divided into several zones; each zone being managed by a group of herders.

In the sketch 1 below, the subunit is divided into 14 PUG zones. Each PUG zone is clearly delineated and altogether the 14 PUG zones cover all the subunit's rangelands; in other words, all rangelands of the subunit are attributed to a specific PUG (if no RUA has been entered into) or to several PUGs (and one RUA if a RUA is entered into). For further discussion, it is useful to define each zone as a rangeland.

Sketch 1—Land management

Each zone:
- for Pasture User Group (PUG)
- for the signed agreement/management (RUA) if a RUA is entered into
- Both covering the whole zone


| PUG - Rua established | Land used for other uses (e.g. forestry, water etc.) |

Sketch 2—Illustration of possible area for carbon sequestration activities

Within ASCDP, as a first step, a similar map of the PUG territories will be obtained or realized by the technical assistance provided to the participating states at the subnational level. Following this step, the participation of all the herder households of a given zone in the formal PUG establishment and subsequent signing of an RUA with the ASCDP, for all herder households of the given zone, will provide the basis for the carbon sequestration activities to operate.

The rangelands belonging to a sub-group targeted by ASDIP form a mosaic of rangeland zones (zones indicated in sketch 1). Each zone is owned by one or more PUGs and may be used by any number of PUGs. A RUA is signed only if any range land is used by one PUG only (only one PUG has the enforceable use rights over that rangeland), and thus, each PUG will have a separate RUA.

In the case where the PUG does not sign an RUA, it is outside of the project boundary but may still benefit from technical assistance support and animal health activities (see footnote 121 below sketch 2).

(ii) Or is in its entirety outside the project boundary for carbon sequestration activities. In the case where the PUG does not sign a RUA, it is outside of the project boundary.

For all the rangelands of a sub-group to be considered as project boundary for carbon sequestration activities, it is required that the entire sub-group signs an RUA. This will be done on a voluntary basis and on a participatory basis, through a consultation and negotiation process to ensure buy-in by the herder communities. This process will be phased in, depending on the willingness and the existence of each PUG to participate.

# Prevention of usage of RUA rangelands by outside herder groups

As explained above, the project boundary is specifically defined by the signing of the RUA. Therefore, within the project boundary, only the landowners (RUA signatories) have the right to use and manage the lands of PUCs with signed RUAs. This notification sets out the rights of participating herder and clearly prevents the use of the rangeland within the RUA by outside herders. The RUA is a legally binding document, which means that any land user who does not comply with its terms (such as extractive industries). Through the Rangeland Use Agreement, the State remains the owner of the land. The RUAs are signed between the government and the PUCs. The RUAs are also signed by the herders themselves or other signatories on their behalf. Any use of the rangeland outside the terms of the RUA (such as by other (non-signatory) herder’s other uses of the land. The RUAs are legally recognized documents, which means that any land user who does not comply with its terms (such as extractive industries). Through the Rangeland Use Agreement, the State remains the owner of the land.

The templates and existing examples of RUAs wherein these details are outlined are provided in the TRTA Final Report Volume III. Some relevant sections in the RUAs are highlighted below:

In the RuA section “rights and duties of the state”:
- To provide grazing lands to herders who have brought their livestock into contracted pasture area without permission;
- To ensure that the pasture is or shall not be allocated to, and used by, others;

In the RuA section “rights and Duties of the User”:
- Demand stopping trespassing by others into pasture or long stay/grazing without official permission or agreement;

In the RuA section “Prohibitions”:
- Without prior written notification, no Parties shall submit or assign the land under the winter and spring campsites and the pastures allocated under this Agreement or part of it to pledge for income generation as collateral;
- No Parties shall assign, pledge, rent or sell the pasture and other assets and equipment accompanied as a whole or in parts.

In summary, the RuA will serve to protect the PUC members and recognize them as users of the rangelands that should not be used by other herders, or for other uses such as mining.

For more details, please refer to TRTA Final Report Vol. II Annex 1 and its appendices.


## D.2 Paradigm shift potential (less 500 words; approximately 1 page)

ASIDF addresses the low-carbon and climate-resilient management of a major type of global landscape, the rangeland. The different rangeland types together constitute about 70% of the earth’s land surface (excluding Antarctica). Rangelands are characterized by their high biodiversity and productivity. For example, temperate grassland globally contain 300-330 billion kg in organic form and 470–550 billion C in organic form, or about one-third of global terrestrial carbon stocks. The rangeland soils have the potential to sequester as much as 1.1 billion tCO2/year. This provides a significant potential for carbon sequestration and mitigation of climate change. ASIDF is an innovative approach that has been piloted in Mongolia. It builds mechanisms to enable low-carbon, climate-resilient rangeland management and restoration while providing economic benefits to the herders. The project has demonstrated its success within Mongolia and beyond. Therefore, the scope for replication of a successful approach to manage rangelands while achieving greenhouse gas mitigation and adaptation concerns is therefore huge.

[2] CERF (2014). The role of rangelands in climate change mitigation. [Online]. Available: <http://www.cerf.org/...>

[3] LeR, R. (2004). Soil carbon sequestration impacts on global climate change and food security. Science 304, 1623–1627.

## Contribution to local and national levels strategies

The comprehensive scope and cross-cutting issues of climate change in Mongolia require sound institutional arrangements and collaboration within the government at all levels and the various stakeholders to be effectively addressed. The proposed ASIDIP model for low-carbon, climate-resilient agro-territorial development will use a fully integrated approach, vertically among the various levels of government (national, provincial, and local), horizontally across different sectors (agriculture, environment, energy, etc.), and with other public institutions, private sector groups, and civil society organizations. The ASIDIP institutional and implementation framework is designed to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the process. It is critical for Mongolia to ensure that climate change can successfully be mainstreamed into national, subnational, and local policies and plans and that there is broad-based support from civil society and private sector groups for the necessary institutional arrangements and LLVCCs to be directly integrated into government policies, strategies, and plans.

The integration of low-carbon and climate-resilient agro-territorial development models with existing policies, encompassing the National Carbon Management Plan (Partnership and Inclusive Heidi Management Plan), Low-Carbon, Climate-Resilient Agriculture Development Plan, and Low-Carbon, Climate-Resilient Land Use Development Plan, will provide a comprehensive framework for Mongolia to develop a model for a low-carbon and climate-resilient agro-territorial development model. Over the 10-year implementation timeframe of ASIDIP, this model will be promoted within ministries and government agencies responsible for land use planning, agriculture, environment, energy, and other relevant sectors. The existing land-use and agrimage laws (such as MCUG, MOFALI, MET, and NIDA), also the role of key civil society organizations, NGOs, and private sector groups will be reviewed, re-evaluated, and reinforced to improve the livestock producers linkages to processing, distribution, and marketing. The ASIDIP model will provide a framework for the development of the Herd Sustainability Program (HSP), which is under preparation and financed by ADB. aims to firms to align socioeconomic and spatial development plans and improve policies and tools to support green, competitive, and sustainable livestock production in Mongolia. This will be integrated into the national land-use and development model into as approach setting the development framework for ASIDIP replication in Mongolia.

ASIDIP will thus establish streamlined and coherent institutional arrangements between local sustainable climate-resilient agro-territorial models planning and policy frameworks to create the necessary environment for replication and scaling-up.

ASDP will continue to be a significant part of its resources in Output 4 to produce and disseminate mainstream and cross-administrative knowledge. It will adopt a highly participatory, consensus-building approach and support knowledge creation and dissemination activities that reach out to the participant-beneficiaries of Transboundary Landscapes (TBLs) and other stakeholders. The objective is to ensure that the knowledge and lessons learned are formulated into government policies and investment programs to drive reforms in land use and management practices. This will be achieved through a combination of face-to-face and mobile broad-based support for the formulation, advocacy, and implementation of the necessary policy reforms at all levels of governance.

Knowledge sharing will also be supported by the professional skills and capacities developed under ASDP, representing more than 10,000 person-months of national consultants and program management inputs financed by ASDP including professional staff in administration and finance, to create strong in-country expertise that can be scaled up nationally and institutionally and replicate the paradigm shift.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

As developed in section D.5, ASDP is fully aligned with and will contribute to key climate change strategies and policies of the government of Mongolia: the National Action Program on Climate Change (NAPCC), Mongolia's first NDC, the Green Development Policy of Mongolia, the Technology Needs Assessment (TNA), the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the National Adaptation Mechanism (NAMAs) submissions of Mongolia and the National Mongolian Livestock Program (NMLP) and its update.

ASDP will provide inputs for the formulation, implementation, and enforcement of the successor to the NAPCC, which is currently under development. The NAPCC was a key driver in ensuring coherence in the recommendations. A strong monitoring and evaluation component and support to the development of an effective institutional framework are critical elements that will help Mongolia to reduce animal numbers and increase herd productivity, such as effective incentives, cost-effective investments, market creation for the LLVLC, MRV of the emission reductions, commercial impacts, on herding communities, and other key issues. The NAPCC also provided a strong foundation for enhanced institutional capacity for policy formulation, implementation, and enforcement to support the new NAPCC. ASDP will continue to provide inputs for the formulation, implementation, and enforcement of the successor to the NAPCC.

In the context of green city development, the Sustainable Development Vision 2035 of the Government of Mongolia calls for the preparation and implementation of "green development standards for urban areas."

### Knowledge production and dissemination

Knowledge production and dissemination will be based on the activities developed in ASDP particularly in relation to rangeland management. The following are some examples: (i) increased access to water, better carrying capacity, importance of vaccination and access to veterinary services, improvement of breeds and breeding of livestock; (ii) improved land use planning and development of a framework for sustainable land use and land cover measures; meeting and objectives of a cooperative and or a contract; knowledge learning and information dissemination through workshops, seminars, training courses, field visits, etc., including the establishment of a National User/Member, or through ASDP sponsored co-op/national regional and local meetings and workshops, as well as educational materials.

A certification system will be established at the national level in cooperation with other initiatives. It will raise awareness and support long-term sustainable rangeland management practices. The certification system will cover all major land uses (e.g., livestock grazing, crop production, open natural areas), among others, animal stockkeeping. It will provide the conditions that need to be met, at each level, to become certified. The certification system will be developed in cooperation with the GCF and the Land Management Center; to be developed under ASDFP, it will also contribute to monitoring rangeland health, soil condition, water availability, and carbon sequestration in the soil. It will also reinforce strong knowledge infrastructure for scaling up and replication of ASDFP beyond its targeted areas through the country.

Scaling-up and replication potential

Making full use of its tranche implementation structure, long-term implementation timeframe, and financial modality, and considering the significant number of complementary initiatives (such as UNDP), ASDFP is developing a low-carbon and climate-resilient land management model that can be scaled up to the national level. During Tranche 1, ASDFP will develop a low-carbon and climate-resilient local development model, which will be tested in pilot areas. The lessons learned from this pilot will be shared with other initiatives and encouraged to coordinate and learn from the ASDFP for complementarity and compliance on selection criteria. This will allow ASDFP to identify opportunities for scaling up and replication of its low-carbon and climate-resilient model, as well as the scaling-up replicability potential of ASDFP's low-carbon and climate-resilient model. This strategy will allow ASDFP to contribute to the development of a national land management framework that integrates all initiatives into an overall low-carbon and climate-resilient development framework. On one side, it will learn how to adapt its model and integrate compatible pre-conditions; and on the other side, develop a clear process for scaling up and replication in the country. This will be done by identifying replicable climate change features in Tranche 1, to be up-scaled in Tranche 2 and 3, for better replication beyond ASDFP's targeted areas (e.g., livestock grazing, crop production, open natural areas, land management technologies, agro-park for LCLVC, sub-urban per-urban areas upgrading, etc.).

The replication potential of ASDFP will also benefit from its long-term financial incentives. The GRAP will be structured to provide a 10-year grace period and a 40-year term incentive for the successful completion of ASDFP; given the GCF's less-hungry concessional terms (40-year term inclusive of a 10-year grace period). Through various financial intermediaries with branches all over the country (on both the ASDFP target and non-target areas), it will be possible to provide national policy enforcement support to influential local practices and local development and achieve LCLVC.

Finally, to further encourage replication inside and outside Mongolia, livestock and crop sectors in Asia (for more information on this proposed partnership, see Box 10). It is proposed that the approach of ASDFP to achieve GCF's goals through sustainable soil carbon sinks in the rangelands will be initiated through support from the GCF. This will include technical assistance for additional national-level institutional arrangements and cover rangelands within and outside Mongolia. Mongolia's total pastoral land is about 4.7 times the areas targeted in ASDFP, while in northwest and Central West Asia, the replication possibilities are a factor over 25.

### D3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### SDGs

In terms of the SDGs, ASDIP will contribute particularly to: **SDG13 Climate Action**, **SDG6 Clean Water and Sanitation**, **SDG8 Decent Work and Economic Growth**, **SDG9 Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure**, **SDG11 Sustainable Cities and Communities**, and **SDG15 Life on Land**.

### Environmental Co-benefits

There are 452,300 beneficiaries from improved environmental conditions in the targeted areas (all aims covered in annexes 1-3), mainly in the form of enhanced urban infrastructure and improved rangelands.

#### Urban environment

The extensive new and upgraded urban and agrobusiness infrastructure and services (i.e., water supply, heating, wastewater collection, electrical power, solid waste management, drainage and flood control) that the project will provide will significantly improve living conditions for residents in the targeted areas. These environmental conditions, and the standard of living of affected communities. In the Amag city capitals of Uzhgorod, Berehove, and Krasylivka, as well as in the towns of Ternopil, Zhovkva, and Yavoriv, the planned improvements for Tranches 1 & 2 of ASDIP will significantly improve living conditions in the cities while greatly improving the quality of life for residents. The Amag city capitals are located within the catchment areas (CAs) of the Ternopil, Zhovkva, and Yavoriv rivers which currently receive minimal to non-treated wastewater effluents from the WWTPs of the three capital cities. Moreover, the ecologically Strictly Protected Areas of Uzhgorod, Berehove, and Krasylivka are located within the catchment areas (CAs) of the Ternopil, Zhovkva, and Yavoriv rivers which currently receive minimal to non-treated wastewater effluents from reduced water pollution. At a smaller scale, improvements to wastewater collection & expanded treatment capacity in the town centres will also improve the quality of living with residents also realizing a cleaner environment.

The expansion and upgrades to central heating and sewerage will greatly improve local air quality from stricter emissions standards for new buildings and existing buildings on urban territories. These improvements will benefit greatly from reduced ambient PM2.5 and PM10 concentrations. In addition to improved ambient air quality, the project will also provide residents with access to modern sanitation facilities and energy-efficient modern apartments in areas which, amongst improved utilities and energy efficient building designs, will significantly reduce indoor air pollution. The introduction of central heating systems will replace wood burning for ger householding heating and cooking produces upwards of 95% of the ground-level loads of PM2.5 and PM10 in ger households (WHO, 2011). The introduction of central heating and modern stoves with improved ventilation will greatly reduce indoor air pollution levels within these households and benefit families.

The planned improvements to drainage and flood control in the capital cities and town centres will improve access and residency during spring runoff and several rainfall events. Less standing water will also improve individual and community health from reduced exposure to gastrointestinal diseases and waterborne infections. The project will also provide residents with improved sanitation facilities, including new sanitary sewers and stormwater drains, as well as a reduction in the volume of urban surface runvbarb in Uzh which routinely overflows causing extensive flooding in the Bag communities along the river. This will reduce the risk of flood damage, and garbage entrapment at the dumpsites in all Amag city capitals and socium centres will result from reduced runoff volumes and cessation of the extensive windborne garbage that now occurs on the landscape near the dumpsite.

#### Rangeland management

Development of modern agri-business technologies and improved rangeland management will allow previously degraded habitats to recover. The introduction to Amag capital cities and town centres of modern animal husbandry techniques including multi-crop fodder production, livestock quarantine and veterinary services, and international slaughterhouse technology will: (i) produce healthier and stronger


---
**Page 95**

Investments will help better cope with winter conditions, thereby significantly reducing incidence of Drought (i) produce livestock of significantly greater value which will improve herders livelihoods from access to previously inaccessible grazing lands, and (ii) reduce land and surface water pollution resulting from livestock slaughtering, meat processing, and waste disposal.

A major target objective of increasing the quality of meat production is to reduce the size of livestock herds (goats and sheep primarily) which currently are significantly overgrazing the pastureslands. A reduction in herd size will also help improve the health status of animals by reducing stress and disease transmission. Aside, from increased CO2 sequestration and reduced CH4 emissions from reduced livestock discussed elsewhere in this document, a reduction in herd size would also reduce the impact on the environment through loss of ground and avian wildlife that are directly or indirectly dependent on the affected rangeland/forest steppe and arid lands.

The latest monitoring data at the start of the implementation of the animal numbers measures of the project will be used as baseline, and ASDIP targets will be formulated such that after 10 years the number of animals per hectare is reduced to a level where rangeland degradation is no longer an issue. The categories in Table 10 are based on the latest monitoring data at the start of implementation of the monitoring points that are rated as the highest health category, for which no higher class exists.

Use of updated information is important, because the latest sharp increases in number of animals has not been reflected in the baseline data used to formulate ASDIP targets. This means that if the target date of the implementation of measures to restore rangelands are likely to be underestimated if the latest data set is not used as a baseline for the formulation of ASDIP targets. connection to the technical unit and improved housing insulation; (ii) affordable housing options with rental housing and rent-to-own schemes; (iii) improved child-friendly and gender-sensitive public space with high community participation in design preparation and operation; (iv) construction of a multi-purpose youth development center for the opportunity to participate in building social cohesion; and (v) infrastructure adapted to the disabled. The MFT aims to target around 30% of the total households living in informal settlements, which is approximately 17,400 households out of the 58,600 households out of the 17,000 households living in ger areas and currently suffering from the lack of public services and access to basic infrastructure.

Urban interventions in the inter-soum centers also include positive social impacts such as (i) impacts of WASH facilities on soum centers residents; (ii) improved dormitory environment & studying conditions for all children, including those with disabilities; (iii) increased access to education for girls and boys; (iv) the provision of dormitories for header households' children also prevents from family separation during the school year; (v) improved access to health services in the inter-soum centers; (vi) reduced child mortality scarcity in livestock breeding sector (increasing herders' vulnerability to natural disasters and other external shocks); increases the number of unemployed people in the settlement; puts economic pressure on the community; and (vii) increased social cohesion. The MFT will target 5 inter-soum centers, each inter-soum has a population of 500 inhabitants in average and provides services to the surrounding communities (soum center residents and the surrounding rural population using the inter-soum center's services).

Output 2 and 3 are expected to lead to: (ii) reduction in land disputes and conflicts between herders through improved access to grazing lands, water resources, and other natural resources; (iii) revenue from mining operations and other land uses, through the signing of long-term Rangeland Use Agreements between PUGs and soum governments; (iv) increased income for herders through better management practices, improved livestock and ability to cope with extreme events; through the financing of facilities and equipment for PUGs engaged in sustainable rangeland management; (v) improved livelihoods of herders, through support to herder cooperatives and other organizations that provide quality services such as veterinary care (improved animal health), for improved marketing of products at higher prices (vi) creation of new job opportunities (see below). The MFT will target 10 PUGs in the three targeted areas (approximately 25,000 households or around 70,000 herder households), of which today two thirds earn below subsistence level income (NSG) in the three targeted areas. They will also create employment in the inter-soum and arid zones where unemployment is high.

Insert 9: Conflict resolution and bringing social harmony

In the livestock sector, several components of the project will directly contribute to conflict resolution and bringing social harmony among herders. The project will support the creation of PUGs (see above), a situation in a shared resource system where individual users, acting independently according to their own self-interest, behave contrary to the common good of all involved parties. This is known as "the tragedy of the commons" (Hardin 1968; Ostrom, 2009). The social organizations which were operating during the socialist period (Hagopian et al., 2014) collapsed after the transition to a market economy and the privatization of land numbers. The collapse of these organizations led to inadequate pastoral management, causing in turn social tension among herders.

Lack of rangeland management and increase of animal numbers is the major source of conflicts among herders that have increased over the past years and can be as serious as killing as reported in the media. Community meetings were held with herders from 2013 to 2015, where they identified the main causes of conflict (Report 1) shown as a result of PUG creation and work, conflicts among herders have reduced. A research survey was conducted by the Mongolian Academy of Sciences in 2014 on the impact of PUGs on rangeland use were reported in the Green Gold project sites then in control sites (Interim report, June 2014). The results showed that conflict among herders has reduced. This result was confirmed by Green Gold internal monitoring as well.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 95 OF 105

## Rangeland Use Agreements (RUUA) also enable herders to secure their traditional user rights against mining operation or migrant herders.

Within the communities, conflicts also stem from the privatization of community facilities, such as water wells, by the richer households. The project will address this issue at both the household and community level. The project is also putting special emphasis and actions to support the activities of the women and vulnerable groups in relation to the project activities all to, including ethnic minorities (see Social Development Action Plan and Gender Action Plan).

The livestock sector is also characterized by the lack of integration of its supply and value chains, resulting into regular price fluctuations. The project will address this issue through capacity building for herders, supporting these actors and their relations, in particular with the development of real cooperatives and contracts.

ASDP is based on traditional practices and do not promote intensive livestock exploitation. It does not aim to move herders from communal grazing centers but to decrease their vulnerability and improve their livelihood notably through higher animal productivity.

## Economic co-benefits

ASDP will promote economic diversification and job creation through the various investments of the project in construction activities, through the agrigoods and input dealers and related financial institutions for women's groups. The project will also provide technical assistance to herders and limited soft support to MSMEs. It is estimated that Output 3 will support the creation of about 11,400 green jobs through its 5 livelihood components.

The project is classified as Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM) considering its impacts on women's access to education, health care, infrastructure and services, economic and financial resources, and enhancing women's voices and rights, which contribute to gender equality and women's empowerment, as described in section G2 and G3 of the Gender Action Plan.

## Gender-sensitive development

The project is especially significant because the targeted areas are less well developed than the rest of Mongolia.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 96 OF 96

## A size is a key limiting strategy, it is counterproductive to the government objectives of reducing animal numbers, reducing overgrazing, restoring grasslands, and mitigating GHG emissions from animal husbandry.

Another factor that limits the ability of the population in western aimags to cope with natural disasters is the limited amount of funds that are available for investment. This reflects the limited scale of the national capital market and the lack of financial intermediation through the banking sector. The interest rates on loans offered combined with high costs (the commercial bank prime lending rate was 20% in December 2017, "14" higher than the World Bank's average for developing countries) have discouraged investment in agriculture, especially in the western aimags; for example, while the three targeted aimags account for over 8.5% of Mongolia’s population and livestock, they received only 3.9% of loans and 2.75% of deposits, indicating the very limited extent of financial intermediation through the banking sector.

Mongolia is classified by the World Bank (June 2018) as a lower middle income country, indicating a limited ability to finance public investment in infrastructure and social services. The government has been generally not able to step in and support cases of catastrophes due to constrained government finances. For example: the budget deficit has been persistent and stood at -6.4-7% of GDP in 2017, while public debt was 9.1% of GDP in 2018. The country is also highly vulnerable to natural disasters or provide hinders with compensation or incentives for reducing the number of animals.

This factor further limits the capacity of the Mongolian government to respond to natural disasters.

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Complete alignment with national priorities

ASDP is fully aligned with key strategies and policies of the government of Mongolia, including the climate change policy framework. In this section we highlight how ASDP matches the National Action Programme on Climate Change (NAPCC), Mongolia’s first NDC, the Green Development Policy of Mongolia, the National Strategy for Sustainable Livestock Development, and the National Agricultural Action (NAMA) submissions of Mongolia and the National Mongolian Livestock Program and its update[16].

### References

[1] Based on data in the National Statistical Yearbook 2018. Tables 13.1 and 14.6, market capitalization of the companies registered with the Securities Commission.

[16] See also the Climate Change Assessment in annex 15.


---
**Page 99**
---

The NAPCC provides the overarching climate change strategy of Mongolia. The implementation of the NAPCC to 2011 aims to help Mongolia create the capacity to adapt to climate change and establish a foundation for green economic growth and development. The NAPCC will also contribute to reducing environmental degradation, improve water resources in the agricultural sector, adapt to climate change, reduce vulnerabilities and risks, and to mitigate GHG emissions as well as to promote economic effectiveness and efficiency of energy use. In the first phase (2011-2016), national mitigation and adaptation capacity will be strengthened; legal environment, strategic planning, institutional arrangements, and monitoring and evaluation systems will be improved; and participation in climate change activities will be improved. In the second phase (2017-2021), climate adaptation strategies will be mainstreamed into sectoral policies and programs.

Both in the first and second phase, range management and other activities covered under ASDIP play a key role. Activities mentioned under the first phase of the NAPCC include:

* Establish water reservoirs and basin constructions for harvesting of river, precipitation and snow melt water (3.2.4).
* Reduce land degradation and desertification, and increase the carbon sequestration potential of pasture and forest lands (3.1.5).
* Conduct measures to reduce livestock vulnerability and risks (3.2.12).
* Improve the livelihoods of pastoralists in order to improve human livelihoods, reduce poverty and build up new green worksites (3.2.14).

In the second phase, the following NAPCC activities are key for ASDIP:

* Establish water reservoirs and basin constructions for harvesting of river, precipitation and snow melt water in gobi areas (3.2.10).
* Implement enduring policies for livestock management adapted to climate change (3.2.22).
* Set up heat supply systems in aimag centers with highly efficient and environmentally sound power generation technologies (3.2.5).
* Meet energy needs of sooms centres and settlements that are not able to be connected to the central electricity grid by using renewable energy sources (3.2.18).
* Explore solar and geothermal energy and bio gas in the heating of private houses and supplying hot water (3.2.26).

For this purpose:

Some of the policies and measures through which Mongolia intends to contribute to global efforts to mitigate GHG emissions, contingent upon the continuation of international support to complement domestic efforts are:

* Maintain livestock population at appropriate levels according to the pasture carrying capacity.
* Reduce fuel use in individual households through improving stove efficiency (with co-benefit of air pollution reduction).
* Increase renewable electricity capacity from 7.62% in 2014 to 20% by 2020 and to 30% by 2030 as a share of total electricity generation capacity.

Sort of the NDC's additional mitigation actions that GOM is interested to pursue, subject to international support are:

* Agriculture (development of a comprehensive plan for emission reductions in the livestock sub-sector for implementation during 2014-2020).
* Reduce fuel use in individual households through improving stove efficiency (with co-benefit of air pollution reduction).

The NDC also mentions that Improving pasture management has the potential to increase carbon sequestration by up to 3.5 million tCO2/y.

A key adaptation priority mentioned is animal husbandry, with the key aim to maintain ecosystem balance through improving pasture management.


Draft second NDC


---
**Page 100**
---

The series of NCCs of Mongolia is under preparation. Among others includes the objective to reduce livestock sector emissions by 23.4% against the baseline by 2030. ASIPD will strongly contribute to the achievement of this target.

* **Green Development Policy:** The Mongolian Green Development Policy has 6 strategic objectives, of which (4) (SO1, SO2, SO4 and SO6) are strongly linked with ASIPD—
    * **SO1:** Promotes sustainable consumption and production pattern with efficient use of natural resources, reduced greenhouse gas emissions and waste generation", and focuses for example on resource efficiency.
    * **SO2:** Sustain ecosystem's carrying capacity by enhancing environmental protection and restoration activities, promoting the development of renewable energy sources, and focusing on creation of sustainable finance systems for community-based natural resources management, promotion of access to safe water and sewerage systems.
    * **SO3:** Promotes rural livelihoods through diversification and promoting green jobs", and focuses for example on improving green production skills and promoting an incentive mechanism for herders who take the initiative in developing their own sustainable livestock enterprises.
    * **SO4:** Develop and implement a population settlement plan in accordance with climate change, while considering the carrying capacity of the environment. This includes focusing on creating settlements that develop self-sufficient "green" and "smart cities and villages that are compatible with the carrying capacity of the environment and climate change trends, in order to prevent the overpopulation in urban cities.
* **TNA:** The TNA of Mongolia consists of two volumes, one dealing with mitigation and one with adaptation. The implementation route of the Mongolian TNA™ deals with the agricultural sector exclusively and to a very important extent with the herdsman sector. Recommended technologies include selective breeding of livestock, livestock disease management, and sustainable pasture management.
* **NAMA:** Mongolia's NAMA submission highlights the objective to "fjmethe increase in the total number of livestock by 2035 compared to the base year of 2010". Furthermore, the NAMA submission mentions "insulation improvements for existing buildings and implement new energy efficient standards for new buildings", and includes promotion of renewable energy.
* **National Mongolian Livestock Program (NMLP):** The NMLP in 2010 was developed by overgrazing. It indicates significant targets to limit the growth of the number of animals, with the target for 2035 (63.5 million animals) far below the reference level of 94.7 million animals. This is a very important step towards achieving better balance between livestock and animal numbers. A comprehensive analysis has been included in Annex 15, the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy.
* **The NMLP as envisaged in 2010 limits overall grazing pressure to much below the BAU scenario while maintaining or even increasing the number of animals. This is a very important step towards achieving better ecological and economic balance. At the same time, the NMLP seeks to increase the carrying capacity of the Mongolian rangelands and to achieve a better balance between carrying capacity and herd size and composition.


This is not to say that the other objectives are not targeted in ASIPD. For example, Strategic Objective 4 focuses on green jobs and includes activities such as "green production promotion", and runs your household and support through preferred loans or equipment" and "Strategically individual's ability to overcome the adverse effects of climate change by increasing their adaptive capacity".


---
**Page 101**
---

The rapid herd growth in herd size since 2010 (by 2019, 71 million animals) means that by 2021, each of the animal species will have excess animals relative to the 2021 NMLP targets. Achievement of the NMLP therefore requires that each animal species be reduced in number.

The herd composition has changed significantly since 2010 with a shift towards changing the herd composition towards large ruminants, with specific targets for the period after the end of the NMLP still to be determined.

The animal reduction objectives included in ASDIP are therefore in line with the NMLP and the long-term livestock development strategy. The key policy target is that by 2035 Mongolia will have achieved herd size reductions as outlined above, with support from the Mongolian government. ASDIP creates the enabling environment for the Mongolian herders to actively pursue these objectives through increased the competitiveness of the livestock sector by supporting rural agri-business and increasingly by increasing herder incomes from a more limited number of animals.

Mongolian livestock successor program

Discussions are ongoing regarding a successor of the NMLP. A key policy target that likely will be inscribed in this new program is to reduce the number of livestock to 512.7 million heads or equivalent of 74 million sheep and goats by 2035. ASDIP provides important lessons for formulating policies and implementing programs and measures.

Consultations with the NDA

Discussions with the NDA took place on 20 May 2019 in a basis a first draft of the concept note that was shared with the NDA earlier in May 2019. Comments from the NDA were received in the meeting on May 2019 and are reflected in the latest versions of the ASDIP document.

The latest discussion with NDA on 15 October 2019, confirmed the strong support of the NDA on the proposal and also provided important comments that will be taken into account during the development of the livestock management technologies in the western areas.

A meeting was held between ASDIP and the NDA on 7 February 2020 and discussion with the heads of department of the MET was held on 7 February 2020. Under NDA recommendation, it was decided that an additional component will be included into the program to implement ecosystem based water conservancy and sustainable water management solutions.

Preparation and consultations

The ASDIP project has been initiated at the request of GOM A TRTA (transaction technical assistance) for the ASDIP MIF is ongoing and includes extensive stakeholder consultations focused especially in the region covered by the project. The TRTA conducted a series of meetings with key stakeholders including in Ulaanbaatar (Ministry of Construction and Urban Development, Ministry of Environment and Tourism). The TRTA also held a series of workshops with key stakeholders from the three target provinces to discuss the mission and consultations, both at the central level, and with the main stakeholder groups in the three targeted provinces. A representative of the National Herd Association, which is one of the focal group user groups, is a major representative of the key stakeholders and has been systematically consulted in the development of ASDIP and will implement significant parts of ASDIP.

The consultations with stakeholders paid significant attention to women and ethnic minorities. As mentioned (Section 2.1), the project includes specific measures for the inclusion of women and ethnic minorities into the project. The TRTA also conducted an analysis on the situation of ethnic minority issues, focusing in particular on the case of the Kazakh, Tuva and the Kheta. The social assessment carried out as part of the preparation phase of the ASDIP MIF has shown that the activities from the project provided sufficient communication and community engagement activities are put in place. In addition, a specific section was included into the ASDIP MIF document to describe the proposed Communications Strategy. The Tranche 1 of the MIF is classified as category "C" for Indigenous Peoples because the social assessment does not establish the present ethnic minorities as Indigenous Peoples

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 300 words, approximately one page)

#### Adequacy and reasonableness

The level of support requested from the GCF for ASDIP is based on ensuring the financial sustainability of the MFF and Tranche 1 (the first tranche). The project will be financially sustainable if the co-financing concessionally requested in appropriate given the restrictions on both Mongolia's and the targeted population's ability to pay. This level of support is also consistent with the expected increase in output (or output 3) and will benefit investments that without the GCF contribution would not be able to attract financial resources from other sources. The level of support is adequate, as it ensures that the level of support is needed, and as argued below, sufficient to ensure that the targeted investment can attract co-financing from other sources. Further justification comes from the comparison with other projects focusing on nature-based mitigation (again, see below).

### Efficiency and effectiveness

ASDIP has very low investment per tCO2e mitigated. With ratios of 6.54 USD/tCO2e (total investment costs per tCO2e mitigated) and 1.98 USD/tCO2e (GCF investment per tCO2e mitigated), ASDIP outperforms the approved projects in Mongolia. These projects mostly concern carbon forestry management exclusively, but there are 6 recent projects that mostly concern nature-based mitigation. The average ratio of total investment costs per tCO2e mitigated is 10 USD/tCO2e and 3.5 USD/tCO2e for ASDIP; all with a higher concessionality of GCF contributions and lower percentages of co-financing. ASDIP compares favorably to these earlier approved projects.

### Economic Analysis

Incorporating with the relevant guidelines, economic internal rates of return confirmed the economic viability of the MFF and Tranche 1 (the first tranche). The economic costs were estimated using the domestic price numeraire. Among the benefits and co-benefits identified during the project design, the following are expected to be significant:
- Incremental consumption benefits from improved water supply, sanitation, heating, and electricity in the targeted population.
- Annual market rental values of the planned housing units.

Other benefits were not quantified either due to the lack of relevant data at the time of the analysis or the fact that they are difficult to quantify. The project is expected to generate significant co-benefits from global warming damages avoided as a result of lower greenhouse gas emissions stemming from sustainable rangeland management, energy savings from reduced consumption as a result of the social and economic benefits, and other environmental benefits such as improved water quality. New investments in the agribusiness value chain or from business expansions induced by the project's fully implemented activities are expected to generate significant co-benefits.

The economic internal rates of return of 11.4%-16.1%, exceeding the ADB minimum prescribed discount rate of 9%. A sensitivity analysis, undertaken to further test economic viability, determined that Tranche 1 will


---
**Page 103**
---

remain economically robust under several scenarios including: (i) a 10% increase in investment cost; (ii) a 10% decrease in benefits; and (iii) a combination of scenarios (i) and (ii).

## Financial Analysis

The financial analysis was undertaken in accordance with the relevant Asian Development Bank (ADB) guidelines. The financial analysis was conducted at the project level and the entity level. At the project level, financial analyses were prepared for each of the three non-revenue-generating subprojects planned for each of the three aimag centers. The subprojects analyzed included infrastructure improvements to existing facilities, as well as the construction of new separate utilities: one for water and wastewater, and one each for heating and electricity. The revenue-generating subprojects were also analyzed at both levels. In addition, a separate project cash flow was also prepared. Based on the cash flows, the financial internal rate of return (FIRR) for each subproject was calculated. To determine the incremental income to be derived from the proposed tariffs, an analysis of the tariff increase was conducted in conjunction with the analysis. Tariff increase proposals were assumed for each utility concerned in the three aimags. An affordability analysis and a willingness-to-pay process was also undertaken, in addition to an assessment of the target households' willingness to pay.

The resulting base case FIRRs for the subprojects ranged from negative, in the case of all non-revenue generating subprojects at the entity level, to 10% or more. Overall, the proposed tariffs and the FIRRs exceeded the estimated weighted average cost of capital (WACC) which was estimated at 6.93%. The affordability analysis showed that the proposed tariffs were affordable for most households in Bayan Ulgii. The proposed tariffs for electricity ranged from 0.94% of the total monthly household income to 15.82%, and for water and wastewater, they ranged from 0.76% to 3.0%. The financial viability of the subprojects under varying scenarios was highly vulnerable to increases in capital costs, increases in operating costs, decreases in revenues, or a combination of all three. The analysis reveals that the FIRRs are sensitive to changes in revenue assumptions and in revenue collection.

The affordability analysis showed that the proposed waste and wastewater tariffs in the FIRR ranges analyzed from 1.02% of the total monthly household income in Khovd, to 15.82% in Uvs. The proposed tariffs for heating ranged from 3.47% of the total monthly household income in Bayan Olgii, to 9.62% in Bayan Ulgii. The proposed tariffs for electricity ranged from 0.94% of the total monthly household income in Khovd, to 15.82%, and for water and wastewater, they ranged from 0.76% to 3.0%. The affordability analysis showed that the proposed tariffs were affordable within the internationally accepted threshold for affordable utility payments of 5% of total monthly household income.

The financial performance analysis was conducted at the project level and entity level for the three aimags in September 2019. At the entity level, a 3-year historical financial performance analysis was conducted and based on trends, financial projections were prepared. This analysis demonstrated that the operation and maintenance of the revenue-generating subprojects (such as heating, water supply, sanitation, drainage, solid waste, and roads) will be sustained by the revenues of the aimag.

## At the aimag level

The impact of non-revenue generating subprojects including those with relatively limited potential income due to existing price or lease regulations (such as affordable housing and the operation and maintenance of the water supply system) is expected to be covered through increased total income and expenditures, all the operation and maintenance of the non-revenue earning subprojects involving flood protection, drainage, solid waste, and roads will be sustained by the revenues of the aimag.

## Technology choices

Technology choices will be based on the best available technologies, considering climate change, environment, social and economic criteria and the desire for innovation. Technology choices will also


---
**Page 104**
---

be informed of the technical assistance that is provided as part of ASDIP and will be subject to systematic learning and dissemination for the benefit of other projects in Mongolia and in the wider region. This includes use of low-carbon technologies as the main component of the project such as solar panels and appropriate insulation materials and use of climate-proven agricultural practices. The project will also seek funding from the International Development Innovations Grant Facility (IGF) for technological green innovations. Innovations to be considered are those that can improve the productivity, profitability and sustainability of smallholder farmers in the agribusiness value chain at the aimags and soums. These may include the following: (i) gel applications to the soil to reduce methane emissions; (ii) use of biogas digesters for energy generation and reduction of methane emissions (iii) digital systems in livestock marketing and distribution (related to product certification) and (iv) renewable energy or energy efficiency solutions for production and processing.

## Establishment of M&I system

### 6. Enhancement of the capacity for policy formulation, implementation and enforcement

- **Support for the formulation, advocacy, and implementation of the necessary policy reforms**: This includes engaging key stakeholders, including the private sector, civil society, and other relevant groups.

### 7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The government, GCF, and ADB reporting requirements will be harmonized by the consultants, who will jointly review the progress of all project components.

The PMO, APD, IPD and PIU will provide ADB with quarterly progress reports in a format consistent with ADB's requirements and consolidated annual reports including: (i) progress achieved by output as measured through the indicator performance targets; (ii) key implementation issues and solutions; (iii) an updated project schedule; (iv) financial information, including budgeted vs. actual expenditures; and (v) any other relevant information.

The government and ADB will jointly review the project at least twice a year. This includes: (i) the performance of the project against its objectives and indicators; (ii) the effectiveness of the project management team in terms of effectiveness of capacity development and awareness building programs, (iii) compliance with loan conditions and other relevant requirements.

In addition to regular reviews, government and ADB will undertake a comprehensive mid-term review after 2.5 years of project implementation to identify problems and constraints and suggest measures to address them. Specific items for the review will include: (i) assessing need to restructure or reformulate the project; (ii) reviewing the effectiveness of the project management team in terms of capacity development and awareness building programs, (iii) compliance with loan conditions and other relevant requirements.

The PMO, APD, IPD and PIU will submit financial reports in the prescribed format to the responsible government entities on a monthly basis. Financial reports will be audited annually by qualified auditors appointed by ADB. The annual audit report together with the consolidated annual report prepared by the PMO, APD, IPD and PIU will be submitted to ADB by the responsible government entities annually. These reports should be adequately reviewed and comply with international accounting standards.


---
**Page 117**
---

The responsible government entities (and the PMO/PSU) will also provide other reports as may be reasonably requested by ADB, including the project’s environmental reports and resettlement reports.

Within 6 months of physical completion of the project, the responsible government entities will submit a project completion report to ADB, detailing, among others, (i) information on project completion, (ii) use of loan proceeds, and (iii) any other matters that have been accomplished.

The relevant reporting schedule is included in the table below.


| MILESTONES | EXPECTED DATES |
| --- | --- |
| Start of Project Implementation | Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) Effective Date |
| Inception report | Within 8 months from the FAA Effective Date |
| Monitoring report | Every 2 years during project implementation |
| Interim evaluation report | Within 6 months after end of Year 5 |
| Project completion report | Within 18 months after project completion date |
| Final independent evaluation report | Within 18 months after the submission of the project completion report |

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (see p.3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

**Description**

Project effectiveness could suffer due to lack of sufficient human resources for project management and implementation.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project includes project implementation and management support consultant (PIMSC) services that will provide the necessary support and on-the-job training for PMO staff. The project management office (PMO) will be set up directly under a steering committee to facilitate coordination between government agencies and reduce the sensitivity of the PMO and ILUs to several levels of government.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Lack of coordination with government agencies, especially departments of MOF ALI and Land Administration, Construction and Urban Development Agency, and the National Environmental Management Authority.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The PMO will be set up directly under a steering committee and executive working group composed of MOF, MOL, and other relevant government agencies. The PMO will be set up in each area to facilitate coordination between government agencies and reduce the sensitivity of the PMO and ILUs to several levels of government.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Lack of PMO capacity to engage with communities, manage the land readjustment process, and reach a land for TU swapping agreement.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The project includes PIMSC services that will provide the necessary support and on-the-job training for PMO staff. The project will also include a capacity building plan in place to support all stakeholders in this process. A project-specific grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be established at the level of the project to address complaints, concerns, and grievances about the social and environmental performance at the level of the project (see section G.1).
