## Description
The community's willingness to participate is low, leading to delay in infrastructure implementation.

### Mitigation Measures
- The project will confirm the high community demand for the project through multiple focus group discussions and willingness to participate surveys.
- Also, willingness to participate will be assessed three times during the project using a pre-designed design, and will ensure the high willingness of participation of the community in the selected subprojects.

## Selected Risk Factor 6
### Category | Probability | Impact
Technical and operational | Low | High

### Description
Project incentives to support sustainable rangeland management and reduce stocking rates are insufficient. The livestock owners are diverted from their initial objective.

### Mitigation Measures
- The project area developed is wide-ranging and covers all herder household in the project areas.
- The feasibility study has confirmed that the level and variety of the incentives are sufficient to induce the desired change in behavior among the livestock owners.
- It was also confirmed that the likelihood of such diversion very low. In any case, the project will develop a certification system for the livestock owners who have adopted sustainable rangeland management practices with the financial support. The certification system will include individual and collective achievement of targets yearly. The PMSC will prepare technical specifications including financial plan to be included in the National Carbon Agribusiness Development Plans that will both identify targeted activities to be supported by the project. The PMSC will then prepare technical specifications including financial plan for these activities.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
### Category | Probability | Impact
Technical and operational | Medium | Medium

### Description
Economic benefits resulting from the project do not reward fairly and sufficiently the efforts made by all actors of the supply and value chains, affecting the sustainability of the project agro-components (sustainable rangeland management, livestock production, and value chain development).

### Mitigation Measures
- Financial support includes conditions to distribute the added value among the operators, and involvement of cooperatives as shareholder is made mandatory in all productive assets to ensure integration of the herders.
- The PMSC will prepare a financial plan for each productive asset including conditions to reward local support, and each productive asset includes preferential conditions to reward herders following good practices.

## Description

## Selected Risk No 19

| Category | Other | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Low |

### Description

The project has only a temporarily effect on herders' activities and impact on land degradation, and once the project is completed practices continue.

### Mitigation Measures

The project will put in place an organizational structure to structure to herders' activities with cooperatives at different levels. These organizations will be developed and their staff trained by the PMSC, with the objective of providing them with a permanent source of funding. Once these structures and infrastructures and services are implemented, it will allow the livestock sector to access new economic opportunities and reduce the risk of land degradation. The project will also provide technical assistance and financial support. Furthermore, a comprehensive set of incentives has been formulated with a long-term horizon in mind. For example, the benefit from having a strong claim to land through a legally binding RUA and the technical assistance provided by the PMSC will be permanent for all herders who continue to comply with the RUA. Such benefit-mechanisms are permanent. Moreover, it is anticipated that the project will provide a more permanent source of funding for Climate-Resilient Rangeland Management in Asia.

### GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

##### Environmental assessment

As part of the TRTA assessment, an environmental assessment<sup>128</sup> was carried out. The assessment found that ASDP is category B for environment pursuant to ADB's 2009 Safeguard Policy Statement. This Category B project will meet all safeguard requirements with mitigation measures and safeguards in place.

* **The construction phase of the diverse array of infrastructure components requires careful mitigation measures, including environmental impact assessments (EIAs), detailed site-specific plans, and best practice construction contract and mitigation measures. Of particular importance is that the current practice of manual transport of household water needs from local kiosks must receive maximum protection during construction activities to avoid contamination or loss of service.**

* **The infrastructure and agro-business components of the ASDP will not negatively impact protected areas, wetlands, and other environmentally sensitive areas. The project will implement a detailed screening and removal of ecologically sensitive components. Two originally proposed project sites were amended in response to comments from the National Park Service (NPS) and Game Reserve A conditions as they were in a National Park and a Game Reserve.**

* **The status of water availability for irrigation is currently under review, with the irrigated fodder and hay farms and meat processing facilities will be clarified along with the source water availability to ensure sufficient water supply for all project components.**

* **The scope of rehabilitation that will occur to existing heating plants will be determined to clarify the scope of investment that is required to ensure emissions meet required quality standards.**

* **The ASDP will implement a comprehensive environmental monitoring plan, and specify the institutional responsibilities and capacity needs for the environmental management of ASDP.**

##### Social assessment

As part of the TRTA, a social and poverty assessment<sup>129</sup> was carried out. In order to mitigate the social risks of the project and maximize the benefits for socially vulnerable people, including the poor, a Social Development Action Plan (SDAP) was developed. In addition, to strengthen the stakeholders' participation in the project, a Community Participation Plan (CPP) and Stakeholder Communication Strategy (SCS) were prepared and are annexed to the Funding Proposal.

Considering the wide scope of Tranche 1, with various projects involving different beneficiaries, the social analysis was conducted at three levels:

* **The agro areas redevelopment project**
* **The fodder and hay farms development**
* **The regional agro-business development**

At each level, the project was designed in a way to include significant positive social impacts (see section D.3.).

**Page 123**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDS PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 121 OF 121

## Key risks identified by the social and poverty assessment were the following:

- In the targeted project areas (output 1): risk of exclusion of poor households and ethnic minorities from project activities and from community management of community center, risk for secondary occupants to be evicted from their homes, risk that the community center will not be used as a replacement for the school based on meaningful consultations); involuntary resettlement;
- Regarding the town centers (output 2): no negative social impact was identified linked to the project activities;
- Regarding the rural areas (output 3): no negative social impact was identified linked to the project activities and in the RuMP/PUG cooperative approach and thus of exclusion from project benefits;
- Regarding regional ag-business development - output 4: risk for the GIRAF to benefit to private sector enterprises at the expense of local communities and households.

The Social Development Action Plan (SDAP) includes measures to mitigate these risks and optimize benefits to the vulnerable households. In particular, examples are:

* Project information flyers will be available in Mongolyn, Kazakh and Tuva where necessary;
* Targeted outreach for poor households of targeted towns in Ulaanbaatar and soum cooperatives integrating 70% of herder households of complying PUGs (inclusive approach);
* Requirements to be filled by the GIRAF Participating Enterprises (PPE) include a form of cooperation with local communities (e.g. land use agreements or sales & purchase agreements) to ensure integration of the herders into the development model.

### Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) for Asset Management Company of the Development Investment Fund (AMC)

An Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) was developed for the three Financial Intermediaries (FI) of the ASDF which are defined by the Asset Management Company (AMC) of the Development Investment Fund (DIF). The ESMS will be implemented in accordance with the ESMS that will guide implementation of Output 3 of the ASDF. The ESMS is an internal management system that will be implemented by the FIs to ensure they successfully manage the potential environmental and social impacts associated with their investments. It includes a set of procedures for risk identification, screening, categorization, and assessment; document preparation requirements and review procedures; conditions for project approval; training needs and competencies in environmental and social areas; training requirements; and monitoring and reporting.

### Involuntary Resettlement

Tranche 1 is classified as category B for Involuntary Resettlement (IR). The FS engineering designs and land acquisition plans were developed with the aim of minimizing the number of households that will be taken. Thus the IR impacts are as follows: affected entities 2965 households, 3 organizations and approximately 4000 herders in total. The Tranche 1 Involuntary Resettlement Action Plan (IRAP) for Tranche 1, fully, and the land acquisition and resettlement plan (LARP) were prepared for Tranche 1 as part of the TRTA™. These two documents will be finalized during the implementation phase. The LARP will be the guiding document for the IR safeguards for Tranche 1.1; but also for the entire ASDF implementation for land acquisition and resettlement (LAR) in compliance with ABS SPS (2009), and relevant Mongolian legislation.

The PAMFCL/UD along with MOPFL and PIUs along with the aimag governments will be responsible for the overall implementation and compliance with the IR and LARP, IR safeguards compliance and LARP.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 (PAGE 121 OF 122)

## Impacts on Indigenous Peoples

The TRTA conducted an analysis of ethnic minority issues; **focusing in particular** on the case of the KENYA INDIAN COMMUNITY (KIC). The analysis found that the ethnic groups will not be negatively affected by the project. They will all equally benefit from the project provided sufficient communication and community engagement are undertaken.

The TRTA has developed a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) in accordance with the Project Participation Plan and Stakeholder Communications Strategy. The Tranche 1 of the MIF is classified as category “C” for Indigenous Peoples because the social assessment does not establish the present ethnic minority groups as being adversely affected by the project, nor does it identify any specific group or entity that may be directly or indirectly affected the dignity, human rights, livelihood systems, or culture of minority ethnic groups.

The Indigenous Peoples Living Framework (IPLF) was prepared for the facility framework in accordance with the Project Participation Plan and Stakeholder Communications Strategy. The IPLF is a living document to guide the preparation of subprojects which may affect any of the 10 recognized indigenous peoples in Kenya, including the KENYA INDIAN COMMUNITY. Indigenous Peoples Plans (IPP) will need to be prepared based on this IPIFF and submitted to ADB for concurrence before approving financing under subsequent tranches.

## Grievance Redress Mechanism

A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) is designed within the Involuntary Resettlement Framework and aims to provide a more effective alternative to existing systems at various levels, which in turn respond to the procedures set out in the “Law on Settlement of Requests and Complaints from Citizens to Government Organizations and Other Public Bodies” (hereinafter referred to as the “Law”) and the “Guidelines for Handling Grievances”.

A separate Grievance Redress Mechanism was developed as part of the IEE. The mechanism is based on three levels of grievance (field level, PMO, MUCD level).

Last, the project-specific grievance redress mechanism that was established for the IEE has been modified for the project to provide a more effective alternative and facilitate the resolution of any and all AP concerns, complaints, and grievances from the social and environmental performance of the Fingerprint project investments into the GIRAF, as described in the ESM.

The project-specific GRM will receive, evaluate, and facilitate the resolution of APs’ concerns, complaints, and grievances. The project-specific GRM is designed to provide a time-bound and transparent mechanism to voice and resolve social and environmental concerns and grievances that may arise during implementation of the project. All affected communities and affected stakeholders will be informed and made aware of the GRM including procedures as to how to access it and how to make use of its services.

The GRM for ADIFOR will be harmonized with the existing grievance redress mechanism at the Airag. All government organizations follow the procedures set in the “Law on Settlement of Requests and Complaints from Citizens to Government Organizations and Other Public Bodies” for handling the grievances from the citizens. The project-specific GRM will provide how to lodge a grievance, how to make a letter/phone or using means such as emails etc., where and whom to submit, once received how to register, convey to the relevant authorities, and follow up on the resolution of the grievance. The Airag will be responsible in case not satisfied with the grievance redress. The Airag Government Office and all the local government

organizations to GIM following this law. The Amag Program Office (APO) will establish GIM in compliance with the local government organizations GIM and considering the specifics of LAR activities and ASEP institutional arrangements. The APO will be the legal representative of the GIM at all times.

The APs can lodge their grievances directly to the APO as a single entry point at the Governor Office. The APO will follow the GIM procedures for reporting and supervision by the Amag Program Office and also provide the GIM statistics and reports to and supervised by this office.

APs will be able to lodge their grievances directly to the APO if they are not being adequately addressed through the GIM. The details of the GIM procedures for registering grievances, reporting by the project authorities including monitoring and grievances management are provided in the IRR for this program and the IEE, RPs for the subprojects.

### Grievance Management and action plan (max. 150 words, approximately = page)

As part of the TRTA, a gender analysis was conducted and a Gender Action Plan (GAP) prepared[1], and main results have been annexed to the Funding Proposal (Annex B). A wide range of tools has been used in this process including surveys, key informant interviews and FGDs.

Considering the wide scope of Tranche 1, with various projects involving different beneficiaries, the analysis was conducted at three levels:

- The area redevelopment project
- The intersectoral centers
- The regional ag-business development

Each targeted population faced different issues and vulnerabilities:
- In the water sector, women are responsible for water collection by women are time-consuming household chores due to poor water, sanitation and heating systems; safety issues in areas streets, gender-based violence and lack of a women center; lack of social amenities such as playgrounds and kindergartens; and lack of work.
- In the intersector centers, main gender issues are the lack of sanitation facilities particularly impacting on girls and women. The lack of access to water and sanitation is also an issue for women in these areas.
- In the herding sector, wives are responsible for specific activities. Despite their important workload and low levels of education, they have limited access to land and other resources. They face low levels of participation in community activities and low levels of participation in herders’ organizations.

Within the ger area development component, the key gender mainstreaming strategies include (i) positive integration of women into the project through the establishment of women’s groups; (ii) improved public space and safety, and development of social amenities prioritized by women; community consultation and participatory planning to ensure that women’s needs are reflected in design priorities for proposed public space and infrastructure development; (iii) Construction of gender-inclusive multi-purpose community development and female participation in community management; (iv) Priority given to female-headed households in the distribution of land titles and other resources; (v) Establishment of a system to encourage women to register their names on the asset property right certificate. In the intersector centers, Wives are responsible for specific activities despite their important workload and low levels of education. They have limited access to land and other resources. They face low levels of participation in community activities and low levels of participation in herders’ organizations.

**GAP details specific indicators and targets. Key expected results include: at least 80% of female-headed households of targeted ger areas streets with improved water, sanitation and heating systems; community centers equipped with solar-powered lighting and charging stations for mobile phones; public buildings equipped with gender-sensitive WASH facilities; at least 50% of TOSK houses units with female occupants equipped with a private toilet; at least 80% of households in the targeted ger areas with access to at least one signatory household sign the RUSA; respectively, at least 20%-30% female leadership in PUGs/Cooperatives; all women participating in the project’s gender equality training; and “GIRAF” (Participating Enterprise) proposals include gender elements; at least 20% of jobs created at construction stage; and at least 40% of jobs created at operational stage to go women.**

Financial management assessments were conducted on the executing and implementing agencies in accordance with the Financial Management Assessment Manual (FAM), ADB, 2005. The FAM is based on the International Financial Institution’s (IFI) will follow ADB’s financial guidelines and procedures. The financial management risk rating for MCUD and MIFAD was high because they have not yet acquired sufficient experience managing projects funded by ADB and other international donors; (ii) their staff have adequate knowledge of ADB’s procurement policies and procedures including the management of advance accounts, and (iii) their accounting, audit, and financial reporting systems are in place. The FAM also assessed the agencies’ compliance with the appropriate reporting requirements. For DBMAG-GBM which will implement the FIL component of the project, the risk rating is moderate because they have not yet acquired sufficient experience managing projects funded by ADB and other international donors; (ii) their staff have adequate knowledge of ADB’s procurement policies and procedures including the management of advance accounts, and (iii) their accounting, audit, and financial reporting systems are in place. The FAM also assessed the agencies’ compliance with the appropriate capacity development and training program outlined in the action plan for each agency as well as the adequacy of their internal control system.

Compiles and high-value contracts are expected under the project. However, both MCUD and MIFAD will have adequate capacity to facilitate full compliance with ADB’s Procurement Policy (2017., as amended from time to time) and Procurement Guidelines (2017., as amended from time to time). The procurement policy for MCUD, MIFAD and DBMAG-GBM is the same. They are in line with the Procurement Law of Mongolia and the procurement plan in the FAM also complies with ADB’s procurement standards. However, the agencies’ capacity development plans have not yet been finalized as they have been incorporated into the project design and their application will be closely monitored during project implementation. The agencies will receive training on ADB procurement procedures to enhance the procurement capacity of the agencies concerned.

Regarding the financial management and procurement of the investments under Community Participation in Project Implementation (CPI), the amount of each PUG’s share is determined by the number of households to be supported, the amount allocated for each PUG, the process of community participation, and the flow of funds and the guiding principles for CPI. The following are the guidelines for the financial management and procurement of Community Participation in Project Implementation (CPI) to finance micro-projects depend on the number of PUG members, with a maximum of $2,000 per PUG household member. Micro-project investments will be confined to small-scale infrastructure projects such as water supply and earth road repair; animal corning and sharing equipment/facilities; small equipment for riding, dairy processing and production, fruit product drying purpose; sewing equipment, get kindergartens, fruits for vegetable growing/small scale greenhouses.

G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

If the funding proposal is not a confidential entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed only to the extent necessary for the purposes of the GCF. In this case, the accredited entity is obliged to inform the Secretariat of the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

- full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of the nature and extent of such portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy; and
- redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Funding Proposal for the GCF by Mongolia / Asian Development Bank regarding the Amags and Soums Green Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP)

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the programme Amags and Soums Green Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP) of the Asian Development Bank (ADB). Mongolia has previously provided a Letter of No Objection, under a slightly different project title: Amag and Soum Livestock Resilient Regional Development Investment Program (ASRIP).

We have reviewed the comments made by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) of ASDIP; questions were raised regarding the compatibility of the National Mongolian Livestock Programme (NMLP) with the objectives of ASDIP, and the need to provide more numbers.

We have taken note of and reviewed the analysis that Asian Development Bank (ADB) prepared titled "Analysis of the livestock sector and the National Mongolian Livestock Programme". We confirm that the proposed project activities in ASDIP are fully compatible with the NMLP objectives included in ASDIP are fully in line with the NMLP and the long-term objectives of the Mongolian government. The proposed project activities will not lead to an increase or decrease in the number between number of animals and the rangelands carrying capacity, which is, as rightly noted by ADB, one of the main principles of the National Livestock Development Programme.

We have also noted that relative to the previous submission, the adaptation and climate resilience contributions of ASDIP have been reintroduced. We welcome discuss as ASDIP's approach to improving livestock production systems through better animal husbandry practices processing and marketing facilities in amag (and inter) soum centers is important for Mongolia's efforts towards sustainable development.

Please let us know in case you have any further question regarding this important programme which addresses one of the principal Mongolian priorities.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP154

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths

- The unique integrated approach of the proposed project aims to significantly improve rangeland management practices and reduce the vulnerability of the rural economy to climate change, while supporting migration to Mongolia's capital city.
- It will contribute to poverty reduction, the country’s basic services and green economic diversification by increasing the capacity of grassland as a carbon sink.

### Points of caution

- The two accredited entities (the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the Asian Development Bank (ADB)) have not yet completed FP141 and FP154 respectively, so will need to work together on this project.
- There are a number of significant barriers to the implementation of rangeland conservation in Mongolia, such as the lack of a viable model integrating rangeland management with other sectors for economic growth; the lack of incentives and capacity-building for local communities to adopt sustainable rangeland management practices; the lack of an enabling regulatory and market environment; and limited access to water resources for livestock and urban services for low-carbon and climate-resilient development.

### Funding

- GCF funding (USD 175 million) will leverage private and public resources (USD 560 million, corresponding to a total project cost of USD 735 million).
- Inclusive Regional Agricultural Fund in structure and implementation, the project has the potential to deliver more impacts beyond the project completion date.

### Rangelands

- Rangelands in Mongolia are considerably overgrazed, with the latest estimate indicating that approximately 80% of rangeland is above carrying capacity.
- While the target locations have a high potential for productivity improvement, reducing animal numbers by 25%, as anticipated by the proposed project, could contribute to better pasture management and improved fodder production, will bring the number of animals approaching sustainable levels in the long term and allow a gradual restoration of the rangelands and an increase in biomass (above- and below-ground).


---
**Page 136**
---

There is clear synergy and complementarity with FP41 in addressing the pressing issue of complementary adaptation and mitigation. As the UNDP proposal is fully in line with the ADB project's approach to climate change, it will create the comparable prerequisites for the ADB project to implement its integrated activities, extend the scope of the project, enhance synergies, and maximize synergies and the climate impact of the two projects. The proposed project would therefore be implemented in parallel and in a synergistic manner, complementing and benefiting from these complementary climate adaptation and mitigation impacts

Although Mongolia is traditionally considered an agricultural country, there are still few viable market-oriented approaches, capitalizing on the increasing number of livestock products. The existing processing industries suffer from insufficient and irregular supplies of raw materials, which is due to the increasing number of animals. The project will therefore aim at improving the productivity in the agribusiness sector by supporting investment in the agricultural sector and the "missing middle" of small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in agribusiness while ensuring a linkage with market-oriented approaches.

The proposed project is accompanied by a clear and dedicated scaling-up strategy to ensure that the project's impact continues beyond the project intervention

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVII, titled "List of conditions and recommendations".

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. The impacts of a drastically warming climate are already being felt in Mongolia, where the average temperature has risen by 2.1°C over the past 70 years. Average precipitation is declining, while extreme weather events have become more frequent and severe. This has led to pastureland and the country’s livestock production that are already fragile before decades of environmental degradation. The combination of these factors with increasing pressure from climate change keeps herders trapped in a vicious cycle: with less pasture to graze their animals, they lose their stock and income. In turn, many herders compensate for those losses by increasing their herd size, which further depletes the already fragile pastures. A recent study showed that 70 per cent of Mongolia’s natural rangelands, which make up more than 80 per cent of its total land area, are in a degraded state.

6. Restoring the pasturland, which has considerable potential for soil carbon sequestration, requires the grazing pressure on the rangelands to be reduced by improving grass management and increasing the productivity of livestock through breeding with a higher value added per animal in order to create incentives for herders. This requires better


---
**Page 137**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Output 1: Climate-resilient, low-carbon and attractive sites and urban centres developed (total cost: USD 441.76 million; GCF cost: USD 8.40 million in loans or per cent)*

This output will focus on one of the three integrated components of the project and its objective to develop climate-resilient, low-carbon, and attractive sites and urban centres that offer better living conditions and support the development of a green and inclusive agribusiness value chain.

The project will undertake an integrated approach to tackle the general infrastructure and services required for the development of these sites and urban centres. Based on consultations with the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry, Water Management and Environment (MAWME), local communities and the community, the project has identified the following interventions required to support urban upgrading and climate resilience.

* The figures in this section exclude the financial charges.


---
**Page 138**
---

### Delivering a comprehensive extension of urban infrastructure, including increasing water supply capacity, upgrading wastewater treatment plant capacity, district heating network extension, urban road extension, drainage extension and treatment of solid waste.

- Rehabilitating roads with networks of green areas and delivering affordable housing units, public landscaping and social amenities;
- Putting in place the infrastructure and services for agriculture parks to enable the initial development of a smart land management and climate-responsive digital centre; and
- Installing solar panels to supply energy to residential buildings, facilities and public amenities constructed under this project.

In addition, this output will contribute to smart land management and climate-responsive digital centre, which will serve as an essential tool to improve land and urban management and monitor of rangeland health, soil erosion and soil carbon dioxide (CO2) sequestration.

For output 1.2GZ proceeds will be earmarked for the uptake of renewable energy technologies and will also leverage co-financing from financial institutions and the European Union (EU) that will cover the majority of the total cost. ADB will finance heating systems, solar panels, water supply and sanitation systems, irrigation systems, communication, agriculture park infrastructure, community facilities, business incubators and additional housing units. The EU grant and EU loan will be dedicated to street redevelopment in green areas and water and sanitation systems.

The ADB grant will finance the smart land management and climate-responsive digital centre.

Output 1.2GZ is expected to generate a net present value of $30,548 per hectare for sustainable herding (total cost: USD 116,667 per hectare; cost share: USD 0.61 per hectare or $5 per acre).

The overall purpose of this output is to increase the resilience of rangelands and provide herders with new tools and means to achieve higher productivity per annum through the following prioritized actions:

- Strengthening herders' management of rangeland use, thereby slowing down and alleviating desertification. A demonstration of inclusive operational rangeland management will be necessary for several years.
- Providing collective herding amenities, such as equipment to build fences for hay-protection areas, storage shelters for hay and fodder, wind-blocking shelters for animals during the winter months, water wells with drinking water facilities for animals, and washing baths for animals;
- Developing irrigation schemes to produce priority fodder crops, including constructing irrigated wells and providing a water supply system for the livestock vaccination programme; and
- Developing a reliable animal health control and traceability system by strengthening veterinary services and the provision of laboratories and disease-free zones.

Many of the above interventions are conditional on participation in sustainable rangeland management. The ADB will provide technical assistance to help implement the approved agreements on pastureland use. These complementary and integrated actions are expected to incentivize herders to reduce the number of animals while maintaining their income, thereby improving the sustainability of livestock production through a better action system.


---
**Page 139**
---

### Output 2 will absorb the majority of GCF grants owing to the lack of capacity of the local population to cope with climate shocks and the high level of poverty among the population in the target areas. ADB will finance civil works for disease-free establishments and primary work on irrigation systems, which will be implemented by the Government of Mongolia. The project will mainly be used for operation and maintenance costs. Beneficiaries will also participate in strengthening herders' management in land-use through in-kind contributions.

Output 2: Capacity building and policy development for green territorial development improved (total cost: USD 157.3 million; GCF cost: USD 109.7 million in loans and USD 47.6 million in grants)

### Output 3 aims to deliver tailored and affordable financing mechanisms to support agribusiness and will be implemented through GRAB. The Government of Mongolia will invest GCF loans into the project, which will be managed by the Asset Management Company (AMC-DEM) under the innovation grant facility. GRAB will promote the use of innovative green finance, including by providing technical assistance for the development of a green credit risk mitigation framework.

### Lending window 1, which provides loans through selected commercial banks to small and medium-sized enterprises in Mongolia that are involved with agribusinesses and herders engaged in sustainable range management, urban-rural linkages and reintegration of the livestock agribusiness value chain.
- Lending window 2, which provides loans through a blended public and private resources for medium-size to large enterprises and herders involved in sustainable range management, urban-rural linkages and reintegration and economic diversification of the livestock agribusiness value chain.

### A green credit guarantee facility that provides green credit risk mitigation for financial institutions. The project will also support the development of green substitutes or alternatives as substitutes to be developed as part of the project's green agribusiness value chain financing scheme, etc.
- An innovation grant facility that provides business incentives designed to commercialize innovations leading to a greener, more inclusive and competitive livestock agribusiness value chain.

### In addition to the above two lending windows, there will be an additional lending window to address the financing needs of micro enterprises and which will channel loans through selected commercial banks. The project will also support the establishment of institutions that will pilot during tranche 1 and further expanded in the subsequent tranches.
- Through GRAB, GCF concessional financing will attract additional investments from commercial banks and other financial institutions to provide green credit risk mitigation for Mongolia's "missing middle". As GRAB is structured as a revolving fund, including the innovation grant facility, it can be scaled up beyond project completion and be expanded to other areas where preconditions are met.

### Output 4: Capacity building and policy development for green territorial development improved (total cost: USD 157.3 million; GCF cost: USD 109.7 million in loans and USD 47.6 million in grants)

28. This output addresses the policy, regulatory, technical capacity and awareness constraints that form the barrier to implementation of the proposed project on the one hand, and its replication elsewhere. It will be implemented through a number of activities: strengthening territorial planning and regulatory framework; raising community awareness, participation and empowerment; providing vocational training and education; strengthening the implementation

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

22. The impact potential of this project is assessed to be high. The project is expected to create significant climate benefits for Mongolia, both in terms of mitigation and adaptation impact.

23. With regard to mitigation impact, the proposed project will contribute to 112.4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e). The project interventions will lead to a reduction in GHG emissions from livestock production by approximately 90% over the lifetime of 40 years, amounting to an estimated 94.6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e), as well as a reduction in GHG emissions from construction and transportation by approximately 7%. The project uses a more conservative methodology (shorter lifetime and more conservative assumptions) for calculating the mitigation potential compared with the reporting practice of Mongolia under the UNFCCC for estimating GHG emission abatement potential compared to those proposed in the 5th Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC). The project also uses a more conservative methodology for calculating the reduction in GHG emissions from construction and transportation, amounting to an estimated GHG emission reduction of 1.1 MCO2e per year over the lifetime. In total, the project is expected to reduce GHG emissions by approximately 95.7 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e).

24. The project has also taken a conservative approach in calculating leakage emissions from construction and transportation, amounting to 3.98 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e). This is based on the assumption that all livestock will benefit from the overall emission reductions, resulting in the project mitigation impact of 112.4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e).

25. Regarding adaptation impact, the project will directly benefit 369,200 inhabitants in the targeted aimag. The animal husbandry sector will become more resilient to the expected reduction in water availability and increased temperatures due to climate change. Livestock will be restored gradually after adoption of sustainable herding practices, while the reduction in the number of livestock will be compensated by a higher value per annual livestock output. The aimag and soum centres will also benefit from the improved climate-resilient infrastructure delivered under the project.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

26. The project has a significant potential for scaling up and replication, and for knowledge creation and learning among the various aimags and soums within Mongolia and with rural communities in other countries. The multi-tranche replicability of the project is represented in its implementation structure. The multi-tranche approach will allow for continuous learning from each tranche to inform a robust policy framework and lessons learned from earlier tranches to be deployed in a timely manner to subsequent tranches, as well as for the necessary adjustments to be made to accommodate the


---
**Page 141**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

In addition to its significant climate impact potential, the project has the potential to generate significant co-benefits, particularly in addressing key environmental, social and economic concerns.

The project impacts on the environment are expected to be high, mainly through the vast area of restored rangelands. The expansion and upgrades to central urban heating will greatly improve air quality by reducing emissions of particulate matter (PM10) and carbon dioxide (CO2). The second major component of the project, which involves the development of modern agro-technologies and integrated land management, will reduce pollution and allow for the restoration of degraded habitats.

The introduction to arid capital cities and some centres of modern animal husbandry techniques including: (i) improved livestock breeds; (ii) use of feed additives and vaccines; (iii) international slaughterhouse technology will: (i) produce healthier and stronger livestock that is more resistant to disease, thus reducing the need for antibiotics; (ii) produce livestock of significantly greater value, which will improve herder livelihoods as a result. The project will also reduce water pollution by improving animal waste management practices.

In terms of social and economic impact, the project will help to balance the country's territorial development, promote economic diversification and increase the attractiveness of remote provinces to reverse migration to Ulaanbaatar.


3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium to high

The needs of the target population are assessed to be high. The proposal targets a sector reliant on livestock production for its livelihood that has been heavily impacted by climate change impacts. Poverty among the herder population is higher than national average and is not able to absorb losses resulting from climate change. However, the overall rate of poverty in Mongolia is low (10%) compared with other countries in Central Asia falling into the category of least developed countries/small island developing States.


3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The project is consistent with key strategies and policies of the Government of Mongolia, including the National Action Programme on Climate Change, the Green Development Policy of Mongolia, and the National Strategy for Sustainable Land Management. It is also aligned with the priorities set out in Mongolia's nationally determined contribution to contribute 10% of its total GGRF emissions by 2030 under support from ADB.

ADB has long-standing experience working with the Government of Mongolia, as well as on grassland management, urban development and dealing with climate change-induced migration. The project will be implemented through a joint venture between ADB and executing entities (EEs), both have experience in managing projects funded by international donors. The Development Bank of Mongolia (DBM) and ADB, which would be managing GGRF, have already been engaged in similar fund operation and management through the aso


---
**Page 142**
---

### Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: High

The total cost per GtCO2eq reduced over the project lifetime is US$8-654, and the total cost for GCF funding is US$103 million. This has been estimated following a conservative approach, which implies that the real cost may be lower and suggests a relatively high financial efficiency of the interventions. However, it should be noted that the funding proposal is yet to provide detailed information on the project’s cash flow assumptions and risks so as to avoid potential double counting in the future.

The economic analysis shows that the project is cost-effective, resulting from annual market value increases of 10-25% for food and incremental consumption benefits from improved water supply, sanitation, heating and electricity in the targeted aimag and sum centers. Using a discounted cash flow (DCF) methodology with a 3% discount rate and a standard economic evaluation methodology, the model resulted in an economic rate of return of 114–161 per cent, exceeding the GCF’s threshold for financial viability. The project is also cost-effective as it results in a 10 per cent increase in investment cost, a 10 per cent decrease in benefits, and a combination of both.

The financial analysis was conducted at the project and entity levels. At the project level, the financial analysis focused on revenue-generating subprojects planned for each of the three aimags covered by the project. The analysis also included an affordability analysis which projected that all households will be able to sustain their operations, including debt servicing of existing and new loans.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project is targeting the GCF mitigation results areas, with a total estimated cost of around US$253 million. It has a lifespan of 80 years and an annual mitigation impact of about 112.4 MtCO2eq with the adaptive management scenario and 169.2 MtCO2eq under the robust beneficiaries, respectively. The mitigation impact will be achieved through the buildings, cities, forestry and land use, energy, water and sanitation, and waste subsectors. The project aims to increase the resilience of vulnerable people, communities and regions, and ecosystems and ecosystem services in Mongolia by improving access to clean drinking water and sanitation; improving urban services in priority provinces of Mongolia. The project will support the Government of Mongolia’s efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, restored rangelands, as well as through avoided methane emissions, improved energy efficiency and the uptake of renewable energy. With regard to adaptation, the Government will address climate change impacts on water resources, agriculture, livestock production, infrastructure and adaptation of the animal husbandry sector to the expected reduction in


---
**Page 143**
---

### ESS: Environmental and social (ES&S) risk category

The accredited entity (AE) has classified the project as category B for both on environment and on involuntary resettlement, and category C for indigenous peoples' people's concerns.

#### 61. Assessment of ES&S risks and impacts

The AE has conducted an assessment of environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the proposed project to identify the related impacts. The assessment has also identified mitigation measures through the various environmental management plans. Assessment of the ES&S impacts is in accordance with the requirements set out in the AEF Safeguard Policy Statement, including the provisions of the AEF Safeguard Policy Statement, given that the AE is the RIS. The AE also prepared a detailed ES&S Management Plan (ESM) to ensure compliance with the safeguard guidance, training and direction to the financial intermediaries (FIs) and ensure that all identified risks are mitigated.

#### 62. ESS: Labour and working conditions

There will be significant investments in construction work, particularly in the employment of skilled and unskilled labour. While there is no particular risk associated with the employment of workers under minimum wage legislations (related to minimum wage, safe working conditions, social security contributions and international conventions), it should be noted that during the construction phase of the project, labourers involved in the project will be closely supervised and monitored by the AE and its contractors. The AE has also prepared a detailed Labour Management Plan. Training will be conducted for construction workers on gender equity in labour relations including labour exploitation and the management plans provide for grievances to be addressed and corrective measures taken.

#### 63. ESS: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

Although the project may cause pollution, this is not expected to have any significant impact on the project's mitigation and monitoring plan. The project also provides for consideration of a more efficient utilization of natural resources, including water and energy, as a result of project implementation.

#### 64. ESS: Community health, safety and security

According to the project, during the construction phase, demolition of buildings will not produce toxic waste. Risk reduction measures regarding the protection of workers (e.g., HIV/AIDS) have been considered, with a recommendation to include information, education and communication on


---
**Page 144**
---

### HIV/AIDS as part of the communication strategy for the project. It is also recommended that an emergency preparedness and response plan be provided in the environmental management plan that outlines how the affected communities will be assisted in case of any emergencies.

#### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.
Required land acquisition or resettlements are not planned. Transchile has already acquired all necessary land for the project, as detailed in the resettlement plan provided by the AE. Business or livelihood activities and income will also be maintained on the lands that have been acquired. The design of the project is based on sports fields, community centres and other amenities will be built on land voluntarily ceded by the residents through a voluntary land-swapping mechanism. A resettlement framework is also prepared to guide the implementation of involuntary resettlements in case they are required for the future.

#### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources.
The project site area is located in regions that are characterised by rich faunal biodiversity but is far from protected areas and national parks noting that some boundaries are already defined as buffer zones around the project site. The project site is not located within an AE. Moreover, the projects sites of Tranche 1 in the three western Amapas are reported to be not located within any AE. However, the project area is located on land where biodiversity hotspots ecosystems and mitigation measures have been provided in the environmental and social management plan. The project will also provide a buffer zone around the project site. Risks related to biodiversity such as the introduction of invasive alien species as well as strategies to manage these risks are included in the ESM.

#### ESS 7: Indigenous peoples.
The AE has provided an indigenous peoples planning framework (IPPF) to guide implementation in the project area where a number of ethnic minorities live. The IPPF is based on the findings from the socio-economic and anthropological study, the finding is that the ethnic groups will not be negatively affected by the project. The AE classifies Tranche 1 as an indigenous peoples focal point (IPFP) project. The IPPF has been developed suggesting there will be no negative impact to ethnic minority groups. Nevertheless, the IPPF provided accounts for circumstances, including in-lieu transfers that may trigger the need for the development of a new IPPF. In line with the IP Policy, the AE should ensure that prior informed consent and establishment of a Grievance Redress Mechanism. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP), the AE should ensure that an indigenous peoples focal point will also be available for affected local communities. In line with the IPP, the AE should ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the principles of free, prior and meaningful consultation has been undertaken. In line with the IP Policy, the AE should ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the principles of free, prior and informed consent and meaningful consultation has been undertaken.

#### ESS 8: Cultural heritage.
None of targeted facilities of the project are located in cultural heritage areas. The project area does not contain any tangible or intangible cultural, artistic or religious value, or contain features considered as critical cultural heritage. It should also be noted that there is no evidence to suggest that the project will have an impact on the cultural heritage that may become apparent during the implementation phase of the project.

#### Institutional arrangements and capacity-building.
The project clearly defines the roles and responsibilities for each institution involved in the project, including the AE. Capacity building in various areas such as on effective management of solid waste collection and disposal, water supply and sanitation, energy efficiency and renewable energy for energy-efficient housing improvements, among others are entrenched in the project design. Technical assistance and training for safeguards staff in implementing the EAP as well as for various officers and contractors in implementing environmental and social management

# Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

### 4.2 Gender policy

- **Legal framework**: Mongolia's commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment through its Constitution, various laws and regulations, including the Gender Equality Law, the Family Law and the Law on Combating Domestic Violence.
- **Violence against women**: The assessment indicates that violence against women is a serious problem in Mongolia. Women are more likely than men to be victims of domestic violence, and this is often exacerbated by their economic dependence on male partners.
- **Education**: Despite favourable policies and laws, gender differences and gaps exist in Mongolia, some of which are visible in the discrimination against women in the labour market, labour force participation rates, and access to education. Women generally earn less than men and women pursue higher educational attainments by women. The gender assessment indicates that many girls drop out of school before completing their secondary education.
- **Employment**: Women's participation in business is mostly in the informal sector, with much lower levels remuneration than among men. The gender assessment also indicates that women experience greater difficulties in accessing credit and other financial services due to their public location as a result of tolerant attitudes towards violence and the lack of institutional assistance for victims.
- **Land rights**: Women's access to land is limited by cultural norms, which often prevent them from owning or inheriting property. The gender assessment indicates that women are less likely than men to own or inherit property, and that women have fewer legal rights over their property compared to men. Women also face greater difficulties in accessing credit for agricultural purposes.

# Risks

## Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

### Accredited bodies/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (medium risk)
- ADB is the AfE for this project. It has been the largest multilateral development partner of Mongolia since 1991.
- The AfE has a stock of over USD 2.5 billion of accumulated support provided by ADB, including USD 30 million in grants and USD 478 million in loans.
- The project will involve multiple EEIs, namely the Government of Mongolia (through the Ministry of Finance and MCUID), DBM, AMC-DBM and GRAF.


---
**Page 147**
---

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

#### Timely availability of co-financing: proposed project will be implemented over a period of 10 years. Initially, tranche I will be fully developed with top-up of corresponding co-financing from GCF and other sources. The AE expects that the co-financing for tranches II and III will be available in phases as the project progresses.

#### Some of the co-financing for those tranche will be top up during implementation of tranche 1. It is necessary to ensure that sufficient funds are available at all times so that the project can deliver on the estimated impact.

#### Guarantee cover: part of the GCF resources will be used to provide guarantee cover to the medium-term borrowers. GRIF is expected to provide guarantee covering 70-80 per cent of the loan value to banks. High guarantee cover would limit the ability of the AE to work with local financial institutions and encourage them to lend. The AE assesses this level of guarantee to be in line with other similar guarantees.

#### The AE is requested to work closely with the Government to ensure that the GCF can provide a sovereign loan to the Government of Mongolia (Moody’s B3 credit rating). The credit profile of Mongolia remains weak, however, the high cover provided by the GCF and long-term repayment profile is expected to support the Government in making the repayment.

#### Concessionality of GCF financing: documents which will describe in more detail the selection criteria for concessional loans. The AE has identified a 10-year concessional window under GRIF. The funding proposal states that the loans products will be the blending of the GCF resources with other sources of finance, and that the terms of the loan will contractually require the EEs to ensure and monitor that the GCF concessionality is passed down to the final borrowers.

#### Economic rate of return analysis and project viability: the GCF economic analysis results in an economic internal rate of return of 11.4-16.1 per cent under the base case scenario. The results of the financial analysis indicate that the project will be financially viable for all subprojects except for electric subprojects by 20 per cent for electric subprojects. The financial analysis includes tariffs based on the surveys on willingness to pay. The AE clarified that all proposed tariffs in the analysis are indicative and subject to change as the tariff approval process is a lengthy one, and level of tariff is approved in a timely manner by the authorities concerned. The AE explained that this could be achieved through an agreement between the AE and the Government (which would be negotiated by the AE with the Government) that would require the Government to issue such tariff approvals.


---
**Page 148**
---

## GCF (R.28/02) Addendum B Rev.01

### Page 159

In its proposal and in the responses to comments made by Compliance team in the project preparation review process, the AE indicated that this conducted due diligence is in accordance with the applicable guidelines and policies with respect to the governance aspects of the project. The AE also provided a summary of the findings from the due diligence carried out as part of the process. The AE indicated that its integrity due diligence carried out on AMC-DBM and two representative participating financial institutions showed that there are no significant integrity risks. From the review, it was noted that AMC-DBM has a strong governance structure with key entities in accordance with the relevant ADB guidelines. The financial management risk rating for MDB and MOPF is medium. The AE also indicated that AMC-DBM has a strong team of staff with experience in managing projects funded by ADB and other international donors; (ii) their staff have adequate training and experience to manage the project, including the implementation, management of advance accounts; and (iii) their accounting, audit and control systems can readily be adapted to meet the requirements of the GCF. The AE also indicated that AMC-DBM has a strong system for financial reporting in accordance with ADB’s internal reporting requirements. For AMC-DBM, which will implement the financial intermediation loan component of the project, the integrity risk rating is low as the level of due diligence being carried out is expected to become moderate as their staff acquire sufficient training and experience under an ongoing ADB-funded project. The financial management risks identified will be mitigated with the support of AMC-DBM’s internal control systems and through the implementation of the capacity building and training programme outlined in the action plan for each agency as discussed in the facility administration section above. An overall summary of the integrity risk assessment, including roles and responsibilities has been developed and will be attached to the funding proposal.

Based on the initial compliance risk assessment conducted by the AE and the assurances that the project team provided with respect to the implementation of appropriate integrity controls, GCF Compliance team agrees with the assessment and that the attendant risks will be adequately mitigated. Accordingly, the compliance risk assessment for this project is rated as medium.

### 4.5.3 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material in terms of cumulative approved amount as per the risk appetite statement. With approval of this proposed project, the projects in Mongolia will be the highest recipient of GCF resources in terms of cumulative approved amount.

### Conclusion

It is recommended that Board consider the above factors in its decision.

# 4.4 Fiduciary

75. The AE for the AIF is this project. The GCF loan and grant will be administered by ADB and will be passed on to the Government of Mongolia through the Ministry of Finance as the borrower. ADB will oversee project administration, monitor project implementation and ensure proper compliance with the terms and conditions of the loan.

76. The EEs for the project are the Government of Mongolia, represented by the Ministry of Finance, MUSD, DIM and AMC-DIM.

77. The implementing entities are MCUD, MOEFL and AMC-DM.

78. Financial management assessments were conducted on the EEs and implementing entities in accordance with the relevant ADB guidelines. The financial management risk rating for MCUD is moderate because it has a relatively small budget compared to other agencies but is expected to become lower as its staff will receive sufficient training and gain experience in financial management. The risk ratings for MOEFL and AMC-DM are low, as all have initiated training on ADB disbursement and financial management using ADB handbooks and manuals.

79. The financial management risks identified will be mitigated with the support of financial management consultants and the appropriate capacity development and training programme outlined in the project’s implementation plan. The EEs and implementing entities will follow the financial guidelines and procedures of the AE.

80. The procurement risk is moderate for MCUD, MOEFL and AMC-DIM, as they are all governed by the same procurement manual. The procurement risk rating for AMC-DM facility administration manual complies with ADB procurement standards. The project will need the necessary approvals from the relevant authorities of the agencies concerned. In addition, the Government of Mongolia and ADB will jointly review the project at least twice a year.

81. Financial statements will be audited annually by qualified auditors approved by ADB and the Government; the audit report, together with comments on any action being taken, will be submitted to the GCF for approval in accordance with its annual reporting requirements. These reports will be adequately reviewed and will comply with international accounting standards.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

### 4.5.1 Logical framework

m. The programme’s Theory of Change (ToC) clearly articulates the climate problems which it wishes to address, as well as how they are to remedy. As a cross-cutting project, the ultimate project goals of supporting the transition from current rangeland management practices into low-carbon and climate-resilient land use systems, and achieving significant GHG mitigation in the long-term, are clearly articulated. The ToC explicitly states the causal logic which underpins these goals. The logical framework is consistent with the GCF’s Theory of Change, as it reflects the project’s planned interventions and stated outcomes in its ‘If then’ formulation.

---

### Implementation timetable

#### 4.3.2.

| Outcome: Climate-informed use and management of land and water resources |
|---|
| Objective: Invest in enhanced climate change adaptation planning at the national level → Activity: Invest in enhanced technical capacity for long-term climate change adaptation planning, including through the development of a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) |

#### 4.3.2.

Finally, as currently stated in the submitted funding proposal from January 2021, the TDC does not contain any links to it; therefore it is not possible to determine the causal links and logic between the programme’s activities and its results. As a consequence, this programme will be of limited use for evaluation purposes.

#### 4.3.2.

The logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including reporting on contributions towards the achievement of the respective impact, outcome and output indicators for the relevant programme results areas and the project targets as per the GCF results matrix. The logic is based on the assumption that the total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries will be used to derive the core indicator for adaptation (total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries), the funding proposal does not provide any information about how this number can be derived.

#### 4.3.2.

The indicators for the targeted mitigation results areas of M10 Reduced emissions through energy efficiency improvements in buildings, cities, industries and appliances and M10 Reduced emissions from land use, reforestation, afforestation, conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks are properly selected with appropriate baselines, targets and assumptions.

#### 4.3.2.

The indicators for the targeted adaptation results areas of A10 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions' and A10 Improved food security and nutrition in rural and urban areas are appropriately selected; however, for 'A10 Increased resilience of health and well-being, and improved disaster security', there is no clear indication of the definition of 'food security'.

#### 4.3.2.

As per the GCF results matrix, the indicators mentioned represent the means by which the relevant outcomes (M6a, M7b and M8f for mitigation and A5d and A7d for adaptation) will be measured as well as verifiable in respect to their targets. At this stage, however, it is not clear how property rights and tenure security will be addressed through the appropriate financial incentives and institutional mechanisms which are such a key feature of ASDF.

#### 4.3.2.

As per the GCF results matrix, the indicators mentioned represent the means by which the relevant outcomes (M6a, M7b and M8f for mitigation and A5d and A7d for adaptation) will be measured as well as verifiable in respect to their targets. At this stage, however, it is not clear how property rights and tenure security will be addressed through the appropriate financial incentives and institutional mechanisms which are such a key feature of ASDF.

#### 4.3.2.

Finally, the details around the establishment of a strong national framework for surveillance and enforcement in order to enhance capacity to follow national targets set out in the NAP and other relevant frameworks, as well as the means by which this framework will monitor and measure this result would further enhance and strengthen the logical framework.

## Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

The arrangements for monitoring and evaluation (M&E) and reporting are adequate. A detailed M&E budget, including planning and evaluation plans have been included.

- Quarterly progress reports will be submitted to the Secretariat.
- Semi-annual and annual reports will be submitted to the Board.
- The synergy report will aim at maximizing the reverse flow of knowledge and adaptation impact by allowing for reasonable flexibility in the timing and sizing of activities and investments.

## Legal assessment

The accreditation matter agreement was signed with the AE on 17 August 2017 and it became effective on 6 September 2017.

- The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals within 180 days of the Board approval.
- It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board: (i) the AE obtains all its internal approvals; and (ii) GCF receives a certificate or legal opinion from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat confirming that all such internal approvals have been obtained and that the AE has the authority and capacity to implement the proposed project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Mongolia, a country in which GCF privileges and immunities are not yet in force. The agreement on privileges and immunities was signed by the Government of Mongolia (GOM) and is subject to ratification and/or approval by the Parliament of Mongolia.

- The Mongolian Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board: (i) GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country; or the agreement on privileges and immunities has been ratified/approved by the Parliament.

## List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

- Delivery by the AE to GCF of a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals within 180 days of the Board approval.
- Signature of the funded activity agreement in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, with the date of signature being no later than the date on which the AE has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals.

Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat:
