Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP154

Proposal name: Mongolia: Altays and Soums Green Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP)

Accredited entity: Asian Development Bank (ADB)

Country(ies): Mongolia

Project/programme size: Large


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

#### 1.1 Rangelands are important ecosystems in many parts of the world. They cover about 70 per cent of earth's land surface (excluding Antarctica). In terms of climate change, improved management practices can lead to a large increase in carbon sequestration and storage, resulting greater carbon sequestration. It has been estimated that this global carbon mitigation potential of rangelands is approximately 15 Gt CO2 equivalent per year.

#### 2. The proposed project, Mongolii Altays and Soums Centres Green and Resilient Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP), will be implemented in Mongolia, where rangelands cover about 80% of the country's land area. Rangelands are a major component of Mongolia’s economy in terms of employment, and rangelands are important ecosystems necessary to the sound perennial productivity of livestock herds. However, the quality and quantity of rangelands in Mongolia are known to be degrading and, as presented in the funding proposal, this degradation has been accelerated by climate change. The average annual temperature over the past 30 years is about 27.2 per cent above the carrying capacity of the country's rangelands. Apart from overgrazing, climate change impact has also been indicated as contributing to rangeland degradation in Mongolia.

#### 3. The funding proposal argues that the current trend of increasing average annual air temperature in recent years has also meant increased evaporation, which is outpacing precipitation and leading to a reduction in soil moisture content. Climate projections have also shown these changes will intensify in the first half of the twenty-first century. This will lead to further degradation of rangelands, requiring targeted interventions if the important animal husbandry sector of Mongolia’s economy is not to be irreversibly negatively impacted.

#### 4. The funding proposal argues that the proposed project will help to bring the animal population on the targeted rangelands to a sustainable level (below rangeland carrying capacity) and thereby reduce the pressure on these lands, which would mitigate the negative impacts of climate change on rangelands. Mongolia is very vulnerable to natural disasters, with the animal husbandry sector being most severely affected by droughts and floods (episodes of extreme weather). The funding proposal argues that the proposed project will help to address this vulnerability. As argued in the funding proposal, Mongolian herders in the areas (provinces) targeted in this proposal are currently not able to cope with these natural disasters. If they were to be relocated (which is what will be covered in subsequent tranches in the event of successful project replication) - are not able to cope with these natural disasters. In the absence of any incentives, the herders


---
**Page 154**
---

strategy coping with natural disasters has been to increase herd sizes.* This strategy is counterproductive to a cardinal goal of this project, which is to reduce the population of animals grazing on the targeted rangelands in order to reduce degradation. Therefore, the proposed project cannot be considered successful if it is found that they do not result in reduced livestock populations when faced with droupl epidemics.

It is an internationally accepted scientific fact that having a population of herds grazing an ecosystem at or above carrying capacity for that area is very apparent for overgrazing, which will in turn (not checked) contribute to the degradation of a country's rangelands. The National Mongolian Livestock Program (NMLP) was designed to mitigate the reversal of the degradation of the targeted rangelands if and only if the intervention can deliver a reduction in livestock population. This is the rationale behind the NMLP, which was based on the basis of a national intervention introduced by the Government of Mongolia in 2010. This intervention, the National Mongolian Livestock Program (NMLP), recognized the importance of reducing herd sizes to reduce degradation and improve rangeland conditions. The main goal of the NMLP included, among other things, the setting of a significant target to limit the growth in the number of livestock herds with an increase in herd size not exceeding 10% above the average level of 2008 (43.3 million animals). The program also covered the goal of achieving a better balance between demand and supply for animal products, which was set at 50% by 2020. ASHPD was submitted to GCF for funding; the NMLP was already a failure. The first phase of the NMLP (2010-2013) had not achieved its goal of reducing herd sizes and increasing livestock reduction target, the plan had increased all types of animal population, resulting in increased rangeland degradation. In 2014, the number of herds with an increase in herd size was already increasing by 64 per cent. Evidence also gathered for the next phase of the NMLP indicated that, by 2033, further escalation of land degradation and water demand throughout rangelands in Mongolia will continue to be a major challenge for the country's livestock sector.

The review of all documents made available to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) during the period 2014-2015 did not provide satisfactory evidence to herders to discourage them from increasing their numbers of animals as a response to droupl epidemics. It was also concluded that the NMLP did not cover the need for livestock diversification and alternative livelihoods for herders in Mongolia. This supply would promote sustainable animal populations as well as good livelihoods for herders, who will be able to adapt better to climate change. The review of all documents indicated that climate change has reduced the productivity of rangelands, affected glacier-fed water regimes, and increased the frequency and intensity of droupl epidemics, which in turn have severely impacted natural disasters. Climate change issues have severely impacted herders, livestock productivity, and the rural economy. Herders are increasingly dependent on government subsidies to meet their functions delivered to the rural economy. Previous efforts at solving the problem of degradation of Mongolian rangelands (the NMLP and various other bilateral cooperation actions) have failed. Herders' livelihoods in Mongolia are heavily dependent on livestock production, which has increased by 25.6% compared to 1990); leading to overgrazing averages of 2.6 per cent above the average level of 2008 (43.3 million animals). The increase in herd sizes is a major factor that severely degrades the rangelands, which aggravates the vulnerability of herders, who then further


---
**Page 155**
---

increase size of their herds as a safety net.* As a result, 70 per cent of pastoral land has been degraded* This means that both the above- and below-ground biomass has been considerably reduced, and the soil carbon is significantly below its capacity. Reversing the degradation and restoring the soil carbon to its pre-degradation state has a large mitigation potential, estimated at over 440 MtCO2eq countrywide over a 20-year period.*


According to the information available in the funding proposal, the proposed ASDP intervention will be implemented through three tranches. It will use a comprehensive, interlinked and participatory approach, where urban and rural transformations are mutually reinforcing. The project will focus on improving living conditions for pastoralists and livestock will be managed sustainably, and well-equipped among and soons (subunit of an animal) will be provided with improved feed. This will lead to the establishment of agroecosystems that will then constitute more diversified low-carbon climate-resilient livestock value chains (LCLVCs) that will promote nontraditional, resilient and high carbon sequestration management in Mongolia’s pastoral areas. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 15.2 and will promote climate finance and private-sector investment mechanisms designed for sustainability and replicability. It is expected that the ASDP will be implemented over a period of five years with the first tranche being launched by mid-2018, followed by the second and third tranches in 2019 and 2020 respectively.


Revere aimed and transformed areas into low-carbon, climate-resilient urban settlements with improved living conditions, and adequate infrastructures and services to: (i) anchor LCLVC investments at the point of need; and (ii) provide adequate services to herders and livestock farmers.


Introduce transformational low-carbon and climate-resilient ranchland management practices, with incentives, organizational structure, services, and transitional support for herders.


Provide innovative climate mitigation finance attractive for LCLVC investments to sustain herders’ low-carbon practices; and


Build capacity of the government to formulate and implement transformational low-carbon, climate-resilient development plans and policy, and the knowledge and the means of verification and reporting (VFR) system necessary for replication.


The following paragraphs provide a brief description of each tranche in the implementation plan of the ASDP for each an aim.


The implementation of ASDP will be carried out in three tranches:


Tranche 1, which is the focus of the present submission. ASDP will implement its fully integrated approach in Bayan-Ulgii and Uvs Aimags, and in the north of Khovd Aimag. The project will also support the Development Programmes that are or will be active in the south of Khovd Aimag, which were launched during project design between the Asian Development Bank (ADB) and UNDP to complement each other and avoid overlaps.


* According to Mongolia’s Third National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, the *pasture area* which urgently requires reversing and enhancing is already exceeding 40% and for the recovery of degraded areas, it is estimated that 75% of the pasture area will be required. This is a large range of present pasture use practices.* Moreover, The Third National Communication states that: “74.8% of Mongolia’s territory has been identified as being under pressure from climate change, and this figure increases to 90% according to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Adaptation Agenda for Livestock in June 2016 given above to estimate the 60 per cent of the pastoralism of Mongolia is already at risk.”

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF (R.28/03)/MARR/FD Rev. 01

### Page 5/6

Although not yet confirmed, it is likely that tranche 2 activities will be implemented in the south of Khor and Dornod Aimag(s), where UNDP is also active on a few related and non-related interventions through a GCF-financed project. Tranche 2 work activities will focus on the implementation of the following outputs: (i) integrated, low-carbon and climate-resilient territorial approach of tranche 1.

Tranche 3 is then expected to extend the success stories of tranches 1 and 2 to other climatically vulnerable areas in Mongolia such as Khovd and Zavkhan Aimag(s).

As a means of organizing the herders in each aiming into functional groups, the pasture user group (PUG) system will be adopted in tranche activities. The PUG system was established by the Mongolian government with support from the World Bank and the Green Gold Project based on traditional Mongolian structures for herding.

Each PUG will have a five-year plan for its low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management (LCR RM) activities, including stocking adjustment plans and herd management plans, indicating the PUG's annual objectives for reducing the size of its herd, targets for changes in livestock composition, and measures to be taken.

The BiLAs will be one component of a larger participatory, inclusive herd management plan. This plan will identify and prioritize actions that will reduce emissions; improve livelihoods and welfare; and the quality of the herds, and the livelihoods of the PUG members. For this initial stage of the low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management activities, the BiLAs will be developed in a participatory manner and will take the form of matching grants to incentivize the commitment on and implementation of the BiLAs.

The matching grants will be co-financed by the PUGs through cash or in-kind contributions. They will be used to implement the community-based microprojects identified as priorities (e.g., construction of water wells, livestock shelters, and hay fields) with a strong adaptation and, to a lesser degree, mitigation focus. The size of these grants is expected to range from 10% to 25%, depending on what is appropriate to incentivize the participation of the herders and to compensate the initial effort required.

To achieve the objective of ASSHP, the intervention has been developed around the following four major outputs:

- **Output 1:** This includes the following activities in tranche 1, which will also be replicated in tranches 2 and 3:
    - Activity 1: Low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services upgrade of armag centres;
    - Activity 2: Low-carbon and climate-resilient infrastructure and services upgrade of inter-summer centres;
    - Activity 3: Implementation of smart land management system;

- **Output 2:** This includes the following activities in tranche 1, which will also be replicated in tranches 2 and 3:
    - Activity 1: Implementation of community-based, low-carbon and climate-resilient investments;
    - Activity 2: Support to low-carbon and climate-resilient PUGs and cooperatives;

### Activity 3 - Implementation of water-efficient irrigation systems for fodder production and animal health

#### Output 3: This is to be implemented through the financial intermediation loan component of the project. The loan will finance activities that include the following in tranche 1, and the same activities will be replicated in tranches 2 and 3:

- Activity 1 - Provision of accessible, low-carbon and climate-resilient agricultural inputs for fodder production to herders cooperatives and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs);
- Activity 2 - Provision of innovation grants for low-carbon and climate-resilient livestock management;
- Activity 4 - Implementation of water-efficient irrigation systems for fodder production; and
- Activity 5 - Improvement of animal health.

#### Output 4: This will build institutional and main stakeholders' capacity, and support policy improvement activities in tranche 1, and to be replicated in tranches 2 and 3:

- Activity 1 - Support for project management and implementation;
- Activity 2 - Support for low-carbon and climate-resilient rangeland management;
- Activity 3 - Improvement of the capacity for low-carbon and climate-resilient agriculture;
- Activity 4 - Implementation of monitoring and evaluation and M&E system; and
- Activity 5 - Enhancement of institutional capacity for policy-reform formulation, implementation, and monitoring.

### Key problem that this ASDIP interventions and earlier NMPL were developed to address is the continuing degradation of the country's rangelands. Reducing the population of animals grazing on these rangelands was expected to halt rangeland degradation in the country. The NMPL, introduced in 2010, failed as it did not offer incentives for herders to reduce their livestock populations. The national population grazing on the rangelands where this programme was implemented had grown above the carrying capacity of these rangelands.

Apparently, the NMPL did not include enough incentives to discourage herders from using these rangelands. This resulted in overgrazing and a decline in the quality of the rangeland, characterised by draids (heavy snowfall and/or low temperatures - occasioned by a combination of summer drought followed by harsh winter). Draids actually result in the death of animals, and a coping strategy for herders is to increase their livestock population. This results in an overpopulation to mitigate income losses.

The earlier ASDIP activities discussed in paragraph 1 included the provision of adequate support to the herders to incentivize them to reduce the size and composition (percentages of goats versus cattle, for example) of their herds as stipulated in the RUSA they will sign up to. The other activities that were included are:

- Limiting the amount of resources (water, and biomass from rangelands) needed to produce a given output and, hence, making better use of these resources, which are diminishing;
- Increasing water resources, water-efficiency measures, breeds that are more resistant to draids, improved grazing practices, and improved feed availability in winter through fodder production.


---
**Page 158**
---

Other incentives built into ASDIP will involve creating an enabling environment and facilities at each PGGC covered that will enhance the efficiency of animal husbandry and its value chain. These include:

- Community-based investment at the PGG level (including development of protected grazing fields to produce hay to feed the animals in winter and to prepare for extreme climate events when grazing becomes impossible); winter shelters to reduce livestock mortality; improved pasture management practices, including occurrence: development of water wells, small bridge/river/stream crossings and earth dams to improve access to water; fencing to protect pastures from encroachment and around natural water points such as rivers), and improve animal and human living conditions in the PGGs; provision of veterinary services through training and sharing equipment/facilities to ease herders' work and improve animal productivity.
- Development of veterinary services through community-based animal health workers and the development of laboratories and provision of breeding services to increase herd fertility, reduce disease incidence due to disease outbreaks, and improve animal productivity with better breeds;
- Development of large, irrigated perimeters to produce fodder to complement local grazing resources; improved pasture management practices during winter feeding; increase resilience to droughts, and increase animal productivity thanks to a better diet; and
- Implementation of agroforestry-based water conservancy and harvesting solutions to collect and accumulate glacier melt water, spring snow meltwater, and other surface runoff in the PGGs. This will reduce the dependence on rain-fed associated ponds and partially distribute to restore ancient irrigation systems, stabilize soil erosion, increase biodiversity, improve livestock access to rich mountain pastures for livestock, and provide the basis for fodder production.
- These activities will directly contribute to improved livelihoods of the herders and their families by increasing animal productivity and income. It is also possible them to agree to reduce their herd size as members of their PGGs in line with the RUG rules. This will lead to a reduction in the number of animals population grazing on the rangelands covered will gradually be reduced.
- The planned ASDIP intervention will also include making the analog and osun crevice areas more accessible for livestock production, and providing the expertise to run some of the infrastructure that will provide animal husbandry services.

## Project Implementation and Funding Sources

The project will be funded using a multitranche financial facility covering the entire project duration of 10 years.

Activities in tranche 1 are expected to cost a total of USD 270 million. The success of tranche 1 is expected to enable the release of funds for tranche 2, which is expected to be completed in tranche 2. Activities in tranche 3 are also expected to commence following the successful completion of tranches 1 and 2 and be completed on or before the end of the 10 years of the project's lifetime.

The requested funding approval from the Board is expected to cover all the activities envisaged under this project, including mitigation and adaptation. The project will rely on financing from several sources (see table 2). Many of these funding sources will become a reality in the next few years as they are currently being developed. It should be noted that all the benefits (mitigation and adaptation strengthening) discussed will be delivered if the assumptions of the climate rationale of the project are well grounded. Following its review of this submission, the TAP has concluded that the climate rationale for mitigation and adaptation presented is acceptable. However, the independent TAP observes that no final decision has been made on the location of the activities in tranche 1 (south of Ulaanbaatar, Aimags and the north of Khovd Aimag), where tranche 1 activities will be located. As a final decision has not made yet, it is only when this is done that the delivery of mitigation and adaptation for tranches 2 and 3 can be assured. It should also be noted that it is only when this is done that the delivery of the mitigation and adaptation benefits in this project can be reliably assumed.

The project will deliver a total of 1,094.8 MCO2e to be delivered by this project as estimated and presented in the funding proposal and the annexes submitted for this review have been evaluated against the criteria set out in the TAP guidance document on mitigation below:

- Climate mitigation impact: 112.40 MCO2e in net greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions, of which 94.56 MCO2e from carbon sequestered in soil, 1.72 MCO2e concentrated in the construction and use of ADIF investments; 38.52 MCO2e through GHG mitigation in urban areas, and 0.49 MCO2e emission reductions through other activities.
- Adaptation beneficiaries: direct beneficiaries: 552,380 people, including about 54,000 vulnerable households, who will benefit from improved livelihoods and environmental conditions in the targeted aimags; indirect beneficiaries: 3.2 million
- Ecosystem restoration: sustainable low-carbon management of an estimated 28.0 million hectares of rangeland in Mongolia, and

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

#### 1.2.1 Comprehensiveness

In the absence of this project intervention, the population of animals grazing on the rangelands in Mongolia will continue to exceed the carrying capacity there and, thus, this ecosystem will continue to degrade. Given that animal husbandry is an important sector of the national economy, it is essential for the Government to take action to reverse this trend at the country's economy. However, information available on the performance of the first phase of the NMLP (2018–2021) showed that, despite the strong interest of the Government to achieve a reduction in herd size and improve rangeland management, the majority of herders who had all types of animal been recorded in the aimags where the NMLP was implemented. A strong argument for this is that the incentives offered by the NMLP were not adequate to induce participation in the project.

The participating PGWs will derive benefits not only from increased value per animal because of their economic performance, but also from improved access to markets and services related to the animal husbandry value chain facilities. The enhanced value of the participating herders will be further complemented by the project's other interventions, such as risk management tools for example, disaster risk reduction by reducing the negative impact of droughts, provision of herders with incentives and organizational structure for rangeland management. Therefore, the ASHP intervention is expected to have a positive effect on the economic performance of participating PGWs because it will enable them to have better incomes and, therefore, provide them with adequate incentives to own a reduced number of animals which can be exploited by the cash-banking sector as well as a participant in ASHP.


---
**Page 162**
---

## Innovation

### 1.2.2 Innovation

Innovation is currently delivered when changes are made to the way things are done, with a value creation and the ultimate creation of a better way of doing what is failing, and it can be seen as an improvement in the system. The current situation in Mongolia is characterised by a continuous degradation of this important country resource due to overgrazing and climate change. This will continue unless the herd carrying capacity of the rangelands is increased. This will remain the case unless the ASHP intervention delivers a lower per animal value for participating herders, which would result in their numbers declining to below the carrying capacity of the rangeland. The review of the funding proposal showed that the various ASHP interventions would deliver a high value per animal for participating herders and that they are therefore likely to be more effective than less effective. Some of the tangible interventions that will enhance the value per animal for participating herders include:

(a) Enabling the participating herders to earn income from their share of the profits (or income ownership shares) from processing activities, which will compensate them for maintaining their livestock.

(b) Provision to herdsmen of inputs such as breeding services, improved access to water, and feed that helps them lower their GHG emissions and grazing pressure while maintaining output. This will make the animal husbandry value per animal higher compared to the current situation.

(c) Reduction in the annual loss of the herds' bushveld value chain, through the introduction of low-carbon and climate-resilient technologies, which will promote the further use of waste materials in processing; and

(d) Provision of lower carbon-emission production facilities that are accessible to herds during the winter season, especially during the dual extreme events (which currently usually lead to the death of animals from inadequate feeding, shortages of drinking


---
**Page 163**
---

water, and inclement weather conditions). Provision of these facilities will ensure that animals have access to fodder and drinking water during these events and thus reduce animal deaths during them.

These interventions, which are ASDF components, among others, are characteristics built into the project design. The independent TAP believes it will incentivize participating herders to reduce the population and composition of their herds compared to what would happen in the absence of this project. This is because the project will provide a more equitable distribution of herders groups in the participating PIGs compared to the status quo. This will create a better way of doing things for the future, which will be sustainable.

The ASDF has provided a range of options for a variety of herd grazing on rangelands covered by ASDF. Given this, the independent TAP has concluded that ASDF as presented in this proposal is innovative

The AIF will also provide co-financing for some of the key outputs of this project. As such, the independent TAP is convinced that the AIF, together with the World Bank (which has already committed to supporting the Government of Mongolia), should contribute to the delivery of an enabling environment. In addition, the ASDF has provided a range of options for a variety of herd grazing on rangelands covered by ASDF. Given this, the independent TAP has concluded that ASDF as presented in this proposal is innovative.

### 1.3.4 Replicability and scalability: knowledge and learning

20 According to information presented in the funding proposal, the proposed project will be implemented under three tranches. Tranche 1 will build on the Green Gold Project implemented by the State Agency for Nature Protection (SAP) in Khovd Aimag and the Tuv Aimag in the western waislands. It will cover Boru Ulgii Aimag, the north of Khovd Aimag, and the west of Ovoo Aimag. Tranche 2 will complete tranche 1 investments and expand to the eastern part of the project area. The purpose of this tranche is to implement the selection criteria that have been pre-designated selection criteria where partial complementary activities have been implemented. Tranche 3 will then extend to other areas that will be selected based on local preconditions and on eligibility for funding under the GCF’s Green Climate Fund (GCF) investment readiness proposal. It is concluded in the funding proposal that, through this implementation strategy, tranches 2 and 3 will contribute to the project’s overall objective of increasing the number of eligible herders, improving herd management readiness, and selection criteria outlined in the funding proposal.

It is important to stress here that the acceptability of the outcomes of tranche 1 is because many of the areas covered by this tranche are already under ASDF. This will provide a convincing climate rationale for the amgaag covered by tranche 1. Therefore, as the aims for tranches 2 and 3 are to extend the project’s coverage in terms of both area and number of eligible herders, it is important that the independent TAP provides a convincing climate rationale for these aims for one to conclude that the work in those future tranches will effectively contribute to the overall objective. It is recommended that the applicant should submit a new funding proposal for tranches 2 and 3 that will cover clearly selected amgaag and provide a convincing climate rationale. This approach is very necessary as the request for GCF

# Sustainable development potential

## Alignment with Sustainable Development Goals

### SDG (Ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all) - Given that water is needed for various purposes in rangeland management (drinking water, livestock watering, irrigation systems for fodder production, etc.), ASHP will cover several water production and conservation measures. These include the construction of small dams to store rainwater during rainy summer rain and flood water, and water from available surface sources; development of water harvesting structures such as catchment basins and reservoirs; and efficient irrigation systems for fodder production, development of ecosystem-based water perimeters; and capacity-building for herders cooperatives and their organization for efficient use of water resources.

### SDG (Promote sustained, inclusive and sustainable economic growth, full productive employment and decent work for all) - Through the degradation-free rangeland management activities, the economy will be made more sustainable. Being a significant sector of the country's economy, sustainable development of the animal husbandry sector is essential to ensure food security and income generation in the country.

### SDG (Build resilient infrastructure, promote inclusive and sustainable industrialization and innovation) - Value chain initiatives will be delivered in the cities of the aimags linked to the activities in the rangelands covered. This action will be enhanced via the development of value chains for livestock products and other commodities.

### SDG (Make cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable) - Cities in the aimags where the value chain facilities will be located will be developed

### Economic co-benefits

25. According to the discussion in the funding proposal, the project will promote economic diversification and employment through its various investments in construction activities; agroparks and irrigated perimeters and related financial instruments for SMEs; and construction of infrastructure such as roads and bridges that are related to agroparks, micro- small and medium-sized enterprises. It has been estimated and presented in the funding proposal that this will result in the creation of 10,852 jobs directly and indirectly, with the creation of about 11,400 green jobs through its 3 binding windows and guarantee component, creating in return more than 150,000 indirect jobs. Again, according to the funding proposal, this will result in an increase in employment rates for women in rural areas from a low of 8.4 per cent to a high of 12.6 per cent in 2018, compared to a national average of about 7.5 per cent.

### Social co-benefits

The funding proposal also describes several impressive social co-benefits. Such benefits that output 1 is expected to deliver include: (i) access to utilities at improved housing units; maid provision and other services provided by the project; (ii) affordable housing options with rent and rent-to-own schemes; (iii) improved child-friendly and gender-sensitive public spaces with high community participation in their design, construction and maintenance; (iv) increased awareness of the community to improve appropriation and to build social cohesion; (v) reduced large-scale outmigration from rural areas; (vi) improved access to education for children living in rural areas; (vii) improved livelihood opportunities with sustainable employment opportunities that will now be available in their communities, which will reduce migration pressures on urban areas; and (viii) a significant social benefit that enhances family social cohesion and a good gender dimension. The funding proposal also describes several impressive social benefits that the loan beneficiaries in the inter-sous-centres will deliver. The funding proposal states that outputs 2 and 3 are also expected to lead to concrete social co-benefits.

### Environmental co-benefits

Key environmental co-benefits likely to be delivered by this project will come specifically from the new and upgraded urban and agrobusiness infrastructure and services [i.e.


---
**Page 166**
---

water supply, storm-water management, wastewater collection, electrical power, solid waste management, drainage and flood control) that ASDFP will develop for aligning capital cities and selected soot centres. These will significantly improve environmental conditions, and the standard of living of communities in these areas.

The project is expected to directly and indirectly improve local air quality in terms of emissions of nitrogen oxides, sulfur oxides and carbon dioxide. The project will also reduce indoor air pollution from traditional cooking and heating, which produces upward of 95 per cent of the ground-level leads of PM2.5 and PM10 in urban areas. This will be replaced by modern stoves in homes. The former will effectively contribute to PM2.5 and PM10 levels in urban areas, while the latter effectively reduce heavy, in-house PM pollution to zero, thereby improving the respiratory health of people.

### 3.3.3 Gender-related co-benefits

The project is classified as effective gender mainstreaming considering its impacts on women's access to basic rural and urban infrastructure and services, economic and financial resources, and decision-making power. The project will also contribute to women's empowerment, as described in section G2 of the funding proposal and in annex 8.

Given the contributions to the various elements of sustainable development, the independent TAP categorizes the sustainable development potential of this intervention as high.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

##### Needs of the country and the rural population

According to the information in the funding proposal, Mongolia is vulnerable to natural disasters such as droughts and floods (episodes of extreme cold and heavy snow). In some years, these result in massive livestock losses, which in turn negatively impact the incomes of herders. A herder group can lose up to 80 per cent of its livestock population during a single year.

While increasing herd size is often a rational coping strategy, it is counterproductive to the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the Government of Mongolia's goals for land use planning, restoring grasslands, and mitigating GHG emission from animal husbandry. The present submission proposes an alternative approach that will help herders maintain their livestock while increasing their animal populations. Many of these activities will deliver adaptive capacity-strengthening changes to the livelihoods of participating herders.

Mitigation benefits are also very likely to be delivered given the fact that the incentive package will be in place to encourage participating herders to keep their animal populations at the level specified by the project. This will ensure that the herd size is maintained, and that livestock populations (and types), at levels that will reveal the ever-increasing current trend of degradation of grasslands. The project will also provide technical assistance to deliver infrastructure and strategies that will protect the animals from the status quo impacts of droughts and floods. In this way, participating herders can expect to see their animal population protected from the losses by the higher value they earn per animal, and thus they will be unlikely to increase herd size in response.

It is important to note that this presentation of the project as a cross-cutting submission, backed by a proper climate rationale (at least for tracker 1 locations) has been adequately and acceptably presented to support the inclusion of the project into the GCF portfolio. The rationale is based on three pillars related to climate change and climate-related disasters: (i) such disasters include droughts and floods, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity as a result of climate change; (ii) these disasters resulting in massive livestock losses; (iii) the interventions incorporated in this ASDFP submission will deliver a higher value per animal for participating herders, which will incentivize such herders


---
**Page 167**
---

not to increase their herd sizes in reaction to these natural-disaster-induced events (4). This, in turn, will eventually deliver a population of animals grazing on the rangeland created by ASHP below the carrying capacity of the rangeland; and (v) this change will eventually catalyze the necessary transition from extensive livestock production systems towards more sustainable units. In this way, the needs of the country and of the rural population are likely to be met.

10. The independent TAP notes that the conclusion above (i.e., that the ASHP interventions will eventually meet the needs of the recipient) is not certain. This conclusion would only be conditional on presenting an acceptable climate rationale for the awaage where tranches 2 and 3 will be implemented, as described in Section 14.2.

The independent TAP concludes that the needs of the country and of the rural population as a metric of the success of ASHP are likely to be met at a high level of confidence, and that, if the AE demonstrates a climate rationale for the awaage where tranches 2 and 3 will be implemented, the needs of the recipient potential can be categorized as high.

### Strengthening of local market capacity

10. Some of the project grant funding that is co-financing crowed by the concessional GCF lending will be used to address some crucial capacity-building needs. One of these needs that will strengthen the local capital market will be activity 3 of output 4, which will target the development of a national credit rating agency and the establishment of business financing. As this will happen with the project architecture discussed in this submission, the independent TAP believes that the AE/ASHP interventions will strengthen the local capital market.

#### Institutional needs

10. Although there is no definite capacity-building programme for government agencies, there is a need identified for the building of the capacity of the project management and implementation support. The independent TAP believes this will consist of releasing staff of the national credit rating agency to work on the project. In addition, the output 4 of ASHP provides a comprehensive capacity-building programme for the government agencies involved in project implementation. This will be implemented through the provision of technical capacity support in-country. Moreover, it may be adequate to impact effects to replicate this intervention beyond the scope of the project. The independent TAP believes that the impact of this work is appropriately monitored and reported. As an MREV protocol will be established as part of the delivery of ASHP, the independent TAP has therefore concluded that the project will deliver on sound institutional capacity.

As discussed above, the various needs of the country and especially those of the herders regarding the success of ASHP are likely to be met at a high level of confidence. This work will be positively promoted by the success of this project. In addition to this, successful implementation of this project and its replication to other rangelands not covered by the proposed AE/ASHP interventions will have a positive impact on the overall economic prosperity of the recipients and of the country. The capacity-building through grant funding from co-financing crowding by concessional GCF lending will be used to address some crucial needs, especially of public and private institutions relevant to the sustainability of the rangeland in Mongolia. In this way, the needs of the country and of the rural population as a metric of the success of ASHP are likely to be met at a high level of confidence.

The independent TAP has decided to categorize the needs of recipient potential of this submission as high.


### Country ownership

#### Relevance to national policies and objectives

Scale: High


---
**Page 168**
---

The proposed reduction in animal numbers, as a main part of the measures to restore rangelands, will deliver a reduction in the emissions of methane and nitrous oxide from the animal husbandry sector.

### Improved range management and the reducing or limiting of animal members have long been policy priorities of the Government of Mongolia. For example, they were part of Mongolia’s nationally appropriate mitigation action submission of 2010 on livestock issues in the absence of ASDP. The Government’s NMLP program coupled with some bilateral interventions may be the only existing national-level initiatives that are directly linked to the objective of reducing emissions from rangelands. As reported in the funding proposal and several annexes, the NMLP, which is technically aligned with the low-carbon sustainable range management approach, has grown beyond the carrying capacity. Therefore, the proposed ASDP intervention is needed so that relevant national objectives are aligned with the objective of reducing the population of animals grazing on rangelands in Mongolia to meet the nationally determined contributions targets that are aligned ASDP objectives include:

The NMLP and other initiatives for the promotion of sustainable range management are long-standing priorities of the Government, including in its national sector policies, such as the National Livestock Program, and in the country’s submission on livestock issues in the absence of ASDP. The NMLP, which is technically aligned with the low-carbon rangeland management will help to address climate change challenges to the animal husbandry sector and contribute towards achieving Mongolia’s nationally determined contributions (see page 79 of that report). In line with this commitment, the Government has already taken steps in the area of sustainable range management in this proposal. The following parts of its section B.1 provides relevant background.

### The National Policy on Climate Change (NAPCC) – This provides the overarching climate change strategy of Mongolia. One of the key goals of the NAPCC, which is aligned with the low-carbon sustainable rangeland management approach to manage GHG emissions as well as to promote economic effectiveness and efficiency, and implementation of “green” policies. Rangeland management and other activities covered by this proposal are also included in the NAPCC.

### The Green Development Policy – The policy has 6 strategic objectives (SOs), of which 4 (SO1, SO2, SO3, and SO5) directly relate to the proposed ASDP.

Again, the NMLP as a national policy is leading in the other direction regarding the population of animals that will graze on the rangelands compared to the aspirations for a lower animal population. The NMLP is aligned with the low-carbon sustainable range management targeted by the Government and included in several international documents. The strong incentives to participate in ASDP will enable achievement of the goal of reducing the population of animals grazing on the rangelands in Mongolia.

### Implementation capacity of the accredited entity and executing entities

* This is not to say that the other objectives are not linked to ASDP. For example, SO4 focuses on green jobs (and in the latter part of the document, it is stated that “The project will support the development of a business plan for the establishment of a rural credit institution through professional loans and equipment”; and “Strengthen individual’s ability/capacity to overcome the adverse impacts of climate change by providing training and capacity building activities.”). However, these are directly dependent on the nature, natural causes and resources*, which again are strongly linked to ASDP.

### The AIF - a regional development bank - will act as the AI for the proposed project.

The AIF will also provide co-financing, while at the same time administering the GCF grant and loan that will be approved for this project. The AIF, in its role as the AI, will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including (i) financial administration; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring that the project is implemented to achieve objectives; (iv) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to GCF on (v) project closure and revocation. This AI is well suited and prepared for these roles.

The AIF was established in 1993 under the Mongolian Law No. 204 dated 15 August 1991. The AIF has approved sovereign loans totaling USD 2.3 billion, non-sovereign loans worth USD 68 million and GCF grants totaling USD 17.1 million. In addition to these, the AIF has committed a loan of USD 16.1 million for Mongolia in 2018. The AIF committed USD 430 million for 9 projects, and USD 115 million for 1 project in 2017. The Mongolian National Bank (MNB) as the AIF was opened in 2001. The AIF is well placed to develop and implement ASDIP, building on its rich experience.

The executing entities for ASDIP are the Government of Mongolia, represented by the Ministry of Finance, and the Ministry of Construction and Urban Development (MCUD), and Development Agency (DA). The MCUD is responsible for the implementation in the city of Ulaanbaatar. The main implementing entities will be the MCUD, Ministry of Food, Agriculture and Light Industry (MFLI) and the DA. In addition to these, other government agencies have been involved in the project preparation process.

Management assessments have been conducted on the executing and implementing agencies in accordance with the GCF requirements. Furthermore, measures are being taken to identify and enhance the capacity of the entities. The financial risks that were identified will be mitigated with the support of financial management consultants under activity 3 of output 4, and the risk mitigation plan is currently being developed.

The project has been designed through a consultative process involving extensive communication with relevant planning and technical agencies at national level, and among local governments, and other stakeholders. The consultations have identified objectives and constraints. It has identified how existing infrastructure can best support the existing and future needs of the population in the target areas. A stakeholder workshop required to support urban upgrading and economic activities promoted by the project. A stakeholder workshop was held on 17 February 2020 at the headquarters of the departments of the Ministry of Environment and Tourism was held on 7 February 2020. At the request of the Secretariat, a letter was sent to the Secretary General of the Ministry of Environment and Tourism, sent a no objection letter to the Secretariat to convey the highest level of support of the Government of Mongolia to the Secretariat on 18 March 2020. This further strong evidence of country ownership.

The project seeks debt and grant financing from GCF to implement the Mongolia: Aimag and Soum Centers Green and Resilient Regional Development Investment Program (ASDIP).
