# Part I: Gender Analysis/Assessment

## Approach and methodology

### Objectives
A gender analysis and Gender Action Plan were prepared with the following objectives:

- Analyze the gender and development legal framework, policies, programs and institutions.
- Detail the gender context and analyze the gender issues;
- Analyze the potential benefits of the project in terms of gender;
- Prioritize actions to address gender inequalities;
- Develop an Action Plan detailing budget/resources requirements and implementation arrangements.

The detailed gender analysis is available in TRTA final report (volume VI, Section Gender Analysis and Gender Action Plan)

## Multi-level analysis

Considering the wide scope of Tranche 1 of the MFF, with various projects involving different beneficiaries, the analysis was conducted at three levels:

- Within the urban development component (Outputs 1 & 2):
    - The gear areas rehabilitation project: These pilot projects take place in the aimag centers. The beneficiaries are the residents of the pilot streets and to a larger extent, the residents of the gear areas.
    - The two soum center development: These projects take place in two soum centers: Umnogovi (Uv) and Delger Bayan Ulgi). The beneficiaries are the residents of these two soum centers and the surrounding population using the facilities of these two soums.

- Within the regional agri-business development component (Outputs 2 & 3):
    - The regional agri-business development projects aim at developing the livestock industry and extending the value chain (economic development) while at the same time improving the living conditions of pastoralists (gender equality) and herder's inclusion in the value chain (inclusive development). The beneficiaries are the herdsmen, the herders' organizations (PUsA and cooperatives) and the businesses (private sector operating in the livestock industry).

All analyses thus be broken down into these three levels of projects.

## Tools

A wide range of tools has been used to conduct social and gender analysis:

- Analysis of secondary data
- Surveys
- Key informant interviews


---
**Page 177**
---

* Focus Group Discussions
* Public meetings and consultations

Women have been engaged in consultations related to all outputs, either using gender-specific consultation tools (such as women-only FGDs) or ensuring their active participation in mixed consultations. The overall approach has been to integrate within each tool specific measures related to gender, in particular:

* All surveys enabled to collect sex-disaggregated data and identify female-headed households who could be consulted;
* When mixed FGDs were conducted, questions were made separating male and female population. When this was not possible due to logistic constraints or small population size, the Consultant ensured sufficient representativeness of women (at least 40% of FGD participants);
* Key informant interviews with aims/g social experts, the Family, youth and children units, also enabled to understand indirectly ger areas' women's concerns. NGOs were also consulted.

**Ger areas redevelopment**

* Women-only FGDs were held. The principle was to ask i same questions as to men to identify women's needs in terms of land ownership, access to water resources, on: public space & safety; women's needs in terms of public facilities; women's needs of support to develop their economic activities;
* Semi-structured interviews with key informants were held, in particular to update the residents on proposed project design. For all mixed consultations, the Consultant ensured participation of women (at least 40% of total possible participants). The Consultant noticed high and substantial participation of women.
* Key informant interviews were conducted to understand gender issues and how to optimize project impacts on women.

**Regional agribusiness development**

* Women-only FGDs were held with female herders to study their specific needs and concerns. Aimed FGD, the Consultant also targeted a 60% participation of women;
* Key informant interviews were also conducted to understand gender issues and how to optimize project impacts on women.
* No women herder group could be identified in the project areas.

**National background**

**National gender policies and strategies**

Gender equality has been one of the primary threats of the Government of Mongolia, and the regulatory framework for gender equality is relatively strong in Mongolia. The main policies


---
**Page 178**
---

which promotes this objective and the protection of women and children are the (a) Gender Equality Law (2011), (b) Law on Combating Domestic Violence, (c) Labor Code, (d) Family Law, (e) Law on Social Welfare, and (f) Law on Social Insurance. Other laws contain provisions to promote gender equality such as the Constitution of Mongolia (1992); the Criminal Code (2003), the Civil Code (2004), the Law on Education (2005), the Law on Education (2002, as amended in 2006); the Law on Combating Trafficking in Persons (2012); the Law on Allocation of Land to Mongolian Citizens for Ownership; the Law on Parliamentarians and the Law on Local Government.

In line with these policies the following programs are being implemented by the government: (a) Comprehensive National Development Strategy**, (b) National Program on Combating Domestic Violence, (c) National Program on Combating Trafficking in Persons Women with the purpose of sexual exploitation, (d) and Midterm Strategy and Action Plan for Implementation of the Law of Mongolia on Promotion of Gender Equality (2013–2016), (e) the National Program on Ensuring Gender Equality (2017-2021).

The National Program on Ensuring Gender Equality (2017-2021) has the following objectives: (i) Provide support to equal participation in the sustainable socio-economic development processes and increase opportunities so that both women and men can benefit equally from development; (ii) Promote gender equality through education, training and awareness raising at national, sector, local and organizational levels; (iii) Raise awareness and promote advocacy for gender equality among all segments of society to ensure equal participation in their families and society, with the objective to change gender stereotypes nationally; (iv) Improve gender-responsive structures and mechanisms at national, sector, local and organizational levels to ensure equal opportunities and access to comprehensive and systematic preventive and care services that advance the elimination of gender-based violence and discrimination; (v) Encourage women's participation in politics and decision-making processes.

Following this national program, sectoral strategies were developed by line ministries. The Ministry of Construction and Urban Development; the Ministry of Food, Agriculture, and Light Industry; the Ministry of Employment and Social Protection issued sectoral gender strategies for 2017-2021, with a deadline to be completed in 2024/2025.

The National Committee on Gender Equality (NICE), established in 2005, is the government body led by Prime Minister responsible in the implementation of the National Program on Gender Equality and is composed of 13 Ministries of Mongolia as its sub-councils; and §

In adopting the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) in 2005, the Mongolian Parliament also set concrete gender-related targets in education, political participation and health. The MDG-based Comprehensive National Development Strategy (2008-2021) reaffirms these commitments and serves as a framework for achieving the MDGs. The strategy is based on the principle that gender equality embodied in the National Development Strategy is aimed at ensuring "human rights-based policies, programs and services" and to achieve this goal, the strategy includes the labor market” and contains provisions to increase the number of female candidates in parliamentary elections. The 2013-2016 Midterm Strategy and Action Plan for Implementation of the Law on Gender equality (adapted in 2011), the integration of gender equality content at all levels of education, and ensuring women have equal rights to property.


---
**Page 179**
---

# Country-level gender issues

Main country-level gender issues are the following:

- **Access to employment opportunities and wage gap:** Discrimination against women at the labor market remains common. Employers still appear to favor hiring men for jobs that require a high level of reproductive age or mothers of young children, and concerns remain about coerced early retirement among female employees (National Program on Ensuring Gender Equality, 2017).

Labor force participation gap is high: In 2018 NSO Statistics show that female labor force participation rate was 56.9%, compared to the male participation of 69.5%. Gap has increased since 2008, when respective rates were of 62.8% and 64.2%. Factors influencing this gap is the high number of women who are married or widowed, the number of women engaged in household activities and childcare, and the fact that women retire earlier than men (UNDP, 2014). Another factor contributing to the labor force participation gap is the difference between male and female wages. Women with higher education levels are likely to take advantage of self-employment outside the agriculture sector (SDC, 2014).

Gender bias exists in the division of labor between men and women by sector. Based on the 2014 Labor Force Survey, the NSO Gender overview shows that "men predominantly employed in mining and quarrying, manufacturing, construction, and agriculture, while women are predominantly employed in education and the health and tourism industries (although less often at senior levels). Women are concentrated in a relatively narrow set of occupations, such as domestic work, food preparation, and cleaning. Men are more concentrated in support positions in retail and catering and in teaching. The most feminized employment sectors are agriculture, fishing, forestry, and hunting, which pay the lowest salaries". Stereotypes of suitable jobs for women and men are still common (ADB: SDC and NCGE 2015).

There are also challenges in reaching wage equity. According to 2018 NSO statistics, the average female worker's monthly salary was MNT 963.3 thousand compared to the male average of MNT 1,103.3 thousand, representing a salary gap of 18%. Wage gap has increased over the last ten years: in 2008, male average was of 291.2 thousand and female


---
**Page 180**
---

average of 258.1 thousand, representing a wage gap of 11.4%. Inequalities are also reflected in pensions*.

The gender imbalance in favor of girls persists at the tertiary level of education, with 59.4% of female students (NSO, 2019); however, girls tend to be particularly at a disadvantage in converting their higher levels of education into higher levels of income, which is demonstrated by consistently higher unemployment among women with higher education and lower wages than men.

* Female-headed households living in poverty
According to National Statistics Office (NSO) of Mongolia, the proportion of female-headed households living below the poverty line was 31.5% in 2016. However, data from Participatory Living Standards Assessment of the NSO have identified that a disproportionate number of women-headed households are living in poverty and that this proportion is growing. Women are limited to working as domestic workers or in small-scale businesses, often at home. Some women, who are employed or engaged in small enterprises, need to work longer hours than men to earn an adequate income for their families (MCA Survey [NSO Mongolia], 2009) has noted that single parent households, which are usually woman-headed, continue to be more vulnerable. In some cases, women are left alone to manage the household and children while working long hours in order to support the family. Female-headed households in urban ger areas living without adequate access to water supply and sanitation and other basic infrastructure and services is high (ADB and World Bank, 2005).

* Vulnerable married-headed households, in particular in rural areas
The intergenerational transmission of poverty perpetuates women's lives. Women are in charge of reproductive activities and in preparing purchasing food, cleaning and heating the gas, providing water for their families. In case of a death or serious illness in the family, in case of loss of wives or in case of short or long-term separation (notably in the case when wives go and live with their children in the soum centers during school year), men face difficulties to manage household and are subject to the situation (these issues are further detailed in the current situation analysis section).

* Reverse gender gaps related to access to education and health
Contrary to other countries in Asia, girls in Mongolia are far more likely to attend school, particularly at secondary level. The number of female students aged 15–24 increased from 1,185 (girls and boys) children aged 6-14 dropped out of school in year 2013-2014; out of which 1,075 (370 girls and 705 boys) were from rural areas – meaning the percentage of boys' dropouts is 60% in rural areas.

Male life expectancy is lower than female life expectancy, and according to a baseline STEPS Survey (MCA-Mongolia 2009), males aged 15-34 had a higher risk of developing non-communicable diseases compared to women in the same age group, due to i) poor lifestyles

* The average monthly retirement pension for man was MNT 38.7 thousand (USD) in 2016 compared to that of women; MNT 295.8 thousand (. Data for females retiree have a person that MNT 584.4 thousand) less, or 18.5% lower than men.
Data for 2009 is similar with 80.7% of female students, indicating stagnation.


---
**Page 181**
---

(high rates of smoking and alcohol consumption), ii) higher incidences of accidents and suicides, and iii) hard labor activities.

* **Gender-based violence (GBV)** Mongolia faces various forms of gender-based violence, including trafficking in persons (i.e., the sexual exploitation of women and girls, labor exploitation and forced labor for men and women). The 2017 National Study on Gender-based Violence in Mongolia "Breaking the silence for equality" (UNFPA) shows that 38.7% of ever-partnered women experienced physical violence perpetrated by their partners during their lifetime. Women who have experienced physical violence by partners are more common than non-partner violence. Violence against women is often perpetuated by family members, including husbands, boyfriends, and other family members*. Women with less education were slightly more likely to report physical violence compared with more educated women, and other research reports suggest that women with lower levels of education may be more vulnerable to gender-based violence. Women experience violence regardless of their age, education, employment status or geographic location. An ADB 2018 study cites alcoholism and severe unemployment as major contributing factors*. The UNFPA report also states that the majority (75%) of women surveyed felt that institutions do not provide sufficient assistance to victims.

* **Political empowerment** Political empowerment (including representation in parliament and other elected bodies) remains a challenge for women in Mongolia, despite laws on gender equality. The National Program on Ensuring Gender Equality shows that although the number of female candidates nominated by political parties has increased over time, women still remain underrepresented in politics. Women make up 17.1 per cent of members in the current parliament and 15.9 per cent in the aimag and capital city, local citizens almost always a man. A public survey conducted by MONFEMNET and IBM in 2009 in Mongolia found that more than half (58 percent) of the survey participants, a candidate's gender was an important factor in their voting decisions.

* *ADB, SDC, NGOE, "Country Gender Assessment", 2010.
* The study shows that 43.6 per cent of the women who experienced violence from their partners stated that they did not seek help because they were afraid to tell anyone about it. Women who have experienced physical and/or sexual violence are more likely to self-assess their general health as poor and also report a greater number of chronic illnesses.
* The study shows that "Women who experienced partner violence were more likely to have children with behavioral problems, such as hyperactivity or aggression". It is also noted that witnessing or experiencing domestic violence in childhood increases the chance of being a violent partner later on.
* *ADB, Translating Women's Voices into Action in Mongolia: Addressing Gender-Based Violence through Community Mobilization and Legal Advocacy*, 2013.
* The UNFPA 2017 study shows that only 4 in 1 women who have never experienced physical or sexual violence sought help from a health facility. This is because they do not consider the violence as unlawful. The rate is much higher among women who have experienced physical and/or sexual violence, with 35% of them seeking help from a health facility. The study also notes that it is justified if a man hits his wife if the spouse is unfaithful.
* The same study shows that only about one in five (20%) of those who experienced violence approached a health facility, and less than 1.5% sought help from a shelter and other care services. A large share of women (26.3%) had told no one about their experiences before the interview.


---
**Page 182**
---

It is worth pointing out that the gender gap exists not only at the highest decision-making level but also at the grassroots level. Male preference is still common among herders when it comes to the selection of group leaders (MCA-Mongolia, 2013; SDC, 2015). Rural women tend to be more engaged in household chores which affords them less time for community work. This leads to their underrepresentation in political and social spheres in rural areas.

- **Access to property ownership:** Property ownership including ownership of land is a concept and practice which originated in the 1990s and hence is relatively new to Mongolia, marked by a culture of nomadic pastoralism and the lack of formal property rights. The study noted that women have played an important role in the new property relations emerging between men and women as households began coming together with the accumulation of assets. The Assessment noticed this bias towards male ownership and recommended that more attention be paid to implementing legal reforms that facilitates structures promoting equity.

Since then the Law on Protection of Gender Equality had been amended and explicitly states that enterprises should have at least 30% female representation in the government and other sectors. "Such a rule has a duty to ensure men and women equal access on equal terms to land and other immovable and movable property, budget allocations, financial assets, credit and other economic wealth and resources."

There is an insufficient quantitative data towards assessing the implementation of the goal; however, existing studies show persistent discrepancies:

- A comparative pilot study undertaken by the ADB in Mongolia, Georgia and the Philippines in 2018, revealed male predominance in the ownership of housing with Mongolian women owning only 35% of all types of property. In terms of ownership of property ownership for Mongolian families, it is 60% of men and 33% of women who own their homes; with 55% of men and 27% of women having registered their ownership rights. The study also found that rural women are 1.8 times more likely to be landless than urban women and 3 times more men in the rural areas who have home ownership certificates compared to urban areas. In terms of livestock, women own only 40% with regard to all other types of property. Of the survey respondents, 65% of rural men and 64.1% of women replied that they were engaged in animal husbandry while 64.1% of men and 53.8% of women replied that they were engaged in crop farming.

- A study conducted by the SDC in 2015 among herding families established that 58.5% of household property was registered under man's name, 10.7% under the name of both spouses and only 8.5 % under name of wives. Livestock, winter and spring camps were also mostly owned by men while property outside the local area would mostly be registered in son's name.


---
**Page 183**
---

* There is also a strong gender bias related to inheritance. A 2013 survey with 8,000 urban and rural respondents showed that female share in land inheritance was only 27.2%[1]. The tradition of passing land and other immovable property down to sons is still strong. For example, 72.4% of the participants in focus group discussions held under the age of 35 years said they were not aware of any female family members who were planning to transfer the title deeds to daughters. A traditional understanding that family property must be written under the name of the head of household survives to this day, and women are often excluded from the decision-making process regarding land ownership. In addition, many women do not have legal documents for their land or other immovable property, and mothers who are marrying off their sons to prepare the dwelling for the newweds further reinforce the idea that they should not own any land. This is a situation that will continue limiting women's rights to land and other important property[2]. This has important impacts on crop production by gender. Focus group discussions with land tenure experts in Mongolia indicate that women are more likely than men to focus on large land areas, or lend their land; women mostly do vegetable gardening on smaller plots (ADB, 2016).

* Current laws and regulations are yet to be strengthened to reduce disparities. For example, under the Civil Code of Mongolia stipulates that assets registered after marriage are a common property of family members, the person in whose name this property is registered has priority over other family members when dividing up family members, which entails gender disparities if property relations are skewed in favor of one particular gender (ADB, 2016).

* Women's participation in businesses is also often informal. It is a common phenomenon that women do not participate in family businesses without formulating their shares in those businesses, and it is a similarly common phenomenon that in the case of a divorce, the woman loses out, having no claim to any share of the business (ADB, 2016).

### Analysis of gender issues related to Tranche 1 projects and areas

Gender issues in ger areas in the aimag centers

Household chores related to water, sanitation, hygiene and heating. Women are mainly responsible for household chores such as cooking, cleaning and other responsibilities related to household sanitation, health, hygiene, and participate in heating-related tasks. Women are heavily affected by the current poor water, sanitation and heating systems. The pit latrines are the primary type of toilet found in ger areas, which is not only inconvenient but also unhygienic for women who do not have the necessary materials and the quality of construction is generally poor. The respondents expressed the difficulty and the discomfort of using the open pit latrines, especially during


---
**Page 184**
---

winter and particularly for women, children, disabled and elderly people. Water is mainly fetched in water kiosks and public wells, which is time-consuming and tiring (some women report spending over one hour for water collection and walking up to 2 km). They are going 2-3 times over the weekdays and 4-5 times during the weekend for washing and cleaning. Most of the households use firewood as a primary source of cooking fuel, which is used in stoves has been contributing significantly to the high levels of air pollution in the camp centers, especially during the winter months. Women and children are exposed to indoor pollution brought by the smoke from these stoves, which can cause respiratory problems and other respiratory diseases. The high levels of exposure to smoke from a biodeg-py of burning of coal is linked with increased risk of lung cancer, bronchitis, asthma, emphysema, and pneumonia. Besides, making fire for heating is time-consuming and tiring (buying and carrying coal, burning, removing ashes), especially in the cold times when they have to burn coal 3-5 times a day. The women are also exposed to smoke from the stoves while cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, etc., which can cause respiratory problems. In addition, the men, women, the children, whoever is available will do this work. Making fire involves also removal of the ashes from the stove, which is moody on women's shoulders.

Public space & safety: Women have stressed different priorities and needs related to public spaces. The most serious issue has been harassment on the streets, especially for adolescent girls walking along the road to go to school at night (cases of gender-based violence). Safety, particularly in the evening hours when women are returning home from work, is a major factor negatively affecting their living conditions in the pilot streets. Girls and women are often exposed to harassment that in some cases result in sexual abuse while walking in ger areas. Rape cases have also been reported by women who live on the streets or in slums of different ages. Perception of insecurity and gender-based violence is strongly linked to the lack of proper lighting and sidewalks, which are needs stressed by women. Walking is the main transportation mode for women in these areas, especially during the night hours. This is crucial for women's independent movement and access to education, health and other important services.

Need of social amenities: Women have insisted on the need of a number of amenities. First, women insist on the need of playgrounds for children and green spaces, to spend time with their children out of the khaishas. As commented by a resident, "many mothers are going out for fresh air and exercise but they do not have anywhere to go. They are just walking doing it." In green spaces, parks with public space furniture, and playgrounds are built, these areas would be much used by families and would make the neighborhoods more children friendly. Second, women have stressed on the need of a park in the neighborhood of public park, therefore children mostly stay within the khaishas. It is also a burden for women who already have to go out with their children to collect water and other daily needs or are unemployed or not to have anywhere else to go with their children to spend free time. Besides, in the three airing centers, there is a need to build new kindergartens. In general, there are no facilities available for children in these areas; therefore, the number of public overcrowded. This need is mainly stressed by women who are primarily in charge of looking after the children. Last, there is a need of dormitories in the streets close to the target street of UGC, where women can stay during their working hours and also have access to safe places. This situation is inconvenient both for the children and the families in which they are staying. The children cannot go out freely because they do not know who will take care of them when they stay and there is a general concern that their dependency on another household is not good for them. There are also some reported cases of household head assaulting the relatives'


---
**Page 185**
---

daughter staying at her home. Families hosting the children are also worried about the safety of the children on the way from school to home. In particular, the families are worried about girls being attacked by men or any crime committed by drunk men.

Gender-based violence and need for a women gender-bias violence is important in all three amagai centers. It is to note that it is too complex to estimate precisely occurrence of gender-based violence (GBV) in the community. GBV includes sexual violence, physical violence against sexual assaults; partner violence and non-partner violence,) and the sensitivity of the matter and absence of relevant data at the amagai center scale. For example, according to the Family, youth and children center, most cases are never reported to the police (only 10% of under-16-year-old women, but it is estimated that there are many unreported cases, notably cases of domestic violence against women by their husbands or ex-husbands (including step-fathers). 73 cases of domestic violence were registered last year through her hotline service. According to the Family, youth and children center, most cases are never reported to the police. The reasons for this are that women do not want to report the case (for example, due to the general need assessment) and through key informant interviews. In terms of statistics, the general overview provided at the national level (of cubs) can be used. Interviewed women and professionals consider that different levels of interventions are needed to address this issue:

- Infrastructure improvements, notably road and public space improvement and street lighting, as well as the modernization of municipal systems.
- Construction of a women's center: Women need a place where they can meet and socialize and exchange on issues they face; they need a space where activities can be organized specifically for them to empower themselves and make them feel part of a community.
- Inclusion of a medical room & consultation service within a common center building. This is important in order to improve the women's identification (the service) and at the same time enabling confidentiality (for example, women victims of gender-based violence may be reluctant to go to the hospital due to what people may think).
- Construction of shelters for women victims of GBV, to provide women with temporary accommodation.
- Soft programs related to education. The project will tackle the two first demands as presented in the following section ("Strategies to address the needs of women and girls").

Lack of workspace: Some women in the target areas have home-based businesses, notably boot-making and sewing businesses. Some women are willing to expand their businesses and lack of proper facilities. They would be interested in working in appropriate workplaces. Some have expressed a desire for training on business management. Others prefer to work from home all their time in their plot. Women with micro-businesses may be interested in learning together within a community center to join resources, be more visible and learn from each other.

Cases of women-headed households: There are cases of women-headed households (the largest district) in 27 out of the 35 surveyed households in Ure (representing 19% of surveyed households), in Bi (representing 3% of surveyed households) and 17 in Khoud (representing 14% of surveyed households). Women-headed households can be considered more vulnerable, as they are left alone to manage the household. They are in much larger

## Gender issues in the primary-school context

### Lack of adequate facilities particularly impacting girls and women
Most public buildings are not equipped with indoor toilets, and only have outdoor pit latrines, sometimes over 50m away from the building. Kindergarten and school children use these pit latrines, including in the very early morning hours when they are still asleep. These latrines are often dirty (sometimes slippery), unhygienic, and do not provide intimacy/privacy to young girls and women. The poor water & sanitation facilities of the dormitory is all the more alarming, as they are permanent places of residence for students. In Western Region Impact evaluation baseline survey, 2018, 95% of primary school dormitory environment for primary students in Western Region Impact evaluation baseline survey reported that girls and women never or hardly ever wash hands after going to the toilet, and never or hardly ever wash once a week. Without indoor toilets facilities, children regardless of their age are exposed to poor sanitation conditions. In addition, girls and women are afraid of doing education. Building houses have inadequate WASH facilities and dormitories and are not gender sensitive. The situation is exposing safety risks for girls and difficulties for them to maintain hygiene.

### Poor conditions of dormitories
As described in the PSA, dormitory buildings are in very poor physical conditions. Besides the absence of adequate WASH facilities, it can be noted that the dormitories do not have enough space for students (10 children per room) or respect of gender division of rooms. Both boys and girls suffer from having to share small rooms with up to 7 other children, leading to a lack of intimacy, private space and overall unfavorable conditions for their education.

### Family issues linked to children schooling
Due in part to unfavourable school dormitory environment, mothers are often required to move closer to school together with their children separately from fathers. The separation of the spouses causes workforce scarcity in the livestock sector, which is a major source of income for many families and contributes to the high number of unemployed people in the settlement, puts economic pressure on the families (with increased housing costs and travel costs involved), creates family conflicts and cases of alcoholism.

### Gender issues in the herding society and related to agriculture
#### Women activities, tasks and specific needs
Both men and women participate in herding activities. However, the TATA baseline surveys distinguished revenues of wife and husband, which work together and, in most cases, do not have separate income. However, there is a gender division of activities in herding (detailed in the TATA final report, Volume VI). In particular, the following must be noted:

- Based on survey of the Improving school dormitory environment for primary students in Western Region Impact evaluation baseline report, 2018. This report was elaborated based on a survey covering 2,911 primary school students and their parents as well as school management, school teachers, dormitory teachers, guards and cooks in 156 dormitories.


---
**Page 187**
---

* Many activities are conducted solely by men, in particular outside activities (away from the ger) and physical activities. This includes shearing camels, the sale of camel wool, the sale of livestock (live animals) and meat, the growing of forage and preparation of animal feed, hay making, water-supplying material/craft production, taking care of livestock, and selling livestock. Women are involved in milking back again; giving water to animals or taking animals to water, other movements, looking for lost/stolen animals, training animals for riding and for household use.
* Collection of firewood and water is a major burden on women and children, due to their predominant role in reproductive activities (cleaning, cooking, washing, collecting drinking water, etc.) and limited access sources, especially from winter camps, and related difficulties is a burden for women.
* Some types of agri-business activities are traditionally women's activities: it is the case with beekeeping, poultry farming, and small-scale animal husbandry. These are micro-activities done at the household levels, and most products are used for own consumption and gifted. However, if supported, women can develop these activities into a business. The sale of honey, eggs, and other by-products can generate complementary revenues if these products are sold. Women stressed the need to support them to improve their skills for markets and the need of equipment to be used as a group.
* Vulnerability of female-headed households and separated households. Herder FGD show that women who live in extended family ger are more vulnerable. Living in the neighborhood of an extended family clan is very helpful especially during the event of droughts, windstorms or other natural disasters, during all family members assist each other to cope with these events. However, this also means that they have less time to work. Some male herders live isolated, due to their wife living in the soum center to look after school children and elder children. The women who are left alone at home can only reunited during the school breaks. Elder members of the herder households also tend to live in the soum center due to the need for medical care of diseases to the seniors. Workforce availability is a major problem, especially when it comes to shearing camels and other leg-out in animal husbandry during breaks by retrieving water, watching over animals, etc. More manpower is needed during shearing, combing goats, and animal birth periods. Family division makes livestock ownership more difficult for women who are left alone at home with activity cycle, separated households and female-headed households can be considered vulnerable groups because of the limited access to resources, clearly they face and dependency on other households or paid workers during activity peaks.
* Low decision power of women and low participation of women in community activities.
* Importance of household chores & low decision power within the households. According to a SDC 2015 study[6], women's workload is higher than men's for all the months of the year, with the exception of March and December." Despite this, women's contribution and role appear not to be adequately recognized in other aspects of herding/housing such as decision-making on major family spending and purchases, participation as community


---
**Page 188**
---

activities, and leadership”. This is a well-documented issue, which has been raised in previous publications. Despite women’s contribution to productive household work, women “are often seen in Mongolia as ‘helpers’ of their male counterparts, who are considered the ‘official’ herders (Burn, 2003; Bernewell, 2006; in Hawkins 2007: p. 77) with the implication of excluding women from decision-making” (Hawkins et al., 2006). In a study by Odgerel, Bulgan, Nananchimeg (2006), Hawkins (2007) shows that women even appear to have less decision-making power than men over decisions that most directly impact them. For example, while women are responsible for the majority of water management and water management decision-making in terms of water collection, they do not seem to have a significant role in livestock production or marketing (Hawkins et al., 2006). While women hold decision-making power within the household, this power is closely related to domestic work. Outside the domestic realm, even the realm of herding, vegetable farming or small business activities, women are often seen as helpers and not as decision-makers holding power and appear to dwindle” (p. 84). The SDC 2015 study shows that most of the decisions related to livestock production are made by men. Livestock production includes such decisions as: breeding and selection; feed management; evaluation of pastoralism management plans and herd management. In average over the 9 aspects, in terms of decision-making, husbands represent 50.2%, wives 26.2%, sons 17.5%, and daughters 5.7%.

The separation of herder households due to children schooling creates women unemployment in the soum centers. Herders underline that job opportunities are scarce in the soum center and even when women

# Migration patterns are gendered mainly due to education being a main female migration pull factor.

Migration patterns are gendered mainly due to education being a main female migration pull factor. There is indeed an important gender gap regarding education, at all levels, as previously described. Young females seeking education generates rural to urban migration which in turn leads to the formation of herds in pastoral areas (SADC 2015). According to the ADB publication, "Monogender Child Survival Situation Analysis: Advances, Challenges and Lessons Learnt from 2009" (2016), The SDC 2015 gender study on herding communities mentions the difficulties faced by young women in accessing education. These include gender disparities in education levels as well as the potential absence of social, cultural and intellectual support for girls to pursue higher education.

The SDC 2015 gender study makes a detailed analysis of visions of future from parents and children in pastoral areas. The study found that when parents are asked about their son or daughter do become herders, there is three times more respondents saying that their sons would become herders, inherit the herds and look after the livestock in the future than saying that their daughters will be herders. This shows that pastoral communities need to improve both men's and women's living conditions in the countryside, and provide incentives on female herders and provide opportunities for women in the countryside.

# Strategies to address gender issues and optimize project impacts on women

## Positive impact of provision of utility services on women, optimized through a gender-sensitive IEC strategy

As exposed previously, women are primarily tasked with water fetching, cooking, cleaning, and other household chores. With tap water as brought to their khanaa, the project will therefore have a positive impact on women in particular. Technical units will have important impacts on hygiene and intimacy. IEC campaign on efficient use of water will also be launched.

## Women's access to affordable housing and increased female ownership

Female-headed households will be given priority to TOSK unit loans. Besides, an IEC strategy will be put in place to encourage women to register their names on the asset property right certificate. This is to encourage women to register their names on the asset property right certificate. Indeed, today, property registration efforts are being towards male ownership. Although joint registration of ownership is possible, it is not common practice and is often seen as a sign of weakness. In fact, only the household head's name is written in the certificate of land ownership. The project is actually an opportunity to promote women's tenurial rights and improve gender equality in this regard through IEC strategy. This will also help reduce the risk of property disputes and the risk of negative perception by husbands' or wives' willingness to register their names on property certificates. It is therefore important that the IEC strategy target the couples and not only the women, to properly explain this concern to both men and women and facilitate understanding by both genders.

## Public space and safety

With paved or concreted road networks, drainage system, sidewalks, and adequate lighting, the mobility and access of women will be improved. As previously exposed, women expressed safety issues, especially for adolescent girls walking along the streets at night. The construction of sidewalks and street lights will help in lessening the incidence of crime and will improve women's sense of security. The IEC strategy to promote gender equality and address gender-based violence Infrastructure improvements have both an impact on women's perception of GBV risk and danger in public spaces affording decisions related to the use of the public space. In addition, the publication, redevelopment will promote measures for resident-friendly environment such as creating safe spaces for women and girls, improving street lighting and improving household sanitation infrastructure and practices. Women will be consulted during the design of public space to ensure to include design features prioritized by women.

## Gender-inclusive multi-purpose community development center

The community centers will serve as a focal point where women can gather and become venue for the residents including the elderly, youth and women to socialize and organize themselves to come up with community activities that may promote social cohesion and productive activities beneficial to all. Community-based projects such as income-generating activities will strengthen the social fabric and support women's economic independence. The community


---
**Page 193**
---

center will enable women to meet, socialize, and organize specific activities. Women organizations can use the community center for specific events, such as women’s day or the New Year. Community centers can also include rooms for businesses to be rented to the community. Community centers can include rooms to be rented out to local businesses including restaurants, cafes, etc., which will help in promoting economic empowerment of women. Even if the community center does not include specific spaces for businesses, but only a community room (multi-purpose room), women have a venue where to present their products and services. This will also make women’s voices more visible.

Playgrounds, parks and activities for children: Parks and green facilities may be utilized for exercise, sports activities and other communal physical activities. Recreational facilities for children will allow them to socialize with their peers and pursue outdoor activities. As women spend more time taking care of children, in the absence or green space/park/creational area where they can relax and engage in recreational activities, women are more stressed. Women, either because they have a home-based business or are unemployed. Thus, playgrounds, parks and recreational areas will benefit all residents and in particular women.

Land use plan taking into account needs of social facilities, in particular education and health facilities: The land use plan for the street will be designed to meet the large target of each aimag center. Experience will be replicated in the following tranches, around these three pilot streets. This will ensure that the land use plan is not only based on the demand but also on social and public services. Tranche 1 will include basic social amenities, which are most needed at the very local level (public parks, outdoor sport fields, community center, etc., as previously mentioned). The second tranche will include more advanced facilities such as kindergarten, school facilities, sport complexes, and family health centers. The land use plan elaborated for the street developed in Tranche 1 will take into account need of future social amenities (which have been identified for social amenities). The construction of kindergartens is a need stressed by women.

Economic development linked with the provision of services: Enough economic development can be created through the provision of services. Many households expressed interest in either continuing their current khasaai-based activity or creating a business once such services are brought to their khaisaa. In particular households can connect existing businesses with new markets and customers, as well as create new businesses that work/shop all-year long. They can also build new facilities connected to the networks from the technical unit. This can benefit women’s existing businesses or support the startup of new businesses.

Project gender mainstreaming benefits in the inter-sector centers

Positive impact of WASH facilities on women and girls: Infrastructure improvements will benefit to men and women, boys and girls equally. It is to note that women and girls suffer more from the lack of indoor toilets. In particular, adolescent girls suffer from the lack of appropriate sanitation facilities. The provision of adequate water supply, sanitation and sanitation facilities are key for Menstrual Hygiene Management (MHM). Women and girls manage their menstrual hygiene in a very different way than men. Therefore, WASH facilities will be sensitive to gender and safe for female. The Project will build separate toilet units for both genders.

## Project gender mainstreaming benefits in the herding society

Improved rangeland management and protection of women’s rights over pastoralland Constituents’ Union (CU) has developed a Participatory and Inclusive Herd Management Plan (PHMP) enables to reduce conflicts over land and restore the rangeland, thus ensuring sustainability of the herdsmen's activities. Rangeland Use Agreements (RUA) will be signed by both spouses of the herder and the CU will provide training for communal herdsmen. The Project will require that both spouses sign the RUA and will therefore secure rights of women in case of divorce or death of their husband. Requiring the wives to sign will ensure a more equitable distribution of resources, including land and livestock.

This is a significant step in the recognition of women as players in rangeland management.


## Financing of women's needs through Community Participation in Procurement (CPP)

The CPP list of eligible projects includes needs that are most expressed by women. As shown in the above table, women have expressed their need for water points, latrines, and for household use. Access to water for cooking and washing is a main issue for herdsmen, which is frequently stressed as a problem. Women also express their need for improved access to water points, which can be supported by CPP. Ger kindergartens are also a need mainly stressed by women, which can be supported by CPP. Basic infrastructure such as roads, bridges and dams, which can be supported by CPP. Ger kindergartens are also a need mainly stressed by women, which can be supported by CPP. Basic infrastructure such as roads, bridges and dams, which can be supported by CPP. Ger kindergartens are also a need mainly stressed by women, which can be supported by CPP.

The CPP can provide financial support for the financing of a ger and necessary equipment/materials. The ger kindergartens would be operated by the community itself.


## All PUGs will have to develop a Participatory and Inclusive Herd Management Plan (PHMP) in order to qualify for inclusion on the Community Participation in Procurement Programme.

The PHMP is participatory: it relies on participation of all PUG members, including participation of poor and vulnerable households and women. It is inclusive: (i). The PHMP includes specific sections on gender equality and women's empowerment; (ii) it includes a section on "Gender for improved female participation – it makes a basic diagnosis of women involvement and orientations; for example, in which activities/duties can women be more involved? What can be done to improve their participation?" (iii). The PHMP is participatory and inclusive and discussed at the very local level, which enables to bring local solutions, from herdsmen themselves. The PHMP will be developed by the PUGs with the support of CU. The PHMP CPP project sheet specifies who benefits from the project (number of PUG members and how poor households/women benefit from the project), number and type of beneficiaries is a project


---
**Page 195**
---

selection criterion. Projects benefiting a large number of PUG members and projects benefiting women and poor households are prioritized.

### Women’s social and political empowerment

Women's involvement in PUGs and community activities related to the elaboration of the Participatory and Inclusive Rangeland Management Plan will have positive externalities such as bringing opportunities to socialize, to share experiences, and to learn from each other. The participation of women in these PUG/cooperative leaders will also benefit women, contributing to women's social and political empowerment. The project will foster women’s inclusion in all project activities through appropriate gender mainstreaming strategies. This will lead to increased access for women to men and women; reduction of logistical difficulties; coordination of activities with other events in which women participate; promotion of the establishment of small groups of women at the local level.

### Creation of job opportunities in the aimag and soum centers

• **Job creation in the agro-park industries and irrigated perimeter.**

The project will provide financing support for processing industries in the agro centers and in Gersen Soum. These economic activities will be located within or close to these aimag and inter-soum centers. These economic amenities will create jobs that will benefit to the local population and in particular women, and measures will be put in place to foster the creation of economic opportunities for women. The job creation activities will be gender-sensitive. The social actions will be the social & gender actions included in the PPEEs proposals. In particular, when assessing PPEEs criteria for assessing/approving private sector proposals include (i) measures to ensure that women are employed and/or hired; (ii) measures to ensure that the workforce and local suppliers/customers; enhancement of employment opportunities for women in agro-processing industries; (iii) measures to ensure that women have access to separate sanitary facilities and separate changing rooms and lockers where necessary. The Project will also ensure that gender responsive core labor law/standards are applied and that female workers are not discriminated against. The project will monitor the employment of particular women employment in PPEEs by the PMO, and PPEEs will be provided with advisory services on several topics including safeguards and gender.

• **Women-led agri-businesses supported through incubators and soft support**

In the agro-park, there are plans to establish a number of micro, small and medium enterprises (MSME), that will be located within the agro-park. The incubator will include space for 20 MSMEs in addition to other facilities such as storage for agricultural products (e.g. bovine milk, felt work, sewing, dairy products, seabuckthorn oils, etc.). All these enterprises will benefit from soft support (training on business plan, accounting, etc.). Similar incubators, with reduced capacity and/or different location, may be established in other soums. The Project will ensure that an incubator; 40% of workshops and related support go to women-led MSMEs.

• **Temporary job opportunities benefiting to women**

The project will also provide temporary job opportunities during the works. Local population participation is encouraged through the creation of jobs, the promotion of job opportunities to soum center residents and appropriate training. These temporary job opportunities can also benefit women, and 20% of jobs at construction stage will be first open to women.

- **Indicators:** At least 80% of households in the RUU are members of an Arrosa where at least 50% of signatory households sign the RUU; At least 20% female leadership is in PUG and 30% female leadership in cooperatives; at least 45 CPP-financed projects benefiting to women

- **Responsibility:** PIU social, gender and community engagement specialist PUGS

#### 2.1: Identifying female-headed households of each PUG and ensuring they are provided with sufficient land for grazing
- **Indicators:** 100% of female-headed households of each PUG and RUA update/signed under the project include an Arrosa where both spouses of signatory households sign the RUU; At least 20% female leadership in PUG system (2018 baseline: 5%); at least 45 CPP-financed projects benefiting to women
- **Timeframe:** 2021-2026
- **Responsibility:** PIU social, gender and community engagement specialist PUGS

#### Output 3: Increased through Community Participation in Procurement (CPP) benefits to women

- **Timeframe:** 2021-2026
- **Responsibility:** Executing Agency PIU social, gender and community engagement specialist PUGS

# Objectives & Activities

## Ensure women benefit from employment opportunities

### 4.2. Ensure provision of new jobs with skills and knowledge of women increased at operation phase

#### Indicators and Targets

- **60 SMEs** using animals' raw material from the PUGs invest in the program agro-parks creating a 1,500 new jobs, of which 300 skilled jobs – with 40% filled by women both directly employed and indirectly through their husbands.
- 3 incubators constructed in aimag centers and 2 incubators in inter-sum centers totaling 60 women-led businesses, including 80% of the businesses led to women-led businesses;
- All PUGs will participate in training programs for entrepreneurs soum cooperatives and SMEs are women; (1) 100 women entrepreneurs generating 80 jobs of which 20% go to women.

#### Timeframe

2021–2026

#### Responsibility

(Social, Gender and Community Engagement expert)

- Executing Agency: Participating enterprises

## For outputs 1.3: Project management and implementation

### 5.1. Appoint a social and gender specialist at PIU level

#### Indicators and Targets

- Social and gender specialists engaged in all stages of the project to implement Gender Action Plan (Target: 102 women-months of engagement, including 60 women-months of community engagement specified in the PRAO, and 25 men-months of engagement with men as part of the social and gender specialist in each aimag PIU).
- All PUGs will be trained by the social and gender specialists on gender-specific needs and priorities, and institutionalized data collection and reporting.

#### Timeframe

2021–2026

#### Responsibility

ADB, executing and implementing agencies: PMO, PIU

### 5.2. Ensure women's inclusiveness in PMO and PIU staff

#### Indicators and Targets

- At least 35% of PMO and PIU staff are female.

#### Timeframe

2021–2026

#### Responsibility

ADB, executing and implementing agencies: PMO, PIU

### 5.3. Maintain a project specific gender action plan (GAP) and conduct periodic reviews and consultations on the implementation of the GAP

#### Indicators and Targets

- Sex-disaggregated data collected and analyzed and reported annually.

#### Timeframe

2021–2026

#### Responsibility

PMO, PIU
